# Governance Flashcards

> One flashcard per examinable item in the repository Governance owners (basic + advanced), plus a concept card per owner file. IDs: GOV-1001 onward by agent block.
> **Verification status:** built from the repository owners with claims checked against Wikipedia and other pages that loaded while building; confirmed items carry a ✅ source tag; unverified details are marked ⚠️. Owner-vs-source conflicts are flagged inside the cards. Time-sensitive facts carry as-of dates from the owner.

---

## GOVERNANCE — Part 1: Good Governance, Policy Design, Development Processes, Civil Society and E-Governance

### GROUP — Good Governance: Concepts and Frameworks

### Flashcard GOV-1001 — Good governance: the normative standard, not a synonym for government
**Front:** What does "good governance" mean, and how does it differ from "government" and "governance"?
**Definition (learn):** Good governance is a normative judgement about how public authority is exercised, measured against criteria such as rule of law, participation, transparency, accountability, responsiveness, equity, effectiveness and efficiency. It is the standard, not the institutions.
**Breakdown:**
- Government = formal institutions and office-holders authorised by the Constitution and law (legislature, executive, judiciary are its core, not its whole).
- Public governance = the mechanisms and processes through which public authority is exercised, implemented, contested and corrected.
- Good governance = the evaluative test applied to public governance.
- Frameworks group and weight the criteria differently; there is no single universal weighted index.
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** Core proposition from the 2nd ARC 12th Report (Citizen-Centric Administration) and 4th Report (Ethics in Governance, 2007). (✅ owner 01)
**Links:** GOV-1002 (WGI), GOV-1005 (UNESCAP), GOV-1006 (2nd ARC pillars), GOV-1036 (non-state participation), GOV-1038 (one-line concept).
**Trap:** "Government" and "governance" are not interchangeable. Non-state participation in governance does not automatically transfer the State's legal duties.
**Mains hook:** Define the three terms, then name the normative test. India's deficits lie largely in process (who decides, how decisions are reviewed), not only in institutional design. Qualified: good governance cannot be proved by a score alone.
**Back:** *"Government names the institutions, governance names the process, good governance names the standard."*

### Flashcard GOV-1002 — World Bank Worldwide Governance Indicators: six dimensions
**Front:** Name the six dimensions of the World Bank Worldwide Governance Indicators (WGI).
**Definition (learn):** WGI are six separately reported governance estimates for cross-country comparison, produced by the World Bank.
**Breakdown:**
- 1. Voice and Accountability
- 2. Political Stability (former name: Political Stability and Absence of Violence/Terrorism)
- 3. Government Effectiveness
- 4. Regulatory Quality
- 5. Rule of Law
- 6. Control of Corruption
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** Dimension names as on the World Bank WGI page, accessed 25 September 2026 (✅ World Bank WGI page; ✅ owner 01). The page covers more than 200 economies, 1996–2025 data, announced 2026 update.
**Links:** GOV-1003 (WGI methodology), GOV-1014 (Rule of Law Index publisher), GOV-1015 (WGI Rule of Law vs standalone index), GOV-1019 (Government Effectiveness vs DPI).
**Trap:** "Rule of Law" is one WGI dimension; the standalone "Rule of Law Index" is a separate World Justice Project product (see GOV-1014). Do not treat WGI as one composite score.
**Mains hook:** WGI gives comparative benchmarking across six separately reported dimensions; it cannot tell you the outcome of any single transaction. Qualify every country comparison with its uncertainty band.
**Back:** *"Voice, Stability, Effectiveness, Regulation, Law, Corruption — six separate scores, never one."*

### Flashcard GOV-1003 — WGI methodology: perception data, uncertainty and the 0–100 scale
**Front:** How are WGI estimates produced, and why are they not a direct observation of public transactions?
**Definition (learn):** WGI aggregate perception-based information from household surveys, firm surveys and expert assessments into composite estimates, combined through an Unobserved Components Model (UCM, per owner 01).
**Breakdown:**
- Inputs: household, firm and expert perception sources (about 35 cross-country sources per the 2026 page).
- Outputs: (a) estimates in a standard statistical unit; (b) scores on a fixed-reference 0–100 presentation.
- Each dimension carries margins of error and model uncertainty.
- Historical estimates recalculated back to 1996 for consistency.
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** More than 200 economies; data 1996–2025; 2026 update (✅ World Bank WGI page, accessed 25 September 2026). The UCM model name is owner-stated (✅ owner 01); the World Bank page itself points to a separate methodology document for the model details (⚠️ not re-verified in full).
**Links:** GOV-1002 (WGI dimensions), GOV-1004 (UNDP frame), GOV-1029 (five index tests).
**Trap:** WGI is not a single "good governance" score and does not assign equal normative weight to one combined ranking. Perception-based measures can lag or lead actual administrative practice.
**Mains hook:** Perception-based aggregation makes WGI useful for benchmarking but weak on outcome linkage. Qualify any claim that a country "improved" by a fraction of a point with the margin of error.
**Back:** *"Perceive, aggregate, scale 0–100 — and quote the margin."*

### Flashcard GOV-1004 — UNDP governance framework (1997 process definition)
**Front:** What is the UNDP's governance framework as quoted by the 2nd ARC, and what does it cover?
**Definition (learn):** The UNDP process definition describes the mechanisms, processes and institutions through which collective decisions are made and implemented, and through which citizens articulate interests, exercise rights, meet obligations and mediate differences.
**Breakdown:**
- Process frame: mechanisms, processes, institutions.
- Citizen side: voice, rights, obligations, mediation of differences.
- Principles (normative checklist): participation, rule of law, transparency, responsiveness, consensus orientation, equity, effectiveness and efficiency, accountability, strategic vision.
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** 1997 process definition as quoted by the 2nd ARC (✅ owner 01). UNDP discussion paper on governance for sustainable development (✅ source cited in owner 01).
**Links:** GOV-1005 (UNESCAP eight), GOV-1002 (WGI), GOV-1006 (2nd ARC pillars).
**Trap:** The UNDP principles are not a UNDP country ranking and are not the UNESCAP eight characteristics. Do not relabel the UNDP list as the "eight UNDP principles".
**Mains hook:** Use UNDP for process language (voice, mediation, consensus) when assessing a mechanism's inclusiveness; it does not supply a numeric index.
**Back:** *"UNDP gives the process; it never gives a rank."*

### Flashcard GOV-1005 — UNESCAP's eight characteristics of good governance (as used by the 2nd ARC)
**Front:** List the eight characteristics of good governance in the UNESCAP formulation used by the 2nd ARC.
**Definition (learn):** A paired eight-attribute formulation of good governance cited in the 2nd ARC 12th Report.
**Breakdown:**
- Participatory
- Consensus oriented
- Accountable
- Transparent
- Responsive
- Effective and efficient
- Equitable and inclusive
- Following the rule of law
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** UNESCAP's paired characteristics; the 2nd ARC adopts this eight-attribute figure (✅ owner 01; UNESCAP good-governance PDF cited in owner 01).
**Links:** GOV-1004 (UNDP), GOV-1006 (2nd ARC four pillars), GOV-1007 (2nd ARC).
**Trap:** UNESCAP pairs some terms and does not list "strategic vision" as a ninth characteristic. Do not merge this eight-part figure with the UNDP checklist or with WGI.
**Mains hook:** When an answer invokes "eight characteristics", name the source. Use them as the criteria you will test an institution against, one by one.
**Back:** *"Eight attributes, paired: participate, agree, answer, reveal, respond, deliver, include, obey the law."*

### Flashcard GOV-1006 — 2nd ARC's four citizen-centric pillars: Ethos, Ethics, Equity, Efficiency
**Front:** What are the four pillars of citizen-centric good governance identified by the 2nd ARC 12th Report?
**Definition (learn):** The 2nd ARC's India-specific framing of citizen-centric good governance rests on four pillars.
**Breakdown:**
- Ethos: service to the citizen.
- Ethics: honesty, integrity and transparency.
- Equity: equal treatment with empathy for weaker sections.
- Efficiency: speedy and effective service without harassment, increasingly using ICT.
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** 2nd ARC 12th Report, Citizen-Centric Administration (✅ owner 01). These are dated reform recommendations, not a numerical index or self-executing law.
**Links:** GOV-1005 (UNESCAP eight), GOV-1007 (2nd ARC), GOV-1024 (trap on binding law).
**Trap:** The 4 Es are a recommendation framework; they do not replace the UNESCAP eight characteristics, and the 4th Report's ethics analysis is a separate source.
**Mains hook:** Use the 4 Es to organise a citizen-centric critique (service orientation, integrity, equity for weaker sections, speed without harassment).
**Back:** *"Ethos serves, Ethics stays honest, Equity includes, Efficiency delivers without harassment."*

### Flashcard GOV-1007 — 2nd Administrative Reforms Commission (2nd ARC): constitution and output
**Front:** When was the 2nd ARC constituted and how many reports did it produce?
**Definition (learn):** The Second Administrative Reforms Commission (2nd ARC) was an Indian reform commission whose 15 reports are the most detailed Indian institutional roadmap for converting the good-governance norm into reform.
**Breakdown:**
- Constituted: 2005.
- Output: 15 reports (routing table in owner 10).
- Status: recommendations, not statutes.
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** Constituted 2005; 15 reports (✅ owner 01; full routing in owner 10). Dated reform analysis 2006–09 (✅ owner 01).
**Links:** GOV-1005, GOV-1006, GOV-1024 (ARC is not binding law).
**Trap:** A common error is to treat ARC reports as enacted law. Verify implementation separately.
**Mains hook:** The 2nd ARC is the Indian reform bridge from normative principles (UNDP/UNESCAP) to institutional design. Qualify: recommendations convert into reform only when a duty-holder and remedy are created.
**Back:** *"Fifteen reports, zero automatic laws."*

### Flashcard GOV-1008 — Good Governance Day: 25 December
**Front:** Which day is observed as Good Governance Day in India, since when, and why?
**Definition (learn):** Good Governance Day is observed every year on 25 December to promote accountability, transparency and citizen-centric administration.
**Breakdown:**
- Date: 25 December.
- Since: 2014.
- Marks the birth anniversary of former Prime Minister Atal Bihari Vajpayee.
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** 25 December; observed since 2014 (✅ owner 01). The DARPG status was rechecked on 5 October 2026 (✅ owner 01 current-anchor note).
**Links:** GOV-1009 (DARPG), GOV-1010 (GGI launch on the same date).
**Trap:** A Good Governance Day event is not a GGI edition. Do not confuse the date with the 2019 GGI launch (also 25 December).
**Mains hook:** A commemorative day signals a norm but does not establish a measured outcome; pair any claim with an instrument (GGI, RTI, CPGRAMS).
**Back:** *"Vajpayee's day, December twenty-five, since 2014 — a commemoration, not a score."*

### Flashcard GOV-1009 — DARPG: nodal department for administrative reform and good governance
**Front:** What is DARPG, under which ministry does it function, and what does it do?
**Definition (learn):** The Department of Administrative Reforms and Public Grievances (DARPG) is the nodal Union department for administrative reform and good-governance measurement.
**Breakdown:**
- Parent ministry: Ministry of Personnel, Public Grievances and Pensions.
- Functions: administrative reform; Sevottam; CPGRAMS; Second ARC follow-up; Good Governance Index; citizen-charter material; NeSDA (see owner 05).
- Also coordinates Good Governance Day.
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** Nodal role under the Ministry of Personnel, Public Grievances and Pensions (✅ owner 01). Official DARPG references are cited in owner 01 (not re-fetched).
**Links:** GOV-1008 (Good Governance Day), GOV-1010 (GGI launch), GOV-1022 (CPGRAMS in responsiveness), GOV-1040 (DARPG-linked tools).
**Trap:** DARPG is both the reform department and the GGI coordinator. This proximity makes publication of definitions, weights and source data important; it does not by itself prove biased self-assessment.
**Mains hook:** A reform ministry that also measures its own performance needs an auditability test: are definitions, weights, missing-data rules and revisions public?
**Back:** *"DARPG reforms, measures and answers grievances, under Personnel, Public Grievances and Pensions."*

### Flashcard GOV-1010 — Good Governance Index (GGI): launch, purpose and sectors
**Front:** When was the Good Governance Index launched, by whom, and what does it measure?
**Definition (learn):** GGI is a composite, diagnostic tool that assesses state/UT governance performance across ten sectors, with states/UTs grouped into comparison categories.
**Breakdown:**
- Launch: 25 December 2019 by DARPG.
- Ten sectors (owner 01 names "economic governance" and "judiciary" as examples).
- Output: sector-wise scores by comparison category, revised each edition.
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** Launch date 25 December 2019 (✅ owner 01; DARPG). Ten sectors (✅ owner 01).
**Links:** GOV-1011 (latest edition 2020-21), GOV-1012 (edition groups), GOV-1013 (DGGI), GOV-1029 (index test).
**Trap:** GGI is not annual and does not yield one static national ranking; a rank is quotable only with its edition year.
**Mains hook:** Use GGI as a sector-wise diagnostic; the composite score can mask sectoral failure (e.g., "economic governance" high but "judiciary" low).
**Back:** *"Launched 25 December 2019, ten sectors, sector-wise not single-rank."*

### Flashcard GOV-1011 — Latest completed national GGI edition: GGI 2020-21
**Front:** What is the latest completed national GGI edition located as of 5 October 2026, and when was it released?
**Definition (learn):** The latest completed national Good Governance Index edition located on DARPG is GGI 2020-21.
**Breakdown:**
- Edition: GGI 2020-21.
- Release: 25 December 2021.
- Groups: four (see GOV-1012).
- Top-rank fact (Gujarat) belongs specifically to GGI 2020-21 (owner 01).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** Released 25 December 2021; status checked 5 October 2026 (✅ owner 01). DARPG pages cited in owner 01 (not re-fetched).
**Links:** GOV-1010 (GGI), GOV-1012 (edition groups), GOV-1013 (DGGI), GOV-1033 (a high index score is not proof).
**Trap:** Do not assert a post-2021 national GGI edition, a state rank, a score or an indicator count without the released DARPG report. The owner records a correction: a previous audit wrongly claimed a "GGI 2025" edition; that claim is withdrawn.
**Mains hook:** Any ranking claim must be dated. "India ranks X" without an edition year is a factual error waiting to happen.
**Back:** *"2020-21 is the last national edition located; 2025 is a withdrawn claim."*

### Flashcard GOV-1012 — GGI comparison groups by edition: three in 2019, four in 2020-21
**Front:** How many comparison groups did GGI 2019 and GGI 2020-21 use, and what were they?
**Definition (learn):** The grouping of states/UTs changed between GGI editions to reflect differences in capacity and geography.
**Breakdown:**
- GGI 2019: three groups.
- GGI 2020-21: four groups — Other States Group A; Other States Group B; North-East and Hill States; Union Territories.
- Grouping improves contextual comparison but does not remove within-group disparities.
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** Edition-specific grouping (✅ owner 01 and owner 01 advanced). The exact name of the 2019 three groups is not restated in the owner, so do not invent them.
**Links:** GOV-1010, GOV-1011, GOV-1029 (federal comparability).
**Trap:** A question asking "how many groups" must name the edition. Four groups is 2020-21, not 2019.
**Mains hook:** Edition-specific grouping is the GGI's answer to federal comparability; it is still only a partial correction.
**Back:** *"Three in 2019, four in 2020-21: Group A, Group B, North-East and Hill, UTs."*

### Flashcard GOV-1013 — District Good Governance Index (DGGI) vs national GGI
**Front:** Is the District Good Governance Index a later national GGI edition?
**Definition (learn):** DGGI is a separate sub-national exercise developed for particular states/UTs.
**Breakdown:**
- Scope: district-level, state/UT-specific.
- Relation to GGI: separate; does not supersede or substitute for a national GGI edition.
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** Owner 01 states DGGI is separate (✅ owner 01). No DGGI release date is asserted here (⚠️ not verified from a DARPG report).
**Links:** GOV-1010, GOV-1011, GOV-1012.
**Trap:** Do not convert a district index into a national edition or a national rank.
**Mains hook:** District indices are useful for local diagnosis, but cannot stand in for a national state ranking.
**Back:** *"District is not national; DGGI is a separate exercise."*

### Flashcard GOV-1014 — Rule of Law Index: publisher is the World Justice Project
**Front:** Which organisation publishes the Rule of Law Index?
**Definition (learn):** The standalone "Rule of Law Index" is published by the World Justice Project (WJP), not the World Bank.
**Breakdown:**
- Publisher: World Justice Project.
- Not the same thing as the WGI "Rule of Law" dimension (a World Bank estimate).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** The 2018 GS-I Prelims Q14 asked which body releases the "Rule of Law Index" (✅ owner 01; exact stem at books\more_previous_papers\QP-CSP-18-GS-I-C.pdf, PDF p. 9). Official key not held locally, so no option letter is recorded here. The WJP publisher identity is stated by owner 01; Wikipedia fetch failed (⚠️ not re-verified online).
**Links:** GOV-1002 (WGI Rule of Law dimension), GOV-1015 (Rule of Law as a WGI dimension trap).
**Trap:** Options naming Amnesty International, the ICJ, the Office of the UN High Commissioner for Human Rights, or the WJP: only the WJP publishes the Rule of Law Index (per owner 01).
**Mains hook:** Distinguish the standalone index from WGI's "Rule of Law" dimension when asked about measuring legal quality.
**Back:** *"Rule of Law Index: World Justice Project, not the World Bank."*

### Flashcard GOV-1015 — WGI "Rule of Law" dimension vs standalone Rule of Law Index
**Front:** Why is "Rule of Law" in the WGI not the same as the standalone Rule of Law Index?
**Definition (learn):** WGI "Rule of Law" is one of six World Bank perception-based estimates; the Rule of Law Index is a separate product of the World Justice Project.
**Breakdown:**
- WGI Rule of Law: a World Bank dimension aggregated from perception sources.
- WJP Rule of Law Index: a separately published index.
- They can share a topic but not a publisher, method or number.
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** WGI dimension name (✅ World Bank WGI page). WJP publisher (✅ owner 01).
**Links:** GOV-1002, GOV-1014.
**Trap:** Quoting the WGI "Rule of Law" score as the "Rule of Law Index" (or vice versa) is a publisher error.
**Mains hook:** Identify the publisher and method before comparing rule-of-law scores across sources.
**Back:** *"WGI counts six; WJP publishes its own index."*

### Flashcard GOV-1016 — Good governance as a dimension of state capacity
**Front:** What is state capacity, and why is capacity not the same as good governance?
**Definition (learn):** State capacity (owner 01 inference) is the ability to formulate, coordinate and implement lawful public decisions.
**Breakdown:**
- Capacity components: staff, records, finance, coordination, accessible delivery.
- Capable administration can still be non-participatory, inequitable or unaccountable.
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** Owner 01 analytical inference (⚠️ inference, not a cited definition).
**Links:** GOV-1017 (justified trust), GOV-1023 (eight-stage mechanism), GOV-1027 (capacity objection).
**Trap:** Efficiency is not effectiveness; a capable state may still lack legitimacy.
**Mains hook:** Separate capacity (can it deliver?) from legitimacy (is its authority justifiably accepted?).
**Back:** *"Capacity delivers; legitimacy justifies; keep them apart."*

### Flashcard GOV-1017 — Justified trust and legitimacy
**Front:** What makes trust in government "justified" and what is legitimacy under the owner's framework?
**Definition (learn):** Justified trust is confidence supported by predictable law, competent performance, meaningful participation, reasons and correction. Legitimacy concerns whether public authority is accepted as justifiable under democratic and constitutional standards.
**Breakdown:**
- Justified trust = law + performance + participation + reasons + correction.
- Legitimacy = electoral authorisation (necessary) plus continuing lawful, inclusive and answerable performance.
- Publicity, compliance or fast disposal alone cannot establish trust.
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** Owner 01 analytical framework (⚠️ inference).
**Links:** GOV-1016 (state capacity), GOV-1023 (eight-stage mechanism), GOV-1027 (measurement and other objections).
**Trap:** Electoral authorisation is necessary, not sufficient, for legitimacy. Dashboard scores do not prove justified trust.
**Mains hook:** Trust is earned provisionally through the chain from Constitution to citizen outcome; test it with reasons and correction, not with publicity.
**Back:** *"Elected is necessary; answerable performance keeps legitimacy."*

### Flashcard GOV-1018 — The Constitution-to-trust chain
**Front:** Trace the chain from Constitution to justified trust, as set out in the owner.
**Definition (learn):** The owner's causal chain runs: Preamble, Fundamental Rights and Directive Principles → lawful rule plus named duty-holder plus administrative capacity → performance, participation, reasons and usable correction → justified trust and legitimacy.
**Breakdown:**
- Constitutional values: justice, liberty, equality, fraternity/dignity; non-arbitrariness; welfare.
- The 2nd ARC links Fundamental Rights and DPSP to citizen-centric administration.
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** ✅ owner 01 (2nd ARC reading of Constitution). The chain is an analytical frame (⚠️).
**Links:** GOV-1016, GOV-1017, GOV-1023.
**Trap:** Constitutional values alone do not deliver services; a named duty-holder must exist for each norm.
**Mains hook:** Start any governance answer with the constitutional value, then show the duty-holder and remedy.
**Back:** *"Constitution gives the value, duty-holder gives the handle, correction keeps trust."*

### Flashcard GOV-1019 — Government Effectiveness: WGI vs India's e-governance/DPI push
**Front:** How do WGI Government Effectiveness and India's DPI/e-governance push relate?
**Definition (learn):** WGI Government Effectiveness is one of the six WGI dimensions; India's e-governance and DPI are domestic reform instruments that measure overlapping but not identical constructs.
**Breakdown:**
- WGI measure: cross-country perception of government effectiveness.
- Domestic e-governance: digital delivery, service portals and infrastructure (see owners 05 and 06).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** WGI dimension name (✅ World Bank WGI page). The link to DPI is owner 01 analysis (⚠️).
**Links:** GOV-1002 (WGI Government Effectiveness), GOV-1034 (WGI vs GGI trap), GOV-1027 (counter-arguments); file 06 DPI cards cross-referenced by owner name.
**Trap:** A digital instrument does not "implement WGI". Overlapping constructs must not be treated as identical.
**Mains hook:** Use Government Effectiveness to test whether digital reform improves perceived effectiveness, not only transaction counts.
**Back:** *"Effectiveness perceived is not effectiveness delivered."*

### Flashcard GOV-1020 — Seven norms with Indian anchors (Evidence Bank A)
**Front:** Which Indian instrument anchors each of the seven governance norms (rule of law, transparency, accountability, participation, responsiveness, equity, effectiveness)?
**Definition (learn):** Each norm has an Indian anchor, a mechanism and a limitation. Use the claim → anchor → mechanism → limitation sequence in answers.
**Breakdown:**
- Rule of law: Article 14 non-arbitrariness; reasoned orders (limit: below regulator tier).
- Transparency: RTI Act 2005, s.4 proactive disclosure (limit: s.4 compliance not independently audited).
- Accountability: CAG (Arts 148–151); Lokpal Act 2013; CVC Act 2003 (limit: none is a general grievance authority).
- Participation: MGNREGA s.17 Gram Sabha social audit; Pre-Legislative Consultation Policy 2014 (limit: executive policy, not binding).
- Responsiveness: State RTS Acts (Madhya Pradesh 2010 onward); CPGRAMS 21-day benchmark (DARPG guidelines Aug 2024) (limit: RTS covers only notified services; CPGRAMS is administrative).
- Equity: DBT/Aadhaar targeting (limit: authentication failure becomes exclusion).
- Effectiveness: Output-Outcome Monitoring Framework; DMEO (limit: output reporting dominates).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** As listed in owner 01 Evidence Bank A (✅ owner 01 for each anchor). Madhya Pradesh RTS 2010 and CPGRAMS 21-day benchmark (August 2024) are owner-stated.
**Links:** GOV-1021 (RTI), GOV-1022 (RTS/CPGRAMS), GOV-1025 (MGNREGA social audit), GOV-1026 (Lokpal/CVC/CAG).
**Trap:** Give each norm its own remedy: CAG audits, Lokpal and CVC handle corruption, not general grievances.
**Mains hook:** Pick 2–3 norms for 10 marks; 4–6 for 15; 5–8 for 20. Each must have an anchor and a limit.
**Back:** *"Seven norms, seven anchors, seven limits."*

### Flashcard GOV-1021 — Transparency: RTI Act 2005, section 4 proactive disclosure
**Front:** Which provision of the RTI Act, 2005 creates proactive disclosure, and what is its limit?
**Definition (learn):** RTI Act 2005 section 4 requires public authorities to publish records proactively, before a citizen must ask.
**Breakdown:**
- Anchor: RTI Act 2005, section 4.
- Mechanism: publication of records and information.
- Limit: s.4 compliance is not independently audited across authorities (owner 01 caution).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** RTI Act 2005 (✅ owner 01). The section 4 proactive-disclosure mandate is owner-stated; section-level text not re-fetched (⚠️).
**Links:** GOV-1020 (seven norms), GOV-1022 (RTS and CPGRAMS), GOV-1023 (mechanism stages 4–5).
**Trap:** Section 4 is proactive; RTI applications are reactive. Do not treat them as the same mechanism.
**Mains hook:** RTI is a transparency tool; its outcome depends on record quality and citizen cost (stages 4–5 of the mechanism chain, GOV-1023).
**Back:** *"Section 4 publishes before you ask; applications come after."*

### Flashcard GOV-1022 — Responsiveness: State Right to Service (RTS) Acts and CPGRAMS
**Front:** What are the responsiveness anchors in the seven-norm bank, and what are their limits?
**Definition (learn):** State RTS Acts give statutory timelines and penalties for notified services; CPGRAMS is an administrative grievance system with a 21-day benchmark.
**Breakdown:**
- RTS: statutory timeline + penalty; Madhya Pradesh 2010 onward.
- CPGRAMS: administrative tracking + appeal; 21-day benchmark (DARPG guidelines, August 2024).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** Madhya Pradesh RTS 2010 (✅ owner 01); CPGRAMS 21-day benchmark DARPG guidelines August 2024 (✅ owner 01).
**Links:** GOV-1020, GOV-1009 (DARPG), GOV-1023.
**Trap:** RTS covers only notified services; CPGRAMS is administrative, not adjudicatory. Do not present CPGRAMS as a court.
**Mains hook:** The RTS penalty is what converts a citizen charter into enforceable responsiveness; without a penalty it stops at stage 3 of the mechanism chain.
**Back:** *"RTS times and penalises; CPGRAMS tracks and appeals, not adjudicates."*

### Flashcard GOV-1023 — Eight-stage mechanism: how a norm becomes an outcome
**Front:** Name the eight stages in the owner's mechanism from norm to legitimacy.
**Definition (learn):** Owner's causal chain: norm → legal form → institution → procedure → capacity → citizen side → remedy → feedback → legitimacy (eight stages, counting from legal form to legitimacy).
**Breakdown:**
- Stage 1: Legal form (Act, rule, guideline or executive policy?)
- Stage 2: Institution (who holds the duty and can be identified?)
- Stage 3: Procedure (timeline, officer, appeal, penalty?)
- Stage 4: Capacity (staff, records, digital capability, budget)
- Stage 5: Citizen side (awareness, access, cost, language, literacy)
- Stage 6: Remedy (is failure correctable, and by whom?)
- Stage 7: Feedback (is failure counted and used for redesign?)
- Stage 8: Legitimacy (lawful, capable, participatory performance earning justified trust)
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** Owner 01 teaching device (⚠️ analytical, not a legal text).
**Links:** GOV-1016, GOV-1017, GOV-1021, GOV-1022.
**Trap:** Name the stage where a reform stops. RTI often stops at stages 4–5; Citizens' Charters at stage 3; dashboards at stage 6.
**Mains hook:** Use the stage map to answer "why does X fail despite Y?" by locating the break.
**Back:** *"Legal form, institution, procedure, capacity, citizen, remedy, feedback, legitimacy — find the break."*

### Flashcard GOV-1024 — Good-governance trap: ARC reports are not binding law
**Front:** Do the 2nd ARC reports create binding law?
**Definition (learn):** No. ARC reports are reform recommendations and dated analytical sources, not enacted statutes, unless separately verified as implemented.
**Breakdown:**
- Recommendation vs law: recommendation proposes; statute or executive order enacts.
- Verify implementation before claiming a recommendation is in force.
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** 2nd ARC constituted 2005; 15 reports (✅ owner 01).
**Links:** GOV-1007 (2nd ARC constitution), GOV-1006 (four pillars), GOV-1037 (ARC not binding law, repeat trap).
**Trap:** Treat "ARC recommended X" and "X is in force" as separate claims.
**Mains hook:** Reform credibility depends on implementation, not on the report.
**Back:** *"Recommended is not enacted; check the act or order."*

### Flashcard GOV-1025 — MGNREGA section 17: Gram Sabha social audit
**Front:** Which MGNREGA provision anchors social audit in the participation norm, and what is its limit?
**Definition (learn):** MGNREGA section 17 provides for social audit by the Gram Sabha as community verification of works.
**Breakdown:**
- Anchor: MGNREGA s.17, Gram Sabha social audit.
- Mechanism: community verification of records and works.
- Limit: depends on Gram Sabha functioning and information access.
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** MGNREGA s.17 (✅ owner 01). Pre-Legislative Consultation Policy 2014 is the parallel participation anchor (executive policy; comments not binding) (✅ owner 01).
**Links:** GOV-1020 (seven norms), GOV-1026 (accountability bodies), GOV-1056 (Pre-Legislative Consultation Policy), GOV-1167 (facilitation is not audit authority).
**Trap:** The 2014 Pre-Legislative Consultation Policy is executive, not statutory, and its comments are not binding. Do not confuse it with MGNREGA s.17.
**Mains hook:** Social audit converts a formal right into verified participation, but only if the Gram Sabha has records and capacity.
**Back:** *"Section 17: the Gram Sabha audits; the 2014 policy only consults."*

### Flashcard GOV-1026 — Accountability bodies: CAG, Lokpal and CVC are different remedies
**Front:** Which bodies provide accountability remedies in the owner's norm bank, and how do their remedies differ?
**Definition (learn):** Three distinct accountability instruments: CAG audit, Lokpal (corruption jurisdiction), CVC (vigilance advice).
**Breakdown:**
- CAG: Articles 148–151 — audit of public accounts.
- Lokpal: Lokpal Act 2013 — corruption jurisdiction.
- CVC: Central Vigilance Commission Act 2003 — vigilance advice.
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** Articles 148–151; Lokpal Act 2013; CVC Act 2003 (✅ owner 01).
**Links:** GOV-1020, GOV-1025, GOV-1024.
**Trap:** None of the three is a general grievance authority; routing a grievance to the CAG or Lokpal is a classic error.
**Mains hook:** Name the specific remedy: audit finds, Lokpal prosecutes corruption jurisdiction, CVC advises on vigilance.
**Back:** *"CAG audits; Lokpal polices graft; CVC advises vigilance; none handles all grievances."*

### Flashcard GOV-1027 — Counter-argument bank for good governance (vagueness, measurement, sovereignty, capacity)
**Front:** What are the four counter-arguments against good-governance discourse and their replies?
**Definition (learn):** Owner 01 lists four objections and replies for critically examine or 20-mark verdicts.
**Breakdown:**
- Vagueness objection: the bundle conflicts internally; reply: name the specific trade-off.
- Measurement objection: perception surveys reflect elite priors; reply: use transaction-level evidence (RTS default rates, grievance recurrence, NeSDA).
- Sovereignty/conditionality objection: norms came via donor conditionality; reply: Indian norms trace to Constitution and statutes (RTI, RTS, MGNREGA s.17).
- Capacity objection: a further charter without records, staff and appeal machinery changes nothing (stage 4).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** Owner 01 counter-argument bank (⚠️ analytical reply, not a sourced quotation).
**Links:** GOV-1003, GOV-1016, GOV-1023, GOV-1020.
**Trap:** Naming the objection without a reply loses marks; a reply grounded in Indian statute converts a defensive answer into an argued one.
**Mains hook:** Use the four objections to show awareness of critique, then reply with an Indian instrument.
**Back:** *"Vague, perceived, imported, unfunded — and each has a reply."*

### Flashcard GOV-1028 — Behavioural insights as a governance tool (Economic Survey 2018-19, Chapter 2)
**Front:** Which four behavioural tools does the owner list as governance instruments, and what is the official teaching anchor?
**Definition (learn):** A nudge changes the choice architecture while preserving formal choice. The owner uses four tools with Indian anchors.
**Breakdown:**
- Social-norm message: Swachh Bharat (Economic Survey 2018-19 discussion).
- Moral-salience appeal: "Give It Up" LPG-subsidy campaign.
- Positive identity / role model: Beti Bachao Beti Padhao and the proposed BBBP-to-BADLAV framing.
- Default / simplification / reminder: the Survey's broader nudge framework.
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** Economic Survey 2018-19, Chapter 2 is the official teaching anchor (✅ owner 01). BADLAV framing is owner-stated (⚠️ not re-verified).
**Links:** GOV-1019 (Government Effectiveness and nudges), GOV-1023 (mechanism stages), GOV-1027 (counter-arguments).
**Trap:** A nudge can improve take-up but cannot cure missing supply, unequal power, weak enforcement or a wrongly designed entitlement. A default becomes manipulative where a citizen cannot understand or reverse it.
**Mains hook:** Test a nudge for transparency, reversibility, distributional impact and measured outcome; use a pre-specified indicator and a comparison group.
**Back:** *"Norm, salience, identity, default — nudges help, they do not replace entitlement."*

### Flashcard GOV-1029 — Five tests for any governance index
**Front:** What are the five tests the owner uses to critically examine a governance index?
**Definition (learn):** A five-test checklist for any index under a critically examine directive.
**Breakdown:**
- 1. Indicator validity: does it measure the outcome or an easy input?
- 2. Data comparability: self-reported by the ranked entity? comparable across reporting capacity?
- 3. Output-versus-outcome balance: how much is expenditure or coverage rather than change?
- 4. Federal and contextual adjustment: are states of different size and base compared fairly?
- 5. Consequence: does a poor score trigger correction, or only headlines?
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** Owner 01 analytical checklist (⚠️ analytical).
**Links:** GOV-1010 (GGI), GOV-1012 (groups), GOV-1002 (WGI), GOV-1032 (indicator capture).
**Trap:** An index with no corrective loop measures governance without improving it.
**Mains hook:** Use the five tests as the spine of any index critique, then state the verdict.
**Back:** *"Valid, comparable, outcome, adjusted, consequential — five tests, one verdict."*

### Flashcard GOV-1030 — Good governance vs development administration
**Front:** How does good governance differ from development administration?
**Definition (learn):** Good governance is a quality standard applicable to any state function. Development administration is a specific administrative orientation directed deliberately at social and economic transformation.
**Breakdown:**
- Good governance: broader; applies to regulatory and law-and-order functions too.
- Development administration: transformative orientation.
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** Owner 01 advanced analysis (⚠️ analytical).
**Links:** GOV-1001 (good governance as standard), GOV-1009 (DARPG), GOV-1045 (top-down vs bottom-up).
**Trap:** Do not confine good governance to development programmes; a regulator or police station can be judged by its governance quality.
**Mains hook:** Use the broader norm when a question is about regulatory or enforcement functions.
**Back:** *"Good governance is the standard for every function; development administration is one orientation."*

### Flashcard GOV-1031 — Index-driven competitive federalism vs principle-driven reform
**Front:** What two reform routes does the owner set up for "what counts as good governance" in India?
**Definition (learn):** The owner identifies two routes: index-driven competitive federalism (GGI rankings) and principle-driven institutional reform (ARC recommendations).
**Breakdown:**
- Index route: rankings create competition among states; risk of gaming indicators.
- Principle route: ARC and UNDP/UNESCAP criteria shape institutional design; cannot be converted to a score without separate indicators.
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** Owner 01 advanced architecture (⚠️ analytical).
**Links:** GOV-1010, GOV-1006, GOV-1029.
**Trap:** Competition can improve reform, or only improve reported indicators.
**Mains hook:** Argue which route the institution uses and where its evidence comes from.
**Back:** *"Index competes, principle reforms; both can be gamed."*

### Flashcard GOV-1032 — Trap: indicator capture and perception-measurement gap
**Front:** What do "indicator capture" and the "perception-measurement gap" mean?
**Definition (learn):** Indicator capture: effort shifts to improving the measured proxy (e.g., grievance disposal rate) instead of resolution quality. Perception-measurement gap: a state can improve practice while its perception score lags, or vice versa.
**Breakdown:**
- Indicator capture: a generic governance risk, not specific to India.
- Perception gap: media visibility can differ from administrative reality.
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** Owner 01 advanced failure modes (⚠️ analytical).
**Links:** GOV-1003 (perception), GOV-1029 (index test), GOV-1022 (CPGRAMS disposal).
**Trap:** A high score does not guarantee accountable process.
**Mains hook:** A "disposal rate" can rise while resolution quality falls; test the outcome, not only the count.
**Back:** *"Score up, substance flat — the proxy got captured."*

### Flashcard GOV-1033 — Trap: a high index score does not guarantee accountable governance
**Front:** Does a rising governance index score prove improving governance?
**Definition (learn):** No. Index scores can rise through indicator capture and perception gaps without the normative standard (rule of law, accountability) improving equally.
**Breakdown:**
- Scores reflect the indicators and sources used.
- Normative improvement needs process change, not only a higher number.
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** Owner 01 advanced prelims trap (⚠️ analytical).
**Links:** GOV-1032, GOV-1029, GOV-1010.
**Trap:** Treating an index as an accountability guarantee.
**Mains hook:** The 10-mark statement "an improving governance index score does not guarantee improving governance" is answered by indicator capture plus the process test.
**Back:** *"A higher score is evidence, not proof."*

### Flashcard GOV-1034 — Trap: WGI and GGI measure identical things at different scales
**Front:** Are WGI and GGI interchangeable governance rankings?
**Definition (learn):** No. WGI is a six-dimension global comparative index; GGI is a ten-sector India-specific diagnostic with a different indicator basis.
**Breakdown:**
- WGI: global; perception-based; six dimensions.
- GGI: India-specific; ten sectors; administrative and sectoral indicators.
- NeSDA measures e-service depth; the Devolution Index measures panchayat devolution; four objects at four scales.
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** Owner 01 measurement layer (✅ owner 01; WGI method ✅ World Bank page).
**Links:** GOV-1002, GOV-1010, GOV-1029, GOV-1030.
**Trap:** Do not convert scores between WGI and GGI; they are not convertible.
**Mains hook:** Four instruments measuring four different objects at four scales: WGI (global, perception), GGI (state sectors), NeSDA (e-service delivery), Devolution Index (panchayat fiscal and functional devolution).
**Back:** *"WGI global six, GGI state ten, NeSDA e-services, Devolution panchayats — not interchangeable."*

### Flashcard GOV-1035 — Trap: good governance as "only about corruption control"
**Front:** Is good governance merely corruption control?
**Definition (learn):** No. "Control of Corruption" is one of six WGI dimensions and one concern within the wider normative frameworks.
**Breakdown:**
- WGI: six separately reported dimensions; source weights are model-derived.
- UNDP, UNESCAP and 2nd ARC do not create one equal-weighted composite.
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** WGI dimension name (✅ World Bank WGI page); framework differences (✅ owner 01).
**Links:** GOV-1002, GOV-1005, GOV-1006.
**Trap:** Equating good governance with anti-corruption narrows the standard to one of many norms.
**Mains hook:** Show rule of law, participation and responsiveness alongside corruption control.
**Back:** *"Corruption is one of six dimensions and one of many norms."*

### Flashcard GOV-1036 — Trap: government vs governance: non-state actors do not become co-governors automatically
**Front:** Do NGOs, firms and citizens acquire the State's legal duties when they participate in governance?
**Definition (learn):** No. Non-state participation can inform, deliver or scrutinise public action but does not automatically transfer the State's legal duties.
**Breakdown:**
- State duties remain with public authorities.
- Participation can be advisory, delivery-oriented or scrutinising.
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** Owner 01 definition (✅ owner 01).
**Links:** GOV-1001 (government vs governance), GOV-1120 (civil society stakeholders), GOV-1166 (complement, not duty-holder), GOV-1167 (facilitation vs audit).
**Trap:** Treating an NGO delivery partner as the accountable duty-holder for a statutory entitlement.
**Mains hook:** Name who holds the legal duty; then the non-state role.
**Back:** *"Participation does not transfer the State's duty."*

### Flashcard GOV-1037 — Trap: the Second ARC created binding law (listed here as reform status)
**Front:** Is a 2nd ARC report a statute, and what should an answer say about its status?
**Definition (learn):** See GOV-1024. The ARC is a reform commission; it recommends and does not enact.
**Breakdown:**
- Recommendation status must be verified.
- Answer: cite the report, then state implementation status.
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** Owner 01 (✅).
**Links:** GOV-1024, GOV-1007.
**Trap:** Stating "the ARC mandated X" when X is only recommended.
**Mains hook:** Separate recommendation from implementation.
**Back:** *"Recommended, implemented, enforced: three different words."*

### Flashcard GOV-1038 — Concept card: good governance in one line
**Front:** In one sentence, what is the Governance file 01 concept?
**Definition (learn):** Good governance is the normative standard — rule of law, transparency, accountability, responsiveness and effectiveness — against which public authority is judged and continuously reformed, by state and non-state actors.
**Breakdown:**
- Norms: legality, transparency, accountability, responsiveness, participation, equity, effectiveness.
- Measurement layer: WGI, GGI, NeSDA, Devolution Index.
- Reform layer: 2nd ARC; RTI; RTS; social audit; CPGRAMS.
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** Owner 01 answer thesis (✅ owner 01).
**Links:** GOV-1001, GOV-1020, GOV-1023, GOV-1034.
**Trap:** Good governance is not the institution itself; it is the test applied to the institution.
**Mains hook:** Open any institutional answer with the thesis, then the normative test.
**Back:** *"Standard, not structure; test, not statute."*

### Flashcard GOV-1039 — 2nd ARC 4th Report: ethics in governance (2007)
**Front:** Which 2nd ARC report supplies the institutional anti-abuse and ethics diagnosis, and when was it published?
**Definition (learn):** The 4th Report, Ethics in Governance, 2007, supplies the institutional anti-abuse and ethics diagnosis.
**Breakdown:**
- Report: 4th Report, Ethics in Governance.
- Year: 2007.
- Reproduces the UNESCAP characteristics in its analysis (owner 01 source note).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** 2007 (✅ owner 01).
**Links:** GOV-1006, GOV-1005, GOV-1038.
**Trap:** Ethics as personal probity is an Ethics-owned topic; the 4th Report contributes institutional diagnosis.
**Mains hook:** Use the 4th Report for institutional integrity, not personal conduct.
**Back:** *"Ethics in Governance, 2007: institutions, not individuals alone."*

### Flashcard GOV-1040 — DARPG-linked measurement and reform toolkit
**Front:** Which DARPG-linked instruments does owner 01 treat as the measurement and reform toolkit?
**Definition (learn):** Owner 01 treats the following as DARPG-linked tools: Good Governance Index, Sevottam, citizen-charter material, CPGRAMS and the Second ARC follow-up.
**Breakdown:**
- GGI: sectoral measurement.
- Sevottam: service-delivery quality framework (see owner 08).
- Citizen charters: service standards (see owner 07).
- CPGRAMS: grievance system (see owner 08).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** Owner 01 section 4 (✅ owner 01).
**Links:** GOV-1009 (DARPG), GOV-1010 (GGI), GOV-1022 (CPGRAMS and RTS), GOV-1006 (2nd ARC pillars).
**Trap:** A DARPG-linked tool is not automatically a statutory right.
**Mains hook:** Map each tool to its norm before evaluating it.
**Back:** *"GGI measures, Sevottam assures, charters promise, CPGRAMS answers."*

### GROUP — Government Policy Design and Implementation

### Flashcard GOV-1041 — Policy cycle: five stages with feedback
**Front:** Name the five stages of the policy cycle and the feedback loop that connects them.
**Definition (learn):** The standard analytical sequence used to locate where a policy succeeds or fails: agenda setting, formulation, decision (enactment), implementation and evaluation, with feedback back to agenda setting.
**Breakdown:**
- 1. Agenda setting: problem recognised.
- 2. Formulation: options and RIA.
- 3. Decision/enactment: law, rule or scheme notified.
- 4. Implementation: street-level delivery (the gap zone).
- 5. Evaluation: feedback loop back to agenda.
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** Standard stages (✅ owner 02; policy-theory basis Pressman & Wildavsky and Michael Lipsky, per owner 02).
**Links:** GOV-1042 (implementation gap), GOV-1043 (street-level bureaucracy), GOV-1048 (six-stage map).
**Trap:** Implementation failure is not located only at the decision stage; the gap usually opens at delivery.
**Mains hook:** Name the stage where failure sits (diagnosis, design realism, clearance points, street-level discretion, evaluation) before blaming "poor implementation".
**Back:** *"Agenda, formulate, decide, deliver, evaluate — then feed back."*

### Flashcard GOV-1042 — Implementation gap: announced policy vs lived outcome
**Front:** What is the implementation gap, and at which stage does it typically open?
**Definition (learn):** The difference between a policy as designed or announced and the policy as delivered on the ground. Classically analysed by Pressman and Wildavsky as arising from the number of decision points (clearance points) a policy must pass.
**Breakdown:**
- Opens at delivery: street-level officials, incomplete capacity and citizen access determine the outcome.
- Causes: design realism, clearance-point count, quality of frontline discretion.
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** Definition (✅ owner 02). Pressman & Wildavsky (✅ owner 02; no edition year stated in the owner).
**Links:** GOV-1041, GOV-1043, GOV-1044 (clearance points), GOV-1055 (implementation gap is not corruption).
**Trap:** The implementation gap is not a synonym for corruption; design failure and resource constraint are also causes.
**Mains hook:** A 10-mark answer on the implementation gap needs one Indian example and one corrective attached to a diagnosed stage.
**Back:** *"Announced is not delivered: the gap opens at the last mile."*

### Flashcard GOV-1043 — Street-level bureaucracy (Michael Lipsky)
**Front:** Who coined "street-level bureaucracy", and what does the term describe?
**Definition (learn):** Michael Lipsky's concept: frontline officials (teachers, police, ration-shop dealers, field health workers) who exercise substantial discretion and, through daily choices, effectively make policy regardless of formal top-level decisions.
**Breakdown:**
- Who: frontline officials with discretion.
- Why it matters: discretion constitutes policy in practice.
- Examples: ration dealer, ANM, teacher, exam-centre invigilator.
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** Michael Lipsky (✅ owner 02). Lipsky's thesis reframes frontline discretion as policy-making (✅ owner 02 advanced).
**Links:** GOV-1041 (policy cycle), GOV-1047 (coping routines), GOV-1042 (implementation gap), GOV-1083 (discretion guided, not eliminated).
**Trap:** Street-level bureaucrats do not merely "follow orders". Discretion can adapt policy humanely or exclude beneficiaries.
**Mains hook:** Use Lipsky to explain frontline outcomes; the governance task is to guide discretion (written reasons, records, appeal), not to abolish it.
**Back:** *"Lipsky: the clerk at the counter is the policy."*

### Flashcard GOV-1044 — Clearance points (Pressman and Wildavsky)
**Front:** What is a "clearance point", and what is the central finding about multiplying them?
**Definition (learn):** Each additional actor or approval a policy must clear before proceeding. The cumulative probability of delay or failure rises as clearance points multiply, even when each single point is likely to succeed.
**Breakdown:**
- Example: Union → State → District → Panchayat sign-off for a centrally sponsored scheme.
- Effect: multiplication of failure probability.
- Reduction: fewer clearance points increases speed but centralises decisions and reduces local adaptation.
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** Pressman and Wildavsky (✅ owner 02).
**Links:** GOV-1042 (implementation gap), GOV-1049 (convergence removes clearance points), GOV-1082 (more clearance points are not more accountability).
**Trap:** More clearance points does not mean more accountability; it multiplies failure probability.
**Mains hook:** Explain why a centrally sponsored scheme with four sign-off levels is more implementation-gap-prone than a single-agency scheme, independent of funding.
**Back:** *"Each approval multiplies the failure odds."*

### Flashcard GOV-1045 — Top-down vs bottom-up implementation theory
**Front:** How do top-down and bottom-up implementation theories differ?
**Definition (learn):** Top-down asks whether frontline action matched the original design (classical Pressman-Wildavsky reading). Bottom-up treats the frontline output as the real policy regardless of design intent (Lipsky).
**Breakdown:**
- Top-down: did action match design?
- Bottom-up: what did frontline actors actually produce?
- Neither is complete alone.
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** Owner 02 advanced (✅ owner 02 as an implementation-theory literature reference; no edition years cited).
**Links:** GOV-1043, GOV-1044, GOV-1046 (Design realism).
**Trap:** Treating bottom-up as the whole truth ignores design failure.
**Mains hook:** Reframe "did the Act work?" as "what did centres actually do?" and then test the design.
**Back:** *"Top-down checks the match; bottom-up asks what really happened."*

### Flashcard GOV-1046 — Design realism vs design ambition
**Front:** What is the difference between design ambition and design realism?
**Definition (learn):** A policy can be ambitious (wide coverage, high targets) without being realistic (matched to administrative capacity). The gap is a design-stage cause, distinct from delivery-stage causes.
**Breakdown:**
- Ambition: wide coverage and high targets.
- Realism: matched to staff, budget, systems and capacity.
- Example: universal-coverage targets with unfilled sanctioned posts.
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** Owner 02 analytical distinction (⚠️ analytical, not a cited statute).
**Links:** GOV-1041, GOV-1042, GOV-1043.
**Trap:** Blaming delivery for a design that was never matched to capacity.
**Mains hook:** Use it for "why did this policy fail": check design first, delivery second.
**Back:** *"Ambition without capacity is a design failure."*

### Flashcard GOV-1047 — Coping routines of street-level bureaucrats: rationing and creaming
**Front:** What are Lipsky's coping routines, and how do they explain welfare exclusion without alleging corruption?
**Definition (learn):** Predictable responses to heavy caseloads and resource scarcity: rationing of time and attention, "creaming" (serving easier cases first) and informal rule-bending.
**Breakdown:**
- Rationing: limiting time or attention per case.
- Creaming: serving easier-to-serve beneficiaries first.
- Informal rule-bending: shortcuts under pressure.
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** Lipsky (✅ owner 02).
**Links:** GOV-1043 (Lipsky), GOV-1050 (inclusion vs exclusion error), GOV-1083 (guided discretion).
**Trap:** Coping routines are not random dereliction; they are predictable under caseload pressure. Exclusion can occur without corruption.
**Mains hook:** Requires caseload or staffing evidence before asserting exclusion in a specific case.
**Back:** *"Ration the time, cream the easy, bend the rule — exclusion without a bribe."*

### Flashcard GOV-1048 — Six-stage implementation map
**Front:** Name the six stages of the owner's implementation map, and what it tests.
**Definition (learn):** A map to apply to any policy: legal authority, rules and SOPs, responsible agency, staff/money/technology, citizen interface, grievance and review.
**Breakdown:**
- 1. Legal authority: Act, rule, guideline or executive scheme; who is bound?
- 2. Rules and SOPs: entitlement, eligibility and procedure specified?
- 3. Responsible agency: Union, State, district, local or parastatal named?
- 4. Staff, money, tech: sanctioned vs filled posts; fund-flow route; data systems.
- 5. Citizen interface: awareness, documents, distance, language, digital access.
- 6. Grievance and review: appeal, penalty, evaluation, sunset or renewal clause.
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** Owner 02 teaching map (⚠️ analytical).
**Links:** GOV-1041, GOV-1060 (Public Examinations Act stages), GOV-1023 (eight-stage chain in file 01).
**Trap:** "The Act exists" answers stage 1 only; an enacted law can stall at any later stage.
**Mains hook:** Walk the six stages and stop at the one the stem is about.
**Back:** *"Authority, rules, agency, resources, citizen, remedy — stop where it stalls."*

### Flashcard GOV-1049 — Convergence of schemes and district convergence committees
**Front:** What is convergence, and what does it remove?
**Definition (learn):** Deliberately aligning multiple schemes or departments toward a common local outcome, instead of running them as isolated silos.
**Breakdown:**
- Example: linking a livelihoods scheme with a watershed or skilling scheme.
- Mechanism: removes clearance points and duplicate targeting between parallel schemes.
- Institutions: district administration and Panchayat-level convergence committees (⚠️ commonly used, see owner 02).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** Convergence definition (✅ owner 02); committee use (⚠️ analytical).
**Links:** GOV-1044 (clearance points), GOV-1051 (Aspirational Districts), GOV-1074 (Gati Shakti coordination).
**Trap:** Convergence adds no new scheme; it removes clearance points between existing ones. It fails where functional devolution is absent.
**Mains hook:** Explain what convergence removes before naming any programme; use it as the corrective whenever schemes overlap.
**Back:** *"Converge, don't create: remove the clearance points between schemes."*

### Flashcard GOV-1050 — Inclusion error (E1) vs exclusion error (E2)
**Front:** Define inclusion error and exclusion error, and state the trade-off in tightening verification.
**Definition (learn):** E1 = ineligible person included. E2 = eligible person excluded.
**Breakdown:**
- Tightening verification reduces E1.
- Tightening verification increases E2.
- Governance resolution: fix the tolerable E2 first for subsistence entitlements; add offline or assisted fallback.
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** Owner 02 targeting bank (⚠️ analytical).
**Links:** GOV-1047 (coping routines), GOV-1078 (universalism vs targeting), GOV-1063 (allocation vs release).
**Trap:** Never present one error as the other; DBT authentication corrects inclusion error while creating a distinct exclusion-error risk.
**Mains hook:** Name the error type first, then the corrective.
**Back:** *"E1 is the wrong person in; E2 is the right person out."*

### Flashcard GOV-1051 — Aspirational Districts Programme: denominators 117 and 112
**Front:** How many districts does the Aspirational Districts Programme cover, and why do sources differ?
**Definition (learn):** NITI Aayog's Aspirational Districts Programme (ADP) identifies districts for inter-district development divergence.
**Breakdown:**
- NITI live page accessed 5 October 2026: 117 selected Aspirational Districts.
- NITI Annual Report 2025-26: 112 operational/reporting districts.
- Dashboard: 49 KPIs across five themes (health and nutrition; education; agriculture and water resources; financial inclusion and skill development; basic infrastructure), tracked on the Champions of Change dashboard.
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** Owner 02 ADP denominator control (✅ owner 02; NITI page and Annual Report 2025-26 cited in owner 02). Not re-fetched (⚠️).
**Links:** GOV-1049 (convergence), GOV-1052 (delta ranking), GOV-1053 (Aspirational Blocks), GOV-1054 (Champions of Change).
**Trap:** Never quote 117 or 112 without its source, date and denominator. Do not assert any district rank or KPI value.
**Mains hook:** The ADP is an administrative-attention and convergence instrument, not primarily a financing one.
**Back:** *"117 selected, 112 reported: quote source and date."*

### Flashcard GOV-1052 — Delta ranking and the three levers of the Aspirational Districts Programme
**Front:** What are the three levers of the Aspirational Districts Programme, and why is delta ranking analytically interesting?
**Definition (learn):** Convergence (Union and State schemes), collaboration (Union/State/district officials and the Prabhari officer arrangement) and competition (delta ranking).
**Breakdown:**
- Delta ranking: ranks on the pace of improvement, not absolute level.
- Effect: the historically worst-off district is not permanently bottom and unmotivated.
- Dashboard: Champions of Change.
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** Owner 02 (✅ owner 02 on the programme's own logic).
**Links:** GOV-1049, GOV-1051, GOV-1053.
**Trap:** Delta ranking rewards pace, not absolute attainment; do not read it as a level ranking.
**Mains hook:** Explain the three levers and their limitation (self-reported data; district unit masks intra-district divergence).
**Back:** *"Converge, collaborate, compete on the delta."*

### Flashcard GOV-1053 — Aspirational Blocks Programme: 513 blocks
**Front:** Why does the Aspirational Blocks Programme exist alongside the Aspirational Districts Programme?
**Definition (learn):** A separate, non-substitutable unit covering blocks, because a district-level unit can mask intra-district (block, habitation) divergence.
**Breakdown:**
- Blocks covered: 513 (owner 02).
- Unit: block, smaller than district.
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** 513 blocks (✅ owner 02; not re-fetched).
**Links:** GOV-1051, GOV-1052, GOV-1054 (Champions of Change).
**Trap:** Do not substitute the block programme for the district programme or vice versa.
**Mains hook:** District averages hide block-level variation; a block-level unit is needed for the last mile.
**Back:** *"513 blocks, because districts hide differences."*

### Flashcard GOV-1054 — Champions of Change dashboard
**Front:** What is the Champions of Change dashboard, and what does it track?
**Definition (learn):** The dashboard tracking the Aspirational Districts Programme KPIs, across 49 KPIs and five themes.
**Breakdown:**
- Tracks: 49 KPIs across five themes.
- Data: largely district self-reported with validation by selected agencies.
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** 49 KPIs, five themes (✅ owner 02).
**Links:** GOV-1051, GOV-1052, GOV-1029 (index tests).
**Trap:** A KPI improvement is an output measure; it does not prove outcome change.
**Mains hook:** Apply the five index tests to dashboards: validity, comparability, output vs outcome, context, consequence.
**Back:** *"Forty-nine KPIs, self-reported, output-heavy."*

### Flashcard GOV-1055 — Trap: implementation failure always means corruption
**Front:** Does implementation failure always mean corruption?
**Definition (learn):** No. Failure can arise from unrealistic design, excessive clearance points, or discretion under real resource constraints. Corruption is one possible cause among several.
**Breakdown:**
- Design failure (realism).
- Clearance-point multiplication.
- Discretion under resource constraint.
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** Owner 02 trap (✅ owner 02).
**Links:** GOV-1042, GOV-1044, GOV-1047.
**Trap:** Naming corruption as the only cause loses marks.
**Mains hook:** Diagnose the stage first; corruption is evidence to test, not a default explanation.
**Back:** *"Corruption is one cause, not the cause."*

### Flashcard GOV-1056 — Trap: Pre-Legislative Consultation Policy (2014) is executive, not statutory
**Front:** What does the 2014 Pre-Legislative Consultation Policy recommend, and what is its legal status?
**Definition (learn):** The Union government's 2014 policy recommends publishing draft legislation or rules, with an explanatory note and a minimum comment period, before introduction.
**Breakdown:**
- Recommended comment period: around 30 days (ordinary practice per owner 02).
- Status: executive policy, not a statute. Comments are not binding.
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** 2014 policy (✅ owner 02). Comment period is owner-stated ordinary practice (⚠️).
**Links:** GOV-1025 (MGNREGA s.17 social audit), GOV-1057 (consultation is not a formality), GOV-1058 (RIA).
**Trap:** Consultation compliance (publication) is not consultation depth (incorporation of feedback).
**Mains hook:** Argue that a comment window without a response matrix is compliance only.
**Back:** *"Publish, comment, respond — the 2014 policy only asks."*

### Flashcard GOV-1057 — Trap: consultation is not a procedural formality
**Front:** Is consultation before formulation only a formality?
**Definition (learn):** No. Pre-legislative consultation and RIA are meant to surface implementation problems before they become costly delivery failures.
**Breakdown:**
- Consultation compliance: publication of a draft.
- Consultation depth: substantive incorporation of feedback.
- Effect: a well-designed law that skips genuine consultation can still fail at delivery.
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** Owner 02 trap (✅ owner 02).
**Links:** GOV-1056 (2014 policy), GOV-1058 (RIA), GOV-1079 (speed vs consultation trade-off).
**Trap:** A published draft with no response is compliance without depth.
**Mains hook:** Test whether feedback was used, not only whether comments were invited.
**Back:** *"Consultation without a response is ceremony."*

### Flashcard GOV-1058 — Regulatory Impact Assessment (RIA): emerging and unevenly institutionalised
**Front:** What is RIA, and what is its status in India?
**Definition (learn):** A structured ex-ante assessment of a proposed regulation's costs, benefits and alternatives before finalisation.
**Breakdown:**
- Purpose: improve policy design quality.
- Status: increasingly recommended but not uniformly mandatory across Indian regulators and ministries.
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** Owner 02 (⚠️ "emerging, unevenly institutionalised"; no statutory date asserted).
**Links:** GOV-1056, GOV-1057, GOV-1044.
**Trap:** Do not describe RIA as a uniformly binding requirement across all ministries and regulators.
**Mains hook:** Cite RIA as an emerging good-governance practice; propose making it systematic.
**Back:** *"RIA weighs costs before rules bite; not yet uniform."*

### Flashcard GOV-1059 — Policy, programme, scheme, law, rule and guideline: distinct authorities
**Front:** Distinguish policy, programme, scheme, law, rule and guideline by authority.
**Definition (learn):** These labels are not interchangeable. A policy states a course; a programme coordinates multiple activities or schemes; a scheme is an operational administrative design; a law is enacted by the competent legislature; a rule is delegated legislation within a parent statute; a guideline guides administration and binds only where lawful authority supports it.
**Breakdown:**
- Policy: governing choice.
- Programme: coordinates schemes or activities.
- Scheme: operational benefit or service design.
- Law: enacted by the legislature.
- Rule: delegated legislation within the parent Act.
- Guideline: administrative guidance; binds only with lawful authority.
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** Owner 02 taxonomy (✅ owner 02).
**Links:** GOV-1060 (Public Examinations Act), GOV-1064 (legislature and delegated legislation), GOV-1062 (Cabinet approval is not law).
**Trap:** A guideline cannot silently amend a law; a rule cannot exceed its parent Act.
**Mains hook:** Use the correct label in each answer; wrong labels signal weak understanding.
**Back:** *"Policy states, programme coordinates, scheme delivers, law binds, rule follows, guideline guides."*

### Flashcard GOV-1060 — Public Examinations (Prevention of Unfair Means) Act, 2024: commencement and coverage
**Front:** When did the Public Examinations (Prevention of Unfair Means) Act, 2024 come into force, through which notification, and which authorities does it cover?
**Definition (learn):** Central Act targeting paper leaks, impersonation and tampering in public examinations conducted by specified authorities.
**Breakdown:**
- In force: 21 June 2024, through S.O. 2422(E) (owner 02).
- Covers examinations of scheduled or Central-Government-notified authorities: UPSC, SSC, Railway Recruitment Boards, IBPS, NTA, Central Government departments and attached offices.
- Not automatically covered: State-board or university examinations (coverage needs notification or separate State law).
- Passed by Parliament 9 February 2024; assented 25 February 2024; Act No. 1 of 2024 (✅ Wikipedia, secondary).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** 21 June 2024 and S.O. 2422(E) (✅ owner 02; commencement date ✅ Wikipedia). Parliamentary and assent dates ✅ Wikipedia (secondary source; a LLM-notice applies; ⚠️ check official text).
**Links:** GOV-1061 (Amendment Act 2026), GOV-1068 (2024 Q11 PYQ), GOV-1059 (rule vs law).
**Trap:** The Act's application to a State board or university exam is not automatic from territorial extent. Scope is authority-based, not subject-based. Wikipedia did not confirm the Schedule text (⚠️).
**Mains hook:** Separate the aims (preventing unfair means, protecting credibility) from the scope (authority-based coverage). Name the notification step for any expansion.
**Back:** *"Twenty-one June two-four, S.O. 2422: central authorities, not all boards."*

### Flashcard GOV-1061 — Public Examinations (Prevention of Unfair Means) Amendment Act, 2026 (Act 10 of 2026)
**Front:** What does Act 10 of 2026 change, and when was it published?
**Definition (learn):** Amendment to the 2024 Act that strengthens penalties and adds time-bound investigation and trial machinery.
**Breakdown:**
- Act number: Act 10 of 2026.
- Assent and publication: 31 July 2026.
- Gazette: CG-DL-E-31072026-275035.
- Adds: time-bound investigation, Special Fast Track Courts, Special Public Prosecutors, appellate route.
- Does not change: the authority-based coverage test.
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** 31 July 2026 (✅ owner 02; commencement "31 July 2026" ✅ Wikipedia secondary).
**Links:** GOV-1060, GOV-1068.
**Trap:** The amendment strengthens enforcement; it does not turn every State or university exam into a covered examination.
**Mains hook:** A tougher penalty regime does not solve a scope question; name both separately.
**Back:** *"Act ten of twenty-six: tougher penalties, same coverage test."*

### Flashcard GOV-1062 — Institutional responsibility map: Cabinet approval is not law or delivery
**Front:** Which institution takes major executive policy decisions, and why is Cabinet approval not enactment or delivery?
**Definition (learn):** The Cabinet / Council of Ministers takes major executive policy decisions under the constitutional business framework, but Cabinet approval is neither enacted law nor proof of delivery.
**Breakdown:**
- Cabinet: executive policy decision.
- Line ministry: formulates, prepares legislation or rules, coordinates, answers for the programme.
- Implementing agencies, States and local bodies: execute within legal powers.
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** Constitutional business framework (✅ owner 02; Articles 77 and Allocation of Business Rules 1961, per owner 02 sources).
**Links:** GOV-1059 (instrument taxonomy), GOV-1063 (Finance Ministry stages), GOV-1064 (legislature), GOV-1066 (CAG ex-post).
**Trap:** Cabinet approval does not commence a law, appropriate money or deliver a service.
**Mains hook:** Map each act to the institution that performs it before evaluating a policy.
**Back:** *"Cabinet decides, ministry designs, agencies deliver — none is proof of the others."*

### Flashcard GOV-1063 — Ministry of Finance: allocation, release, expenditure and outcome are separate
**Front:** Why are allocation, release, expenditure and outcome separate stages in public finance?
**Definition (learn):** The Ministry of Finance tests fiscal affordability and supports budget and expenditure control. Allocation (budget provision) is not release (fund flow), release is not expenditure, and expenditure is not outcome.
**Breakdown:**
- Allocation: budget provision.
- Release: funds moved to implementing agency.
- Expenditure: money spent.
- Outcome: measured effect for citizens.
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** Owner 02 (✅ owner 02, Budget and PFMS material cited).
**Links:** GOV-1062, GOV-1066, GOV-1050.
**Trap:** Quoting an allocation as delivered; quoting expenditure as outcome.
**Mains hook:** Use the four stages to test a scheme's claims.
**Back:** *"Allocated is not released, released is not spent, spent is not delivered."*

### Flashcard GOV-1064 — Legislature and delegated legislation: the executive is bounded by the parent Act
**Front:** What limits executive rules made under delegated power?
**Definition (learn):** Parliament or a State legislature enacts the parent law and authorises expenditure. The executive may make rules only within delegated power. Rules remain open to legislative scrutiny and judicial review for excess delegation.
**Breakdown:**
- Parent law: legislature.
- Rules and regulations: delegated, bounded by parent Act.
- Guideline: cannot amend law silently.
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** Owner 02 (✅ owner 02; Constitutional framework cited in owner 02).
**Links:** GOV-1059, GOV-1062, GOV-1066.
**Trap:** An executive rule cannot exceed the parent Act; a guideline cannot amend a statute.
**Mains hook:** Check the parent Act before attributing force to a rule or guideline.
**Back:** *"Rule follows the Act; guideline never amends it."*

### Flashcard GOV-1065 — NITI Aayog: advisory and coordinating, not binding
**Front:** What are the powers and limits of NITI Aayog as a policy institution?
**Definition (learn):** NITI Aayog is an advisory, coordinating and evaluation institution; it does not legislate, allocate the divisible pool, bind States or directly implement every programme.
**Breakdown:**
- Functions: advice, coordination, knowledge, monitoring and evaluation.
- Not: legislator, Finance Commission, binding authority on States.
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** Owner 02 (✅ owner 02).
**Links:** GOV-1066 (CAG), GOV-1067 (Finance Commission), GOV-1051 (ADP is NITI's programme).
**Trap:** NITI Aayog does not allocate tax devolution (Finance Commission, Article 280).
**Mains hook:** Separate advice (NITI), constitutional transfer (Finance Commission) and ex-post audit (CAG).
**Back:** *"NITI advises, it does not bind or divide the pool."*

### Flashcard GOV-1066 — CAG: ex-post audit, not pre-audit
**Front:** What is the CAG's role in the policy-implementation chain, and why is it not a pre-auditor?
**Definition (learn):** The CAG conducts constitutionally and statutorily authorised ex-post audit and reports to the legislature. It is not a pre-auditor that approves policy or expenditure before execution.
**Breakdown:**
- Ex-post: after execution.
- Reports to: legislature (PAC scrutiny).
- Constitutional basis: Articles 148–151 (owner 02 sources).
- Not: pre-approver, policy approval authority, prosecutor or recovery authority.
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** Articles 148–151 (✅ owner 02). CAG (Duties, Powers and Conditions of Service) Act 1971 (✅ owner 02 source list).
**Links:** GOV-1026 (accountability bodies), GOV-1065, GOV-1068.
**Trap:** Expecting the CAG to approve a scheme before launch.
**Mains hook:** Place CAG audit at the accountability stage, after delivery, then PAC scrutiny.
**Back:** *"CAG audits after the spend, the PAC follows."*

### Flashcard GOV-1067 — Finance Commission (Article 280): constitutional transfers, not CSS design
**Front:** What does the Finance Commission under Article 280 recommend, and what does it not do?
**Definition (learn):** It recommends constitutional tax devolution and grants. It does not design or administer Centrally Sponsored Schemes (CSS).
**Breakdown:**
- Recommends: tax devolution, grants, local-body augmentation.
- Does not: design, approve or administer CSS.
- Detailed CSS fund flow belongs to Topic 13.
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** Article 280 (✅ owner 02). 16th Finance Commission report referenced in owner 02 sources (not re-fetched).
**Links:** GOV-1065, GOV-1068, GOV-1063.
**Trap:** Constitutional transfers and CSS are different routes.
**Mains hook:** A Finance Commission grant is not a CSS design decision; keep the routes separate.
**Back:** *"Article 280 recommends transfers; CSS runs on a different route."*

### Flashcard GOV-1068 — 2024 GS-II Q11: aims and scope of the Public Examinations Act
**Front:** What does the 2024 GS-II Q11 ask, and how should the answer be structured?
**Definition (learn):** The question asks for the aims and objects of the Public Examinations (Prevention of Unfair Means) Act, 2024, and whether university or State education-board examinations are covered.
**Breakdown:**
- Aims: prevent unfair means; protect credibility of public examinations.
- Scope: authority-based coverage (scheduled or notified authorities).
- Structure: state the aims; then address scope directly, separately.
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** Verified normalised transcription, 2024 GS-II Q11, 15 marks, 250 words (✅ owner 02; official local paper books/mains/02 UPSC 2024 Paper-II.pdf, PDF p. 3).
**Links:** GOV-1060, GOV-1061, GOV-1069 (scope trap).
**Trap:** Describing the Act generally without answering the scope question.
**Mains hook:** The 2024 pattern rewards separating aims from scope.
**Back:** *"Aims first, then scope — answer both halves."*

### Flashcard GOV-1069 — Trap: the 2024 Act automatically covers all state board and university exams
**Front:** Does the 2024 Public Examinations Act automatically cover every state board or university examination?
**Definition (learn):** No. The Act applies to examinations conducted by scheduled or Central-Government-notified public examination authorities. Ordinary state-board or university exams are not automatically covered.
**Breakdown:**
- Covered: central authorities listed in Schedule or later notified.
- Not automatic: state boards, universities.
- Needs: notification or separate State legislation.
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** Owner 02 (✅ owner 02). Wikipedia confirms the Act's commencement but not the Schedule text (⚠️).
**Links:** GOV-1060, GOV-1068.
**Trap:** Territorial extent is not coverage.
**Mains hook:** Separate territorial extent from authority-based coverage.
**Back:** *"Extent is not coverage; authority is."*

### Flashcard GOV-1070 — 2024 GS-II Q17: public healthcare and the marketisation problem
**Front:** What does the 2024 GS-II Q17 test on public healthcare, and how should the answer treat design and delivery?
**Definition (learn):** The Q17 question asks how the Indian State should contain marketisation in public healthcare and enhance grassroots reach.
**Breakdown:**
- Design stage: financing and regulation of private providers.
- Delivery stage: primary health infrastructure and grassroots reach.
- A strong answer treats both stages.
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** 2024 GS-II Q17 (✅ owner 02 verified transcription).
**Links:** GOV-1041 (policy cycle), GOV-1042 (implementation gap), GOV-1049 (convergence), GOV-1078 (regulation and provision trade-off).
**Trap:** Drifting into a scheme list without a governance lens.
**Mains hook:** Regulate the market and strengthen delivery together; neither alone works.
**Back:** *"Regulate the market, strengthen the last mile: design and delivery together."*

### Flashcard GOV-1071 — 2018 GS-I Q17: aspirational districts transformation and convergence, collaboration, competition
**Front:** What does the 2018 GS-I Q17 ask, and what are the three levers it names?
**Definition (learn):** The question asks for core strategies for transforming aspirational districts and an explanation of convergence, collaboration and competition.
**Breakdown:**
- Problem: inter-district development divergence.
- Levers: convergence, collaboration, competition (delta ranking).
- Limit: self-reported data; intra-district divergence masked.
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** Verified normalised transcription (owner 02, books/more_previous_papers/GENERAL-STUDIES-PAPER-I.pdf, PDF p. 4). Marks: 15 (owner 02).
**Links:** GOV-1051, GOV-1052, GOV-1053.
**Trap:** Describing aspirational districts without explaining the levers.
**Mains hook:** Attach each lever to a clearance-point or attention mechanism.
**Back:** *"Converge, collaborate, compete on delta: the three levers."*

### Flashcard GOV-1072 — 2018 GS-II Q6: policy contradictions and environmental degradation
**Front:** What does the 2018 GS-II Q6 ask, and what is the analytical core?
