**Key facts / Dates / Data:** ✅ (owner 06 §3 causal chain, §6, §13.6). Owner ⚠️ analytical.
**Links:** GOV-2001, GOV-2015, GOV-2025 (four safeguards), GOV-2033 (algorithmic accountability).
**Trap:** The owner lists "automatically compatible" as an advanced trap; the tension is structural, not solved by default.
**Mains hook:** Core 15/20-mark argument: interoperability is the value and purpose limitation is the test; resolve by layer-specific design, not a single legal fix.
**Back:** *"Reuse rails, tag every purpose."*

### Flashcard GOV-2024 — Presence-less, paperless, cashless (DPI design philosophy)
**Front:** What is India Stack's original "presence-less, paperless, cashless" design philosophy, and what is its hidden cost?
**Definition (learn):** The design goal is identity without physical presence, documents without paper and transactions without cash; each removed friction also removes the traditional fallback for citizens who cannot use the digital substitute.
**Breakdown:**
- Presence-less: Aadhaar/eKYC.
- Paperless: DigiLocker.
- Cashless: UPI.
- Hidden cost: every removed friction was someone's fallback.
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** ✅ (owner 06 §2, advanced 06 §2 labels it as the India Stack philosophy).
**Links:** GOV-2003, GOV-2006, GOV-2007, GOV-2026 (digital illiteracy), GOV-2027 (intermediary paradox).
**Trap:** "Efficiency gain" without the fallback question is an incomplete answer.
**Mains hook:** Use T3 (owner 06 §13.2): efficiency at the median is compatible with denial at the tail.
**Back:** *"No presence, no paper, no cash, no fallback."*

### Flashcard GOV-2025 — Four safeguards for any DPI proposal
**Front:** What four safeguards must any DPI proposal carry?
**Definition (learn):** The four-safeguard test: (1) necessity and proportionality; (2) data minimisation and purpose tagging; (3) assisted and offline fallback; (4) reasoned human review and appeal.
**Breakdown:**
- 1. Necessity and proportionality — Puttaswamy framework (Polity owns the derivation).
- 2. Minimisation and purpose tagging — collect minimum, record purpose, no silent reuse.
- 3. Assisted/offline fallback — non-digital route must survive for entitlements.
- 4. Reasoned human review/appeal — adverse automated outcome must be explainable and contestable.
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** ✅ (owner 06 §13.7, §10). The safeguards are the owner's analytical framework.
**Links:** GOV-2023, GOV-2026, GOV-2032, GOV-2033.
**Trap:** Presenting a safeguard as satisfied by a portal or registration alone (owner §Semantic-completeness control).
**Mains hook:** Apply the four tests to ONDC, ABDM, AI-assisted delivery or any new consent layer (owner 06 §13.1 "novel DPI" row).
**Back:** *"Necessity, minimise, fallback, human appeal."*

### Flashcard GOV-2026 — Six constraints of rural digital illiteracy (2021 GS-II Q16)
**Front:** Which six binding constraints disaggregate rural digital illiteracy in the 2021 GS-II Q16 answer route?
**Definition (learn):** The 2021 GS-II Q16 (15 marks, examine with justification) asked about digital illiteracy in rural areas and access to information; the owner disaggregates it into six separate constraints.
**Breakdown:**
- 1. Device access and sharing (one phone controlled by one member).
- 2. Connectivity quality and cost, not merely coverage.
- 3. Language and script (content and English error messages).
- 4. Functional digital literacy (ability to complete a task).
- 5. Trust and fear of error (suppresses independent use).
- 6. Authentication failure (worn fingerprints, address mismatch, connectivity drop).
- Governance consequence: as services migrate online, illiteracy becomes access denial.
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** ✅ (owner 06 §13.0; PYQ 2021 GS-II Q16, 15 marks, 250 words; stem partly truncated in scan per owner). Do not cite internet or PMGDISHA figures (owner prohibition).
**Links:** GOV-2024, GOV-2027, GOV-2025, GOV-2030.
**Trap:** "Digital literacy is low" without disaggregation cannot be examined; the stem is about access to information.
**Mains hook:** Verdict: a public service must be usable by the least-capable eligible user, not only by the trained one (owner 06 §13.0 point 7).
**Back:** *"Device, link, language, skill, trust, login."*

### Flashcard GOV-2027 — The intermediary paradox (assisted access)
**Front:** Why do assisted-access channels solve digital illiteracy but create a new governance problem?
**Definition (learn):** Assisted access (Common Service Centres, shopkeepers, family members) is the realistic remedy, but each new intermediary brings a fee, an error rate and access to the citizen's credentials, reproducing the discretion digitisation was meant to remove.
**Breakdown:**
- Benefit: reach and assistance.
- Risk: fee, error, credential access, discretion.
- Corrective: published fees, audit, reasoned rejection, non-digital appeal.
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** ✅ (owner 06 §13.0 point 5); Common Service Centres named as the owner's example (owner-named, not further detailed).
**Links:** GOV-2026, GOV-2024, GOV-2025.
**Trap:** Do not treat assisted access as a costless fix; the owner calls it "a genuine trade-off, not a costless fix" (§5).
**Mains hook:** Highest-value point for the 2021 digital-illiteracy question: intermediary is both solution and new risk.
**Back:** *"Helper solves, helper can also take a cut."*

### Flashcard GOV-2028 — Digital India Land Records Modernisation Programme (2024 Prelims)
**Front:** What does the 2024 Prelims GS-I Q98 demand on the Digital India Land Records Modernisation Programme test?
**Definition (learn):** The 2024 Prelims GS-I Q98 demand names the Digital India Land Records Modernisation Programme (DILRMP) and is an objective question; the official Set-A key is present in the repository, but the answer is not recorded here.
**Breakdown:**
- Demand type: named programme and its statement-level distinctions.
- Key status: official Set-A key present locally; answer not inferred (owner 06 §9 PYQ integration).
- Owner requirement: cover the named fact and its likely statement-level distinctions.
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** ✅ (owner 06, 2024-2025 PYQ integration block). ⚠️ Programme objectives, launch year and ministry were not stated by the owner and were not fetched; verify before using any figure.
**Links:** GOV-2001, `13` (public finance and service tools), `05` (e-governance).
**Trap:** Do not answer the objective demand from memory; use the official key.
**Mains hook:** Land-record digitisation is a DPI-adjacent service: link to records, title and grievance routes (`08`).
**Back:** *"Land records, digitised, statement-tested."* ⚠️

### Flashcard GOV-2029 — Anonymised vs pseudonymised vs personal data
**Front:** Distinguish personal data, pseudonymised data and anonymised data for DPI risk.
**Definition (learn):** Personal data relates to an identifiable person; pseudonymised data replaces identifiers but can be re-linked; anonymised data is claimed to be non-identifiable and must be tested.
**Breakdown:**
- Personal: directly or indirectly identifiable.
- Pseudonymised: identifiers replaced; re-identification possible with a key.
- Anonymised: claim of non-identifiability; re-identification risk tested, not assumed.
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** ✅ (owner 06 §13.5 distinction list). No statutory section number is asserted in the owner for these three terms.
**Links:** GOV-2013, GOV-2012 (health data re-identification), GOV-2014.
**Trap:** "Anonymised" is a claim to be tested, not a status to assume.
**Mains hook:** Health and welfare datasets are where re-identification risk is highest; insist on tested anonymisation.
**Back:** *"Anonymous is a claim to test."*

### Flashcard GOV-2030 — Grievance routing discipline for DPI problems
**Front:** Where should each type of DPI-related grievance be routed?
**Definition (learn):** Route each grievance to the mechanism that owns that harm, not to a single generic portal.
**Breakdown:**
- Service delay → department / RTS / CPGRAMS (`07`).
- Information denial → RTI / Central Information Commission (`08`).
- Personal-data breach → DPBI, once members appointed and duties commence.
- Financial-data consent → RBI's regulated AA framework.
- Health-record consent → ABDM's own architecture.
- Sectoral service dispute → the sector regulator (`11`).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** ✅ (owner 06 §13.8 ⚠️ routing; CPGRAMS and RTI routes are cross-referenced to owners `07` and `08`).
**Links:** GOV-2017, GOV-2008, GOV-2012, GOV-2033.
**Trap:** Routing a personal-data breach to a body not yet adjudicating (the DPBI before appointments) is a practical error.
**Mains hook:** Good grievance design maps each harm to its authority and a human appeal (owner 06 §13.8).
**Back:** *"Each harm, its own door."*

### Flashcard GOV-2031 — India AI Governance Guidelines (MeitY, 5 November 2025)
**Front:** What are the India AI Governance Guidelines, when were they released and what is their legal status?
**Definition (learn):** The India AI Governance Guidelines (subtitle "Enabling Safe and Trusted AI Innovation") were released by MeitY on 5 November 2025; they are a non-statutory, voluntary-compliance framework, not an AI Act or binding code.
**Breakdown:**
- Principles: trust, people first, innovation over restraint, fairness and equity, accountability, understandability by design, safety and sustainability.
- Legal status: non-statutory, voluntary compliance.
- Use in service delivery: testing error rates by group, explainable reasons, human review, audit log.
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** ✅ (owner 06 §13.8, date and title stated in owner). ⚠️ Legal status as characterised by owner; not fetched.
**Links:** GOV-2033 (algorithmic accountability), GOV-2025, GOV-2012.
**Trap:** Do not describe the guidelines as an AI statute; they are voluntary.
**Mains hook:** "Augment officials, do not erase the duty-holder": an adverse welfare decision must remain attributable to a public authority.
**Back:** *"Five November, voluntary, principles not law."*

### Flashcard GOV-2032 — Authentication exclusion vs offline fallback (trade-off)
**Front:** Why does the offline or manual override fix for biometric failure not come without cost?
**Definition (learn):** Authentication-based delivery improves efficiency for most users but creates an exclusion tail; the offline or manual override mitigates it but can reintroduce the discretion and leakage problems DPI was meant to reduce.
**Breakdown:**
- Example: a PDS beneficiary denied grain after biometric authentication failure despite entitlement.
- Corrective: offline/manual override.
- Cost: reintroduced discretion and leakage.
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** ⚠️ Example is the owner's illustrative case (advanced 06 §5); no PDS statistics asserted.
**Links:** GOV-2003, GOV-2025, GOV-2026, GOV-2030.
**Trap:** "Aadhaar authentication is failure-proof" is false (owner 06 §7).
**Mains hook:** Concede the scale gain, then design the tail's fallback, accepting the discretion risk.
**Back:** *"Fallback fixes exclusion, reopens discretion."*

### Flashcard GOV-2033 — Algorithmic accountability chain for public services
**Front:** What minimum chain of controls should an automated public decision have?
**Definition (learn):** Where eligibility, ranking or flagging is automated, the citizen must know the decision was automated, obtain its reason and have a human review it; the design must state which error it optimises against.
**Breakdown:**
- Chain: lawful purpose → representative data → tested error rates by group → understandable reason → human review/appeal → audit log and incident reporting → periodic outcome and bias review.
- Error asymmetry: false negative (eligible rejected) vs false positive (ineligible included) are not symmetric harms.
- Audit trail must be designed in, not retrofitted.
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** ✅ (owner 06 §13.8 chain and error-asymmetry statements; ⚠️ analytical).
**Links:** GOV-2031, GOV-2025, GOV-2023.
**Trap:** Treating welfare error types as symmetric is a common answer error.
**Mains hook:** Augment officials, do not erase the duty-holder: decisions must stay explainable, reviewable and attributable.
**Back:** *"Lawful, representative, tested, explained, reviewed, logged."*

### Flashcard GOV-2034 — Constitutional challenge to the RTI amendment (status)
**Front:** What is the current legal status of the constitutional challenges to the RTI amendment made through DPDP s.44(3)?
**Definition (learn):** Challenges to the RTI amendment have been filed; as of 13 August 2026 no final judgment had been located, so no outcome can be stated.
**Breakdown:**
- Amendment: DPDP s.44(3) substituting RTI s.8(1)(j), in force since Nov 2025 (GOV-2020).
- Challenge: filed; pending.
- Answer rule: state the amendment and that challenges are pending; do not state an outcome.
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** ⚠️ As of 13 Aug 2026 per owner 06 §8 correction record; no final judgment located.
**Links:** GOV-2020, GOV-2013, `08` transparency cards.
**Trap:** Stating that the courts struck down or upheld the amendment is unsupported.
**Mains hook:** Privacy and transparency conflict here; the proportionality test (GOV-2025) is the analytical tool.
**Back:** *"Filed, pending, no verdict yet."*

### Flashcard GOV-2035 — Cyber-resilience and concentration risk in DPI
**Front:** Why is continuity planning a governance obligation for DPI rails?
**Definition (learn):** Rails used by everything fail for everything; a concentrated shared system creates single points of failure, so continuity and cyber-resilience must be designed as governance, not left to IT.
**Breakdown:**
- Concentration: shared identity/payment rails.
- Consequence: a failure propagates across services.
- Obligation: continuity planning and security audit.
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** ⚠️ Analytical claim in owner 06 §13.8; no incident data asserted.
**Links:** GOV-2001, GOV-2011, GOV-2025.
**Trap:** Treating outages as only technical; the owner counts them as a governance matter.
**Mains hook:** Use with the 20-mark answer on sovereignty and concentration (owner 06 §13.3).
**Back:** *"Shared rails, shared failure, plan for it."*

### Flashcard GOV-2036 — Puttaswamy privacy foundation for DPI
**Front:** Which constitutional case supplies the right-to-privacy foundation for data-protection law, and where is it owned?
**Definition (learn):** Justice K.S. Puttaswamy v. Union of India (2017) supplies the constitutional foundation, the right to privacy under Article 21; the Polity owner holds the constitutional derivation.
**Breakdown:**
- Case: Justice K.S. Puttaswamy v. Union of India, 2017.
- Constitutional basis: Article 21 (owner 06 §3 deeper layers).
- Test: proportionality (necessity, suitability, least-restrictive means, balancing).
- Ownership: Polity/Fundamental Rights (`Polity/advanced/07`).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** ✅ (owner 06 §3, §10, §13.1 — cross-link to Polity). Article 21 reference is as stated in owner.
**Links:** GOV-2025, GOV-2013, GOV-2023.
**Trap:** Do not re-derive the constitutional doctrine here; cross-link Polity.
**Mains hook:** Use Puttaswamy's proportionality test for "privacy vs welfare targeting" stems (owner 06 §13.1).
**Back:** *"Twenty-one, proportion, privacy."*

### GROUP — Citizen-Centric Administration

### Flashcard GOV-2037 — Citizen-centric administration as an institutional architecture
**Front:** What does citizen-centric administration mean in Governance, and what is it not?
**Definition (learn):** A set of institutions and tools that makes a public service accessible, time-bound, enforceable and remediable for the citizen. It is an architecture, not one charter document.
**Breakdown:**
- Sevottam's three pillars: Citizens' Charter (standard) + grievance redress (remedy) + service-delivery capability (capacity to deliver).
- Practice layer: state RTS laws (statutory timelines and penalty), CPGRAMS (Union grievance portal), single-window delivery.
- Core proposition: a charter alone is necessary but far from sufficient.
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** ✅ owner 07 (basic). Owner's grounding: DARPG Sevottam model; state RTS Acts (MP 2010 onward); CPGRAMS; 2024 GS-II PYQ.
**Links:** GOV-2038, GOV-2039, GOV-2040, GOV-2041, GOV-2042, GOV-2058 (status control), GOV-2070 (accountability ecosystem in 08).
**Trap:** Treating "charter exists" as "citizen-centricity achieved". The 2024 PYQ rewards separating adoption from enforceability and capability.
**Mains hook:** "Citizen-centricity needs enforceability, a working grievance channel and delivery capacity together; a charter document covers only the first of these in part." Qualify: statutory conversion without capacity produces penalised officers, not service.
**Back:** *"Promise, Remedy, Capacity — three legs; a charter is only one."*

### Flashcard GOV-2038 — Sevottam (DARPG service-delivery excellence model)
**Front:** What is Sevottam, and what are its components?
**Definition (learn):** DARPG's service-delivery excellence model, built on three components: Citizens' Charter, Public Grievance Redress Mechanism and Service Delivery Capability.
**Breakdown:**
- Citizens' Charter: service standards and timelines.
- Public grievance redress: mechanism when the charter promise is not met.
- Service delivery capability: staff, process and technology to actually deliver.
- Sevottam is an assessment/improvement model, not a statute.
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** ✅ owner 07 (three components). ✅ Wikipedia (Sevottam article): DARPG benchmarking model "Excellence in Public Service Delivery through Sevottam". ⚠️ Naming conflict: Wikipedia labels the third component "Service Delivery Enablers" (customer feedback, employee motivation, infrastructure), while the owner says "Service Delivery Capability". Same idea, different label; use the owner's term in answers. Year of introduction not confirmed by any fetched page.
**Links:** GOV-2039 (charter), GOV-2041 (CPGRAMS), GOV-2050 (capability as pillar 3 in limitation card), GOV-2037.
**Trap:** Sevottam and the Citizens' Charter are not the same thing; the charter is only one of three components (see GOV-2062).
**Mains hook:** "Explain the three components of Sevottam and their interdependence." Argue that a promise without capability is a paper standard, and capability without a grievance route is unaccountable.
**Back:** *"Charter, Grievance, Capability: C-G-C, all three or none."*

### Flashcard GOV-2039 — Citizens' Charter
**Front:** What is a Citizens' Charter, when did India adopt it, and where did the model come from?
**Definition (learn):** A public statement of the services a public authority provides, the standards/timelines for delivery and the remedy if the standard is not met.
**Breakdown:**
- Administrative commitment; voluntary in most cases.
- Publicises an expectation so that failure becomes visible.
- Charters outside RTS-notified services carry no statutory penalty.
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** ✅ owner 07: India's charter initiative dates to 1997, on the UK model. ✅ Wikipedia (Citizen's Charter): UK initiative "launched by the then prime minister, John Major, on 22 July 1991", so the UK precedes India by six years. The India-side 1997 date is ✅ owner 07 only (no India-specific page fetched).
**Links:** GOV-2040 (RTS laws convert charter to entitlement), GOV-2059 (promise vs entitlement), GOV-2048 (limitation 1), GOV-2054 (static charter trap).
**Trap:** A charter is legally enforceable by default. It is not; only RTS-covered services carry a statutory timeline and penalty (GOV-2053).
**Mains hook:** Use the 1997 administrative origin as the diagnosis: a design-stage limitation, not merely an implementation failure.
**Back:** *"1991 UK promise, 1997 India copy, no law behind it."*

### Flashcard GOV-2040 — Right to Public Services (RTS) laws
**Front:** What do state Right to Public Services laws do that a Citizens' Charter does not?
**Definition (learn):** State statutes giving citizens a legally enforceable right to specified public services within a stipulated time, with a penalty on the defaulting official.
**Breakdown:**
- Notified services only, each with a timeline running from a defined trigger.
- Designated officer, appeal and penalty/compensation design, which differ by State.
- Converts a voluntary charter promise into a statutory entitlement.
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** ✅ owner 07 (MP 2010 first, several States followed). Not all States have such Acts; the count is not asserted here (see GOV-2058).
**Links:** GOV-2045 (MP 2010), GOV-2060 (five-element test), GOV-2059 (promise vs entitlement), GOV-2063 (variation).
**Trap:** Describing RTS as one uniform national law. It is State-specific, and each Act covers only its notified list.
**Mains hook:** "Only a Right to Public Services Act creates an entitlement; the charter states a standard." Qualify with the capability counterpoint.
**Back:** *"Notified, Named officer, Timed, Penalised — the RTS test."*

### Flashcard GOV-2041 — CPGRAMS (Centralised Public Grievance Redress and Monitoring System)
**Front:** What is CPGRAMS, who runs it, and what can it not do?
**Definition (learn):** DARPG's administrative platform for lodging, routing, tracking, feedback and appeal of grievances about participating Union ministries/departments and integrated entities.
**Breakdown:**
- Lodge, route, track, feedback, appeal.
- Nodal grievance officers and appellate officers in each Ministry/Department.
- Monitoring and escalation system, not a court.
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** ✅ owner 07 and ✅ owner 08. Administered by DARPG. Benchmarks in GOV-2046.
**Links:** GOV-2046 (benchmarks), GOV-2061 (monitoring not adjudication), GOV-2093 (not the only channel), GOV-2094 (not Lokpal).
**Trap:** CPGRAMS is a statutory right-to-service authority or an ombudsman. It is neither.
**Mains hook:** Explain why CPGRAMS improves visibility of grievances but cannot guarantee resolution, because it cannot compel the receiving department.
**Back:** *"Logs, routes, tracks, appeals — but never adjudicates."*

### Flashcard GOV-2042 — Single-window service delivery
**Front:** What is single-window delivery, and what is its main limit?
**Definition (learn):** An institutional design that lets a citizen access multiple related services/approvals through one integrated point of contact, rather than navigating departments serially.
**Breakdown:**
- Front-office unification for the citizen.
- Can be physical (counter) or digital (portal).
- Reduces navigation cost; does not by itself simplify the back-end approval chain (GOV-2067).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** ✅ owner 07 (definition). ⚠️ Owner gives no specific single-window portal statistics; none are asserted here.
**Links:** GOV-2047 (CSC), GOV-2068 (front counter vs back-end), GOV-2037.
**Trap:** Single-window equals single accountability. The counter may unify the interface while responsibility stays fragmented across departments.
**Mains hook:** Evaluate single-window as a front-end fix that must be paired with process redesign and a named accountable officer.
**Back:** *"One door, many rooms: the door is single, the accountability is not."*

### Flashcard GOV-2043 — Charter design and delivery: Governance vs Ethics boundary
**Front:** Who owns Citizens' Charter design in the UPSC syllabus, Governance or Ethics?
**Definition (learn):** Governance owns service-standard design (service, timeline, responsible officer, user consultation, review) and the delivery architecture (Sevottam, grievance, capability, RTS). Ethics adds work culture and public-service values.
**Breakdown:**
- Governance: institutional architecture and statutory service guarantee.
- Ethics: work-culture, service values and ethical design of charters (`Ethics/basic/17`).
- Answer a Governance stem with architecture, not a work-culture essay.
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** ⚠️ owner 07 analytical boundary. Cross-owner file name cited: Ethics/basic/17_Citizens-Charters-Work-Culture-and-Service-Delivery.md (not read here).
**Links:** GOV-2037, GOV-2038, GOV-2059.
**Trap:** The owner records this as the "Governance needs only portal/RTS machinery, Ethics alone owns Charter design" error. Both are needed; GS-II Governance requires both the promise quality and the delivery architecture.
**Mains hook:** Use Governance for the enforcement limb and Ethics for the culture limb; state which one the question tests.
**Back:** *"Ethics brings the spirit, Governance builds the spine."*

### Flashcard GOV-2044 — DARPG (Department of Administrative Reforms and Public Grievances)
**Front:** Which department is the nodal body for Sevottam, Citizens' Charters and CPGRAMS?
**Definition (learn):** The nodal department for Sevottam, Citizens' Charters and CPGRAMS in the Union government.
**Breakdown:**
- Issues charter-preparation guidelines (measurable standards, grievance officers, periodic review).
- Issues CPGRAMS guidelines (August 2024 comprehensive guidelines, GOV-2046).
- Its guidelines are executive instruments; uptake varies by department.
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** ✅ owner 07 and 07 advanced. ✅ Wikipedia (Sevottam article) names "Department of Administrative Reforms and Public Grievances" as the developer of the Sevottam model.
**Links:** GOV-2038, GOV-2041, GOV-2046.
**Trap:** DARPG guidelines are not statutes. Compliance with a DARPG guideline does not create an enforceable entitlement.
**Mains hook:** Note that DARPG's guidelines improve design quality, but only statutory backing creates an enforceable right.
**Back:** *"DARPG writes the rules of the house, not the laws of the land."*

### Flashcard GOV-2045 — Madhya Pradesh Right to Public Services Act, 2010
**Front:** Which state enacted India's first Right to Public Services Act, and in which year?
**Definition (learn):** Madhya Pradesh's 2010 Act, the pioneering state RTS statute giving specified services a statutory delivery timeline with a penalty for default.
**Breakdown:**
- First Indian state legislation converting specified services into a time-bound, penalty-backed entitlement.
- Designated officer, appeal and penalty design (as described by the owner).
- Followed by several other States, each with its own design.
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** ✅ owner 07 (2010, MP, first). ⚠️ No India-specific fetched page confirmed the MP Act; treat the owner's grounding as the source. Count of States with RTS Acts is not asserted (GOV-2058).
**Links:** GOV-2040, GOV-2063, GOV-2060.
**Trap:** Generalising MP's penalty or appeal design to all States.
**Mains hook:** Use MP 2010 as the model for statutory conversion, then flag its State-specific limits.
**Back:** *"MP 2010: the first to make a promise a right."*

### Flashcard GOV-2046 — CPGRAMS redress and appeal benchmarks (August 2024)
**Front:** What are DARPG's current CPGRAMS benchmarks for grievance redress and appeal?
**Definition (learn):** The benchmarks in DARPG's Comprehensive Guidelines for Handling Public Grievances, August 2024: 21 days for grievance redress and 30 days for appeals.
**Breakdown:**
- 21-day redress benchmark; reasons or interim communication needed where resolution takes longer.
- 30-day appeal benchmark.
- Administrative benchmark, not a judicial limitation period; guideline-based and revisable.
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** ✅ owner 07 and owner 08 (as-of: August 2024; re-verification dates in GOV-2058). ⚠️ Not verified by a fetched DARPG page (pgportal/darpg fetch not available). Cite as "DARPG's August 2024 guidelines".
**Links:** GOV-2041, GOV-2067 (disposal vs quality), GOV-2058.
**Trap:** Treating 21 days as a statutory deadline. It is a guideline benchmark, and CPGRAMS cannot award compensation.
**Mains hook:** Use the benchmark as a yardstick, then test quality through reasoned disposal, appeal outcome and repeat grievances.
**Back:** *"Twenty-one to resolve, thirty to appeal, zero to compel."*

### Flashcard GOV-2047 — Common Service Centres (CSC) and integrated service centres
**Front:** What role do Common Service Centres play in single-window access?
**Definition (learn):** Access points that aggregate multiple department services at one counter, supporting the single-window idea.
**Breakdown:**
- Lower citizen navigation cost.
- Operated by intermediaries (CSC operator, agent) with their own incentives.
- Can sit on an unchanged fragmented back end.
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** ⚠️ Owner 07 names CSCs and state single-window portals without figures; no count is asserted.
**Links:** GOV-2042, GOV-2068 (back-end).
**Trap:** Assuming CSC operators are the citizen's legal grievance route. The grievance route is the department, RTS authority or CPGRAMS.
**Mains hook:** Assess intermediary-based access against exclusion of the least digitally literate.
**Back:** *"CSC: a counter, not a court."*

### Flashcard GOV-2048 — Limitation 1: charter without enforceability
**Front:** Why does a Citizens' Charter fall short when it has no statutory backing?
**Definition (learn):** Most charters outside RTS-covered States remain voluntary administrative statements with no statutory penalty for default.
**Breakdown:**
- Promise without remedy: the standard exists but breach costs the official nothing.
- Design-stage cause, from the 1997 administrative introduction.
- Remedy: RTS-style statutory conversion for high-volume services.
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** ✅ owner 07 (mechanism 1; 2024 PYQ analysis).
**Links:** GOV-2039, GOV-2040, GOV-2053, GOV-2055 (2024 PYQ).
**Trap:** Thinking a charter's unmet promise is always a delivery failure. Under this limitation, the failure is designed in.
**Mains hook:** "Charters states a standard; only an enforceable Act creates an entitlement." Qualify by noting the capacity counterpoint.
**Back:** *"No penalty, no promise."*

### Flashcard GOV-2049 — Limitation 2: weak grievance follow-through
**Front:** How does weak grievance follow-through undermine a charter?
**Definition (learn):** The grievance pillar depends on CPGRAMS or departmental mechanisms escalating and resolving complaints, not merely logging them.
**Breakdown:**
- Logging and routing are not resolution.
- Disposal can be gamed by closing tickets without fixing the cause.
- Needs reasoned closure, appeal and feedback.
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** ✅ owner 07 (mechanism 2).
**Links:** GOV-2041, GOV-2067 (disposal vs quality), GOV-2061 (CPGRAMS monitoring).
**Trap:** Measuring success by disposal count.
**Mains hook:** Propose quality audit of grievances: substantive resolution, recurrence, appeal reversal.
**Back:** *"Logged is not solved."*

### Flashcard GOV-2050 — Limitation 3: capacity mismatch
**Front:** Why does a well-drafted charter still fail?
**Definition (learn):** The Service Delivery Capability pillar (staffing, process and digital tools) is not strengthened alongside the charter promise.
**Breakdown:**
- Charter documents a capacity the department lacks.
- Capability is a precondition, not an afterthought.
- Statutory penalties over an under-staffed office produce defensive behaviour (see GOV-2066).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** ✅ owner 07 (mechanism 3).
**Links:** GOV-2038 (pillar 3), GOV-2060, GOV-2065.
**Trap:** Assuming an RTS Act alone creates service. It converts a broken promise into a penalised officer.
**Mains hook:** "Enforceability and capability must move together."
**Back:** *"A promise needs people, not only penalties."*

### Flashcard GOV-2051 — Limitation 4: low citizen awareness
**Front:** How does low awareness reduce the charter's accountability value?
**Definition (learn):** Charters are often not actively publicised or explained at the point of service, so citizens cannot invoke the standard.
**Breakdown:**
- Display and vernacular publication at the service point.
- Unknown standards cannot be enforced by citizens.
- Cross-cutting limitation, not solely a design problem.
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** ✅ owner 07 (mechanism 4).
**Links:** GOV-2048, GOV-2052, GOV-2064.
**Trap:** Treating awareness campaigns as the main fix while skipping statutory backing.
**Mains hook:** Suggest display at the point of service, local-language publication and grievance-channel information.
**Back:** *"Unknown standards are unenforceable standards."*

### Flashcard GOV-2052 — Limitation 5: static, non-consultative drafting
**Front:** Why do charters drafted without consultation specify the wrong standards?
**Definition (learn):** Departments draft standards without user consultation, so the standards measure what is administratively convenient rather than what citizens value.
**Breakdown:**
- Standards like "application acknowledged in 2 days" versus "problem resolved".
- Remedy: consultative drafting with user panels; periodic revision.
- Repeated static drafting ignores changing service conditions.
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** ✅ owner 07 (mechanism 5).
**Links:** GOV-2054 (static trap), GOV-2060 (standard-setting stage), GOV-2064 (proliferation vs quality).
**Trap:** Assuming more charters means better charters (see GOV-2064).
**Mains hook:** "Measure what the citizen values; test the standard against the user's own priority services."
**Back:** *"Measure the citizen's need, not the clerk's convenience."*

### Flashcard GOV-2053 — Trap: a charter is legally enforceable by default
**Front:** Is a Citizens' Charter legally enforceable?
**Definition (learn):** No. Most charters are voluntary administrative commitments. Only RTS-covered services carry a statutory, penalty-backed enforceable timeline.
**Breakdown:**
- Charter: voluntary standard, no penalty by default.
- RTS Act: notified services, timeline, designated officer, penalty.
- CPGRAMS: administrative grievance system, not a statutory right.
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** ✅ owner 07 (trap).
**Links:** GOV-2039, GOV-2040, GOV-2048, GOV-2060.
**Trap:** This is the exact distinction the 2024 PYQ rewards ("charter exists" vs "charter is enforceable").
**Mains hook:** Open the answer by stating the enforceability gap before listing causes.
**Back:** *"Charter states, RTS binds."*

### Flashcard GOV-2054 — Trap: a Citizens' Charter is a static one-time document
**Front:** Should a Citizens' Charter be revised over time?
**Definition (learn):** Good practice is periodic, consultative revision as service conditions change.
**Breakdown:**
- Revision cycle missing in many charters.
- Revision follows citizen feedback, new digital channels and changing conditions.
- Implicit expectation of the 2024 PYQ.
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** ✅ owner 07 (trap); DARPG guidelines recommend periodic review (owner 07 advanced §4).
**Links:** GOV-2052, GOV-2064.
**Trap:** Thinking adoption is the end point. Under the static model, charters become unreliable over time.
**Mains hook:** Propose a mandatory revision cycle linked to grievance data.
**Back:** *"Revise or retire."*

### Flashcard GOV-2055 — 2024 GS-II Q16: Citizens' Charter's full potential
**Front:** What does the 2024 GS-II Q16 PYQ ask, and how should the answer be structured?
**Definition (learn):** PYQ (2024, GS-II Q16, 15 marks, identify and suggest): the Citizens' Charter is a landmark initiative but yet to reach its full potential; identify the hindering factors and measures to overcome them.
**Breakdown:**
- (a) Landmark significance: 1997 origin, Sevottam framing.
- (b) Hindering factors: the five limitations (GOV-2048 to GOV-2052).
- (c) Measures: RTS-style statutory backing, CPGRAMS-integrated escalation, capability investment, awareness campaigns, periodic consultative revision.
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** ✅ owner 07 (PYQ wording confirmed in owner, "word for word in locally held official papers" per owner 08 ledger). Routing conflict: owner 07 treats it as its own; owner 08 says the audited 2024-2025 ledger routes it to 08 (see GOV-2106 in 08).
**Links:** GOV-2048 to GOV-2052, GOV-2053, GOV-2059, GOV-2065.
**Trap:** Answering generically ("lack of awareness and corruption") without the enforceability layer.
**Mains hook:** Answer with the layer mismatch: strong charter layer, partial RTS layer, non-adjudicatory CPGRAMS, weak capability.
**Back:** *"Five causes, three fixes: statute, escalation, capacity."*

### Flashcard GOV-2056 — 2018 GS-II Q18: Citizens' Charter limitations and measures
**Front:** What does the 2018 GS-II Q18 PYQ demand?
**Definition (learn):** PYQ (2018, GS-II Q18, 15 marks, identify and suggest): limitations of Citizens' Charters and measures for effectiveness.
**Breakdown:**
- Same demand family as 2024 Q16; prepare as one.
- Limitations by Sevottam pillar: charter (non-consultative, vague standards, no named officer, no revision), grievance (logs but cannot compel), capability (not strengthened).
- Cross-cutting: low awareness; no enforceability outside RTS-notified services.
- Measures map one-to-one onto the limitations.
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** ✅ owner 07 (basic §13.0; routed by the core file, superseding the older advanced pointer; advanced 07 integration table confirms Q18).
**Links:** GOV-2048 to GOV-2052, GOV-2055, GOV-2060, GOV-2065.
**Trap:** Listing measures without naming the limitation each repairs.
**Mains hook:** Verdict: the promise fails less from indifference than from a missing enforcement limb.
**Back:** *"Every measure repairs a named defect."*

### Flashcard GOV-2057 — 2024 GS-II Q8: public perception of civil servants
**Front:** What does the 2024 GS-II Q8 PYQ (10 marks) ask?
**Definition (learn):** PYQ (2024, GS-II Q8, 10 marks, discuss): the doctrine of democratic governance and public perception of the integrity and commitment of civil servants.
**Breakdown:**
- Routed to 07 in the basic owner's §13.1 demand map (legitimacy, not efficiency).
- Answer the democratic-legitimacy half here; cross-link the service-design half to the Governance file 09 (not in this fragment).
- Public perception is produced at the counter, not in policy.
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** ✅ owner 07 (2024-2025 ledger). Owner says the answer is "authored route, not an official key".
**Links:** GOV-2037, GOV-2050, GOV-2058.
**Trap:** Answering with a communication-campaign remedy alone.
**Mains hook:** Service standards, reasoned decisions and working grievance redress do more for legitimacy than any communication campaign.
**Back:** *"Legitimacy is earned at the counter."*

### Flashcard GOV-2058 — Current-status controls: owner dates for CPGRAMS and RTS
**Front:** What is the current status of CPGRAMS and RTS coverage, and as of which date?
**Definition (learn):** The owner records checks made on different dates; the status is that CPGRAMS is administrative and non-adjudicatory, and RTS coverage remains State-specific.
**Breakdown:**
- Basic 07 §8: re-verified 13 August 2026.
- Basic 07 and basic 08 closing 'verified current fact' blocks: recheck dated 5 September 2026 (sources listed: DARPG Sevottam, pgportal, citizen-charter pages; not fetched here).
- Advanced 07 §9: status checked 21 July 2026.
- No later CPGRAMS revision located.
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** ⚠️ Owner-internal inconsistency in as-of dates (13 Aug, 5 Sept, 21 July 2026). Do not cite one as a permanent rule; cite "DARPG's August 2024 guidelines" as the source.
**Links:** GOV-2046, GOV-2045, GOV-2063.
**Trap:** Quoting a current-status claim from memory without an as-of date.
**Mains hook:** Use the August 2024 guidelines explicitly and state that RTS coverage is State-specific.
**Back:** *"Date the benchmark, scope the State."*

#### Advanced depth — Citizen-Centric Administration (owner 07 advanced)

### Flashcard GOV-2059 — Charter as promise vs RTS Act as entitlement
**Front:** What is the difference between a Citizens' Charter promise and an RTS Act entitlement?
**Definition (learn):** A charter is an administrative promise. An RTS Act creates enforceability only for notified services and according to that State's designated-officer, appeal, compensation/penalty and exception provisions.
**Breakdown:**
- Charter: standard + timeline + remedy statement; no legal penalty by default.
- RTS Act: notified service + timeline + designated officer + appeal + penalty (and, in some Acts, compensation).
- State laws are not institutionally identical; do not universalise one State.
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** ✅ owner 07 advanced §2; ✅ owner 07 basic §2 (definition of RTS laws).
**Links:** GOV-2039, GOV-2040, GOV-2060 (five-element test), GOV-2066 (penalty vs compensation), GOV-2053 (trap).
**Trap:** Saying an RTS Act makes every charter enforceable. Only the notified services are covered.
**Mains hook:** "A charter states a standard; only an enforceable Act creates an entitlement." Qualify by flagging State variation.
**Back:** *"Promise is administrative; entitlement is statutory."*

### Flashcard GOV-2060 — The five-element RTS test for service-guarantee questions
**Front:** What five tests should you apply to any service-guarantee or RTS question?
**Definition (learn):** A checklist for whether a service guarantee is real: (1) notified service? (2) designated officer named? (3) timeline specified and running from a defined trigger? (4) first and second appeal with its own timeline? (5) what follows default: penalty, compensation, deemed approval, or nothing?
**Breakdown:**
- Element 1: an unlisted service has no statutory timeline.
- Element 2: without a named addressee there is no accountability.
- Element 3: trigger matters (date of application or complete application).
- Element 4: appeal structure gives the remedy.
- Element 5: penalty on the officer and compensation to the citizen are different remedies.
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** ✅ owner 07 advanced §13.5 (owner-authored test; no external statute quoted).
**Links:** GOV-2040, GOV-2059, GOV-2066, GOV-2063.
**Trap:** A "service" with no default consequence is a charter, whatever it is called.
**Mains hook:** Run the five elements on the scheme in the question, then ask what happens on default.
**Back:** *"Notified, Named, Timed, Appealable, Penalised — the five-element test."*

### Flashcard GOV-2061 — CPGRAMS: monitoring and escalation, not adjudication
**Front:** Why can CPGRAMS track a grievance but not guarantee its resolution?
**Definition (learn):** CPGRAMS logs, routes, tracks and enables escalation of grievances, but is not a quasi-judicial adjudicatory body. Its efficacy depends on the receiving department's responsiveness.
**Breakdown:**
- Strength: visibility, routing, feedback and appeal architecture.
- Limit: cannot compel a service, award compensation or decide the merits.
- Failure: grievance transferred between departments without resolution (owner example).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** ✅ owner 07 advanced §2, ✅ owner 08 §13.4; DARPG 2024 guidelines say it "does not turn CPGRAMS into a tribunal" (owner 07 advanced §4).
**Links:** GOV-2041, GOV-2046, GOV-2049, GOV-2067, GOV-2093 (not the only channel), GOV-2118 (individual vs systemic).
**Trap:** CPGRAMS resolves grievances by itself. It does not; the receiving department does, and CPGRAMS only makes non-response visible.
**Mains hook:** "CPGRAMS improves grievance visibility but cannot by itself guarantee resolution." Explain with the compulsion gap.
**Back:** *"CPGRAMS watches; the department must act."*

### Flashcard GOV-2062 — Trap: Sevottam and the Citizens' Charter are the same thing
**Front:** Is Sevottam just another name for the Citizens' Charter?
**Definition (learn):** No. The Citizens' Charter is one of Sevottam's three components; Sevottam also includes public grievance redress and service delivery capability.
**Breakdown:**
- Charter = promise.
- Grievance redress = remedy.
- Capability = ability to keep the promise.
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** ✅ owner 07 advanced §8 (trap).
**Links:** GOV-2038 (Sevottam), GOV-2039 (charter), GOV-2050 (capability).
**Trap:** Answering a Sevottam question with only charter content loses two of three pillars.
**Mains hook:** Name all three pillars before analysing one.
**Back:** *"Charter is one leg of the Sevottam stool."*

### Flashcard GOV-2063 — Trap: RTS Acts cover all public services uniformly
**Front:** Do RTS Acts give the same coverage and remedies across India?
**Definition (learn):** No. RTS coverage is State-specific and limited to a notified list of services per State. Appeal, penalty and compensation design differ.
**Breakdown:**
- Coverage: only notified services.
- Design: designated officer, appeal, penalty or compensation vary.
- Count of States with RTS Acts is not asserted here (owner says do not assert it).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** ✅ owner 07 advanced §8 and basic §13.10 (do not universalise one State). ⚠️ No fetched State-level source.
**Links:** GOV-2040, GOV-2045, GOV-2060, GOV-2058.
**Trap:** Quoting one State's compensation figure or appeal structure as the national rule.
**Mains hook:** Use a federal-design point: a citizen experiences different accountability regimes depending on State or Union service ownership.
**Back:** *"Thirty States, thirty designs: check the State Act."*

### Flashcard GOV-2064 — Charter proliferation vs charter quality
**Front:** Why is charter quality, not adoption, now the main problem?
**Definition (learn):** Many departments have adopted charters (a proliferation success), but measurable standards, consultative drafting and periodic revision vary widely.
**Breakdown:**
- Adoption is widespread; quality is uneven.
- Quality markers: measurable standard, named officer, consultation, revision cycle.
- Proliferation without quality control is a separate failure from non-adoption.
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** ⚠️ Owner analytical inference; no count of charters asserted (owner: do not assert the count of departments with charters).
**Links:** GOV-2052, GOV-2054, GOV-2060.
**Trap:** Citing adoption numbers as evidence of effectiveness.
**Mains hook:** "India's charter problem is no longer adoption; it is quality." Propose standards with outcome-expressed metrics and designated officers.
**Back:** *"More charters, same wait: adoption is not quality."*

### Flashcard GOV-2065 — Standard-to-remedy causal chain
**Front:** Trace a citizen service from standard-setting to consequence, and say where India's chain breaks.
**Definition (learn):** The chain runs: standard set (consultative, measurable, outcome-expressed, officer named) -> publicised -> service attempted -> breach occurs (auto-detectable or not) -> grievance route -> outcome -> consequence (penalty, compensation, none) which feeds back into standard-setting.
**Breakdown:**
- Design failures occur at standard-setting and publicising.
- Implementation failures occur at service attempt and breach detection.
- Remedy failures occur at grievance route and consequence.
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** ✅ owner 07 advanced §13.6 (owner-authored chain).
**Links:** GOV-2052, GOV-2048, GOV-2050, GOV-2066, GOV-2067.
**Trap:** Diagnosing every breakdown as implementation failure; some are design failures.
**Mains hook:** "Most services never reach 'breach auto-detectable'; enforcement depends on the citizen's own initiative." Recommend machine-checkable timelines in grievance portals.
**Back:** *"Standard, Publish, Attempt, Breach, Route, Outcome, Consequence."*

### Flashcard GOV-2066 — Penalty on the officer vs compensation to the citizen
**Front:** Are a penalty on an official and compensation to a citizen the same remedy?
**Definition (learn):** No. A penalty targets the defaulting official; compensation goes to the citizen. Many State Acts provide the first without the second.
**Breakdown:**
- Penalty: sanction on the officer for delay.
- Compensation: remedy to the affected citizen.
- Deemed approval is a third type of consequence (owner lists it).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** ✅ owner 07 advanced §13.5 point 5 (owner-authored). ⚠️ No specific penalty amount asserted.
**Links:** GOV-2040, GOV-2060, GOV-2065.
**Trap:** Assuming an RTS penalty compensates the citizen.
**Mains hook:** "Saying so is a precision mark." Distinguish sanction (deterrence) from remedy (relief).
**Back:** *"Officer pays a fine; citizen needs a remedy."*

### Flashcard GOV-2067 — Grievance quality vs disposal rate
**Front:** How should grievance redress quality be measured?
**Definition (learn):** Disposal rate counts closures; quality tests whether the problem was solved: substantive resolution, reasoned closure, recurrence, appeal outcome, citizen feedback.
**Breakdown:**
- Disposal: count of tickets closed.
- Quality: resolution, reasoned closure, recurrence, appeal outcome, feedback.
- Any measured target invites optimisation of the measure (Goodhart's problem; see `15`).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** ⚠️ Owner analytical framing. No CPGRAMS disposal rate or pendency figure is asserted (owner: do not assert).
**Links:** GOV-2046, GOV-2049, GOV-2065, GOV-2118 (individual vs systemic).
**Trap:** Presenting a high disposal rate as proof of grievance quality.
**Mains hook:** Recommend independent audit of grievance quality rather than disposal alone.
**Back:** *"Closed is not cured."*

### Flashcard GOV-2068 — Single-window front counter vs back-end integration
**Front:** Can a single-window counter simplify the process on its own?
**Definition (learn):** No. A unified front counter can present one interface while the approval chain behind it remains fragmented.
**Breakdown:**
- Front: one interface, lower navigation cost.
- Back: separate approvals, separate officers, separate timelines unless integrated.
- Analogous to the e-governance back-end bias noted in `05`.
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** ✅ owner 07 advanced §2 (⚠️ analytical), cross-link `05` (not read here).
**Links:** GOV-2042, GOV-2047, GOV-2065.
**Trap:** Counting a single-window counter as evidence of process reform.
**Mains hook:** Ask whether the approval chain was redesigned, not just the counter.
**Back:** *"Front door unified, back rooms still separate."*

### Flashcard GOV-2069 — Layer mismatch: the analytical diagnosis of the charter's unmet potential
**Front:** How does the layer-mismatch diagnosis explain the 2024 PYQ's "yet to reach its full potential"?
**Definition (learn):** India built the voluntary charter layer and the CPGRAMS escalation layer relatively well, but statutory RTS conversion is uneven and the capability layer is often the weakest link.
**Breakdown:**
- Layer 1: voluntary charter (widespread).
- Layer 2: statutory RTS (State-specific).
- Layer 3: CPGRAMS escalation (functioning, non-adjudicatory).
- Layer 4: service-delivery capability (often under-invested).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** ⚠️ Owner analytical claim (advanced §1 and §11). Not a verified empirical finding.
**Links:** GOV-2048 to GOV-2052, GOV-2055, GOV-2059, GOV-2061.
**Trap:** Thinking charter-language reform alone solves the problem; it misses the weakest layer.
**Mains hook:** "Durable reform must close the weakest layer, not simply rewrite charter language." Use this thesis as the answer's spine.
**Back:** *"Charter strong, RTS partial, CPGRAMS escalates, capacity weakest."*

### GROUP — Transparency, Accountability, Grievance Redress and Social Audit

### Flashcard GOV-2070 — Transparency, answerability and enforceability: the accountability chain
**Front:** What three things must an accountability system deliver, and how do they differ?
**Definition (learn):** Transparency makes records and decisions visible; answerability requires an identifiable authority to explain; enforceability requires a correction, remedy or consequence.
**Breakdown:**
- Transparency: visibility of records, spending and decisions (RTI, proactive disclosure, dashboards).
- Answerability: a named official explains in public or on record.
- Enforceability: correction, recovery, penalty or consequence follows.
- RTI, social audit, grievance redress, vigilance, Lokpal and CAG are complementary, not interchangeable.
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** ✅ owner 08 (basic §1 and Master Framework cross-reference). Owner states India built the first extensively, the second unevenly, the third rarely.
**Links:** GOV-2092 (trap), GOV-2101 (typology), GOV-2102 (causal chain), GOV-2099 (non-interchangeable functions), GOV-2111 (answerability without enforceability).
**Trap:** Equating visibility with accountability. Transparency can reveal failure without fixing it.
**Mains hook:** "Accountability is not transparency plus good intentions." Add a remedy-routing test to every answer.
**Back:** *"See, Answer, Enforce — all three or it is only disclosure."*

### Flashcard GOV-2071 — RTI Act, 2005: the request route
**Front:** What does the RTI request route give a citizen, and what can it not give?
**Definition (learn):** A statutory right to seek existing information held by a public authority, through a Public Information Officer (PIO), with a first appeal and a second appeal to the CIC/SIC.
**Breakdown:**
- Request to PIO; response period (GOV-2072).
- First appeal to appellate authority; second appeal to CIC/SIC (GOV-2073).
- Complaints to CIC/SIC on information access.
- PIO penalty for default (GOV-2074).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** ✅ owner 08 (RTI Act, 2005). ✅ Wikipedia (RTI Act 2005 article) for the 30-day and 48-hour periods.
**Links:** GOV-2072, GOV-2073, GOV-2074, GOV-2075, GOV-2080.
**Trap:** RTI gives information, not service relief or punishment. It cannot create information or order a service.
**Mains hook:** Use RTI as the first step in visibility, not the whole of accountability.
**Back:** *"RTI opens the file; it does not decide the case."*

### Flashcard GOV-2072 — RTI response periods: 30 days and 48 hours
**Front:** What are the RTI response periods for ordinary and life/liberty requests?
**Definition (learn):** A public authority must reply within 30 days, or within 48 hours where the matter involves the life and liberty of the petitioner.
**Breakdown:**
- Ordinary request: 30 days.
- Life or liberty: 48 hours.
- Delay beyond the period engages appeal and penalty routes.
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** ✅ Wikipedia (RTI Act 2005 article): "thirty days, or within 48 hours in case of the matter involving a petitioner's life and liberty". ✅ owner 08 (same figures).
**Links:** GOV-2071, GOV-2074.
**Trap:** Confusing the 48-hour life-or-liberty route with the ordinary 30-day route.
**Mains hook:** Note that the 48-hour route exists for urgent matters, showing a graded response design.
**Back:** *"Thirty for ordinary, forty-eight hours for life and liberty."*

### Flashcard GOV-2073 — RTI appeal timelines: first appeal and second appeal to CIC/SIC
**Front:** What are the RTI appeal time limits?
**Definition (learn):** The first appeal lies to the appellate authority, ordinarily within 30 days of the response or the lapse of the period; the second appeal lies to the CIC/SIC within 90 days.
**Breakdown:**
- First appeal: appellate authority (generally within 30 days, per owner).
- Second appeal: CIC or SIC, within 90 days (per owner).
- CIC/SIC also hear specified complaints.
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** ✅ owner 08 (basic §2, §13.4 "30-day first appeal", "90-day second appeal"). ⚠️ The Act text (dopt PDF) could not be fetched (404), and Wikipedia gave no deadline for either appeal. Verify against Section 19 before citing.
**Links:** GOV-2071, GOV-2080, GOV-2074.
**Trap:** Stating a first-appeal deadline without checking the Act text; the owner's figure is unverified here.
**Mains hook:** Use the appeal ladder as an example of graded remedies within the information regime.
**Back:** *"Thirty to the first appeal, ninety to the Commission (verify)."*

### Flashcard GOV-2074 — PIO penalty: ₹250 per day up to ₹25,000
**Front:** What penalty can a PIO face for delay or denial under the RTI Act?
**Definition (learn):** A daily penalty on the PIO for failure to furnish information within time, capped at a statutory maximum after the statutory process.
**Breakdown:**
- Rate: ₹250 per day.
- Maximum: ₹25,000.
- Imposed after the statutory process, not automatically.
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** ✅ owner 08 (figure stated in §6 and §13.10). ⚠️ The Act (s.20) text was not fetched (dopt PDF 404; Wikipedia gave no penalty figure). Verify before citing.
**Links:** GOV-2071, GOV-2073, GOV-2080.
**Trap:** Describing the penalty as payable to the applicant. It is a penalty on the official; compensation is a separate route.
**Mains hook:** Use the PIO penalty to show that accountability depends on personal sanctions, which RTI alone does not guarantee.
**Back:** *"Two-fifty a day, twenty-five thousand cap."*

### Flashcard GOV-2075 — RTI section 4: proactive (suo motu) disclosure
**Front:** What must public authorities publish without waiting for an RTI application?
**Definition (learn):** Section 4 duties require public authorities to maintain records and publish specified organisational, decision, budget, subsidy, concession and PIO information suo motu.
**Breakdown:**
- Organisation, functions and duties.
- Decisions and their rationale.
- Budgets, subsidies, concessions and beneficiary information.
- PIO details.
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** ✅ owner 08 (section 4 proactive disclosure). Wikipedia confirms that authorities must "proactively publish certain categories of information" (no section number given there).
**Links:** GOV-2070, GOV-2071, GOV-2100 (routing).
**Trap:** Treating proactive disclosure as the same as RTI request-driven disclosure.
**Mains hook:** Proactive disclosure cuts grievance volume upstream, but compliance is not independently audited.
**Back:** *"Publish first, so citizens need not ask."*

### Flashcard GOV-2076 — RTI exemptions (s.8) and the public-interest override (s.8(2))
**Front:** What are the RTI exemptions, and what overrides them?
**Definition (learn):** Section 8 contains exemptions, subject to severability; section 8(2) is the general public-interest override by which disclosure can still be directed.
**Breakdown:**
- Exemptions are listed in s.8(1); partial disclosure by severability.
- s.8(2) public-interest override.
- Section 24 is a separate exclusion (GOV-2078).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** ✅ owner 08 (basic §2 and advanced §13.8). The exemption list was not fetched; numbering beyond s.8(1)(j), s.8(2) and s.24 is not asserted.
**Links:** GOV-2077 (s.8(1)(j) change), GOV-2078 (s.24), GOV-2075.
**Trap:** Assuming all exempted information is absolutely secret. The override and severability apply.
**Mains hook:** Present transparency vs privacy/security as a balance, not a binary.
**Back:** *"Exempt, sever, override under 8(2)."*

### Flashcard GOV-2077 — RTI s.8(1)(j) substituted by DPDP Act s.44(3), from 13 November 2025
**Front:** What changed in RTI s.8(1)(j), and when?
**Definition (learn):** The owner records that the Digital Personal Data Protection (DPDP) Act, 2023, s.44(3), substituted RTI s.8(1)(j) in the first, already-commenced tranche (G.S.R. 843(E), 13 November 2025). The substituted clause is a personal-information exemption.
**Breakdown:**
- Earlier three-part test (relationship to public activity, unwarranted invasion, larger public interest) is not the current text.
- s.8(2) public-interest override is unaffected.
- A constitutional challenge is pending; no outcome located as of 13 August 2026.
- DPDP s.44(2) amends the IT Act, 2000, commencing 13 May 2027 (not the RTI clause).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** ⚠️ Owner 08 (basic §8, §13.10). ⚠️ Wikipedia's RTI article does not mention DPDP or any date; the owner's claim was not confirmed by a fetched source. Owner's own correction record: an earlier version stated the opposite and was corrected.
**Links:** GOV-2076, GOV-2104 (status), GOV-2105 (2020 PYQ on RTI amendments), GOV-2106.
**Trap:** Quoting the pre-substitution three-part test as current law, or claiming the challenge has been decided.
**Mains hook:** "The RTI amendment narrowed the personal-information gateway while leaving the s.8(2) override intact." Present both sides of transparency vs privacy.
**Back:** *"2025 swapped 8(1)(j) for privacy; 8(2) still overrides."*

### Flashcard GOV-2078 — RTI section 24: exclusion of intelligence and security organisations
**Front:** Which organisations does RTI s.24 exclude, and what provisos apply?
**Definition (learn):** Section 24 exempts intelligence and security organisations listed in the Schedule, but not where the information concerns allegations of corruption or human-rights violations.
**Breakdown:**
- Central bodies listed in the Second Schedule (Wikipedia).
- Exclusion does not cover corruption or human-rights information.
- State bodies are exempt too (Wikipedia: "Central and state intelligence and security bodies are exempt").
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** ✅ Wikipedia (RTI Act 2005 article): "Central and state intelligence and security bodies are exempt, except where corruption or human rights violations are involved. The central bodies are listed in the Second Schedule." ✅ owner 08 (s.24).
**Links:** GOV-2076, GOV-2080.
**Trap:** Assuming s.24 excludes every intelligence-linked record. The corruption and human-rights provisos preserve access.
**Mains hook:** The statutory compromise between disclosure and security is the proviso design.
**Back:** *"Intelligence is shielded, but corruption and rights abuse are not."*

### Flashcard GOV-2079 — RTI (Amendment) Act, 2019: service conditions of Information Commissioners
**Front:** What did the 2019 RTI amendment change in the Information Commissions?
**Definition (learn):** The Right to Information (Amendment) Act, 2019, passed on 25 July 2019, changed the service terms and conditions of the CIC and Information Commissioners, amending sections 13, 16 and 27.
**Breakdown:**
- Passed 25 July 2019.
- Amended ss.13, 16 and 27 (service terms of CIC and Commissioners).
- Relevant to the autonomy question (GOV-2105, 2020 PYQ).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** ✅ Wikipedia (RTI Act 2005 article). Owner 08 does not state this 2019 Act; it is an added fetched cross-reference. Composition detail belongs to Polity (`Polity/basic/CIC-and-SIC.md`).
**Links:** GOV-2080, GOV-2105, GOV-2104.
**Trap:** Confusing the 2019 amendment (service terms) with the 2025 DPDP substitution of s.8(1)(j).
**Mains hook:** Tenure and salary set by the executive raise an autonomy question, regardless of the holder's integrity.
**Back:** *"2019 changed the Commissioners' terms; 2025 changed the privacy exemption."*

### Flashcard GOV-2080 — CIC and SIC: RTI second-appeal and complaint bodies
**Front:** What are the CIC and SIC, and what can they decide?
**Definition (learn):** The Central and State Information Commissions are statutory bodies under the RTI Act that decide second appeals and complaints about information access.
**Breakdown:**
- Statutory, not constitutional.
- Second appeal from the first appellate authority.
- Can secure information, compensation and RTI compliance/penalty.
- Cannot adjudicate the merits of the underlying service, pension or licence.
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** ✅ owner 08 (basic §4 and §13.4). Composition, appointment and tenure are Polity-owned (not restated here).
**Links:** GOV-2073, GOV-2074, GOV-2081, GOV-2079.
**Trap:** Treating the CIC as a general grievance authority (see GOV-2081).
**Mains hook:** Route an information denial to the Commission, and a service denial to the department or RTS authority.
**Back:** *"Commission decides the file, not the pension."*

### Flashcard GOV-2081 — Trap: the CIC can order the grant of a service
**Front:** Can the CIC order a pension, licence or service to be granted?
**Definition (learn):** No. CIC/SIC jurisdiction concerns information access and RTI compliance, not merits adjudication of the underlying entitlement.
**Breakdown:**
- Can order disclosure of information.
- Can impose penalty and direct compensation under RTI.
- Cannot decide the entitlement (pension, licence, service).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** ✅ owner 08 (trap; §13.4 "cannot adjudicate the merits").
**Links:** GOV-2080, GOV-2060 (service route), GOV-2100 (routing).
**Trap:** This is a high-frequency exam trap.
**Mains hook:** The routing error produces a disposal recorded as redress (T3 thesis).
**Back:** *"Records yes, service no."*

### Flashcard GOV-2082 — Grievance redress: individual complaint route
**Front:** What is grievance redress, and how does it differ from RTI and social audit?
**Definition (learn):** The institutional process by which an individual's complaint about a public service or entitlement is registered, tracked, escalated and ideally resolved, operationalised through CPGRAMS and sector-specific mechanisms.
**Breakdown:**
- Individual, complaint-driven.
- Tracked and escalated up the hierarchy.
- Distinct from collective social audit and demand-driven RTI.
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** ✅ owner 08 (definition). CPGRAMS: GOV-2041.
**Links:** GOV-2041, GOV-2093, GOV-2118, GOV-2100.
**Trap:** Treating grievance disposal as proof of resolution (see GOV-2067).
**Mains hook:** Name all three instruments (social audit, RTI, grievance) and state which one the question tests.
**Back:** *"One citizen, one complaint, one escalation ladder."*

### Flashcard GOV-2083 — Social audit: participatory verification
**Front:** What is social audit, and how does it differ from financial, compliance and performance audit?
**Definition (learn):** A participatory verification of records, expenditure and delivery, facilitated independently of implementation and culminating in a public hearing and follow-up.
**Breakdown:**
- Community verification of muster rolls, expenditure and work done.
- Public hearing (jan sunwai) with official responses.
- Distinct from CAG financial/performance audit and compliance audit.
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** ✅ owner 08 (definition; §13.4). MGNREGA social audit is in GOV-2084.
**Links:** GOV-2084, GOV-2085, GOV-2086, GOV-2087, GOV-2091 (trap), GOV-2113 (advanced trap).
**Trap:** Calling social audit a voluntary civil-society activity (GOV-2090).
**Mains hook:** Social audit converts records into public answerability.
**Back:** *"Community checks the records; officials answer in public."*

### Flashcard GOV-2084 — MGNREGA Section 17: statutory social audit
**Front:** Which section of the MGNREGA mandates social audit, and which body conducts it?
**Definition (learn):** MGNREGA, 2005, Section 17 makes social audit of all works under the scheme a statutory requirement, with the Gram Sabha as the designated social-audit body.
**Breakdown:**
- Statutory mandate under s.17.
- Gram Sabha meetings held to conduct social audit serve as "Social Audit Forums".
- Final stage: mandatory social audit in the Gram Sabha or Social Audit Forum.
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** ✅ owner 08 (s.17). ✅ Wikipedia (MGNREGA article): "statutory mandate of Section 17 (As outlined in Chapter 11 of the NREGA Operational Guidelines)" and Gram Sabha as "Social Audit Forums". Wikipedia also states that only seven States had the capacity to run audits to prescribed norms (undated; not asserted here).
**Links:** GOV-2085, GOV-2086, GOV-2089, GOV-2109 (complementarity), GOV-2083.
**Trap:** Answering that MGNREGA has no independent auditor (see GOV-2109).
**Mains hook:** Use s.17 as the strongest example of statutorily mandated social audit.
**Back:** *"Section 17: the Gram Sabha audits the works."*

### Flashcard GOV-2085 — Audit of Schemes Rules, 2011: independent SAU and six-monthly audit
**Front:** What do the MGNREGA Audit of Schemes Rules, 2011 require?
**Definition (learn):** Rules that require an independent State Social Audit Unit (SAU) to facilitate audit in every Gram Panchayat at least once every six months.
**Breakdown:**
- Independent State Social Audit Unit.
- Audit in every Gram Panchayat, at least six-monthly.
- Facilitation, resource-person training, record access, public hearing and reporting.
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** ✅ owner 08 (basic §4 and §13.4). ⚠️ The 2011 Rules text was not fetched; Wikipedia's MGNREGA article does not confirm the rule name, SAU independence or six-month frequency. Verify before citing as a legal rule.
**Links:** GOV-2084, GOV-2086, GOV-2110.
**Trap:** Asserting that Section 17 alone names an independent SAU (see GOV-2109).
**Mains hook:** The independence of the SAU is the single design variable determining audit quality (owner's claim).
**Back:** *"Eleven-eleven, six-monthly, SAU independent (verify)."*

### Flashcard GOV-2086 — State Social Audit Unit (SAU): independence as the design variable
**Front:** Why is the State Social Audit Unit's independence from the implementing department critical?
**Definition (learn):** The SAU facilitates audit and is meant to be separate from the department implementing the scheme. The owner treats this separation as the design variable that most determines audit quality.
**Breakdown:**
- Facilitates audit, training, record access, hearing and reporting.
- Independence reduces capture by the implementing hierarchy.
- Capacity and independence vary by State (owner: no uniform rating asserted).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** ✅ owner 08 (basic §9, §13.9). ⚠️ No State-level independence data asserted.
**Links:** GOV-2085, GOV-2114 (capture), GOV-2116 (trade-off).
**Trap:** Assuming all SAUs are equally independent.
**Mains hook:** Recommend a dedicated, independent SAU with a mandated follow-up timeline.
**Back:** *"Independent unit, honest audit."*

### Flashcard GOV-2087 — Public hearing (jan sunwai): answerability in public
**Front:** What does a public hearing (jan sunwai) add to social audit?
**Definition (learn):** A public forum where audit findings are presented and officials must respond in front of the affected community, creating face-to-face answerability distinct from a written RTI reply.
**Breakdown:**
- Findings presented publicly.
- Officials respond on the record.
- Attendance itself is a measurable indicator.
- Answerability is not automatic enforceability (GOV-2111).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** ✅ owner 08 (basic §3, §13.7). ✅ Wikipedia (MGNREGA article) describes a "Janasunani" public hearing at Panchayat and Mandal levels after RTI application and field verification.
**Links:** GOV-2083, GOV-2111, GOV-2112.
**Trap:** Treating a public hearing as proof of enforceable accountability (see GOV-2112).
**Mains hook:** "Officials must answer in public, to the affected people, on the record."
**Back:** *"Public hearing: the answer is on the record."*

### Flashcard GOV-2088 — Meghalaya Community Participation and Public Services Social Audit Act, 2017
**Front:** What does the Meghalaya Act, 2017 provide, and why is it significant?
**Definition (learn):** A State statute giving statutory backing to social audits of public programmes and services more broadly than MGNREGA, establishing Social Audit Units, independent social auditors, community participation and mandated follow-up on findings.
**Breakdown:**
- Generalised beyond one scheme.
- Social Audit Units and independent auditors.
- Community participation (owner advanced: including access to records).
- Mandated consideration/follow-up of recommendations.
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** ✅ owner 08 (basic §2, §4; advanced §4). ⚠️ Not verified by a fetched source. Owner: one of very few State laws of its kind; do not present as a national model in force.
**Links:** GOV-2084, GOV-2089, GOV-2110.
**Trap:** Describing the Meghalaya Act as a national model in force. It is State-level.
**Mains hook:** Use the Act as the strongest example of generalised social-audit legislation; recommend statutory backing where social audit is ad hoc.
**Back:** *"Meghalaya 2017: social audit beyond one scheme (verify text)."*

### Flashcard GOV-2089 — MGNREGA Section 17 vs Meghalaya Act 2017: scheme-specific vs generalised
**Front:** How do MGNREGA's social audit and the Meghalaya Act differ?
**Definition (learn):** MGNREGA s.17 is a scheme-specific statutory mandate (Gram Sabha plus SAU). The Meghalaya Act is a generalised State framework covering public programmes and services.
**Breakdown:**
- Scope: one scheme vs multiple public services.
- Auditor: Gram Sabha plus independent SAU vs dedicated Social Audit Units and independent auditors.
- Both statutory; both need follow-up to be enforceable.
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** ✅ owner 08 (basic §10, advanced §2, §4).
**Links:** GOV-2084, GOV-2085, GOV-2088, GOV-2110.
**Trap:** Presenting the two as the same model; the Meghalaya Act is broader in scope.
**Mains hook:** "Use MGNREGA as the strongest statutory example and the Meghalaya Act as the strongest generalised example."
**Back:** *"One scheme vs many services: both statutory."*

### Flashcard GOV-2090 — Trap: social audit is a purely voluntary civil-society practice
**Front:** Is social audit a voluntary civil-society initiative with no legal basis?
**Definition (learn):** No. Under MGNREGA s.17 and the Meghalaya Act, 2017, social audit is a statutory obligation for the implementing agency.
**Breakdown:**
- Statutory: MGNREGA s.17, the 2011 Rules, the Meghalaya Act.
- Voluntary civil-society audits are a separate, weaker practice.
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** ✅ owner 08 (trap, §7).
**Links:** GOV-2084, GOV-2088, GOV-2110.
**Trap:** Conflating a statutory duty with a community initiative.
**Mains hook:** Statutory backing converts audit into obligation.
**Back:** *"Statute makes it a duty, not a favour."*

### Flashcard GOV-2091 — Trap: social audit and RTI are the same mechanism
**Front:** Are social audit and RTI the same accountability instrument?
**Definition (learn):** No. Social audit is participatory, collective and scheme-specific verification; RTI is an individual, demand-driven information request.
**Breakdown:**
- Social audit: collective, verifies records against work done, public hearing.
- RTI: individual, request-driven, gives records.
- RTI can feed a social audit (owner's Mains flow), but they are not interchangeable.
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** ✅ owner 08 (trap, §10).
**Links:** GOV-2071, GOV-2083, GOV-2082.
**Trap:** Using RTI replies as proof of community verification.
**Mains hook:** Name all three instruments: social audit, RTI and grievance redress.
**Back:** *"RTI asks; social audit checks; grievance chases."*

### Flashcard GOV-2092 — Trap: transparency automatically produces accountability
**Front:** Does publishing information automatically create accountability?
**Definition (learn):** No. Accountability also needs an identifiable authority to answer and an enforceable remedy. Transparency alone can reveal failure without fixing it.
**Breakdown:**
- Visibility is the first limb.
- Answerability and enforceability are additional.
- Dashboards that show failure without naming who must correct it produce "informed helplessness" (owner's Master Framework trap).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** ✅ owner 08 (trap; §13.8).
**Links:** GOV-2070, GOV-2075, GOV-2101, GOV-2102.
**Trap:** Presenting a dashboard as accountability.
**Mains hook:** "Transparency has been India's most successful accountability reform and its most over-claimed one."
**Back:** *"Visible is not answerable."*

### Flashcard GOV-2093 — CPGRAMS is not the only grievance-redress channel
**Front:** Is CPGRAMS the only grievance channel in India?
**Definition (learn):** No. Sector-specific mechanisms (banking ombudsman, consumer forums, scheme-specific helplines) operate alongside CPGRAMS, which is the principal Union cross-department portal.
**Breakdown:**
- CPGRAMS: Union, cross-department, administrative.
- Sector bodies: banking ombudsman, consumer forums, scheme helplines.
- Regulator and appellate tribunal routes exist for regulated sectors (see `11`).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** ✅ owner 08 (basic §7 trap). Owner gives no figures for these bodies.
**Links:** GOV-2041, GOV-2082, GOV-2100.
**Trap:** Routing a regulated-sector dispute to CPGRAMS, which cannot review a regulatory order.
**Mains hook:** Route each complaint to the forum with the power to grant the remedy.
**Back:** *"CPGRAMS is the main door, not the only one."*

### Flashcard GOV-2094 — Trap: Lokpal and CPGRAMS are interchangeable
**Front:** Can a corruption complaint be routed through CPGRAMS, and does Lokpal handle ordinary grievances?
**Definition (learn):** No. Lokpal handles corruption complaints within statutory jurisdiction; CPGRAMS routes administrative grievances.
**Breakdown:**
- Lokpal: corruption, covered public servants.
- CPGRAMS: administrative grievance routing and tracking.
- Corruption can also engage vigilance and investigative agencies.
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** ✅ owner 08 (basic §7 trap; §13.4 Lokpal).
**Links:** GOV-2096, GOV-2095, GOV-2100.
**Trap:** Treating Lokpal as a general grievance portal.
**Mains hook:** Corruption requires vigilance, Lokpal or investigation, not an administrative grievance.
**Back:** *"Corruption goes to Lokpal; service delay goes to CPGRAMS."*

### Flashcard GOV-2095 — Central Vigilance Commission (CVC) and the CVC Act, 2003
**Front:** What is the CVC's role in accountability?
**Definition (learn):** A statutory central body providing superintendence and advice on vigilance in central organisations under the CVC Act, 2003.
**Breakdown:**
- Superintendence and advice on vigilance matters.
- Advice, not conviction; not a criminal court.
- Vigilance route for corruption in central organisations.
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** ✅ owner 08 (basic §4; advanced §13.4 "CVC Act, 2003").
**Links:** GOV-2094, GOV-2100, GOV-2107.
**Trap:** Treating CVC advice as a judicial finding of guilt.
**Mains hook:** Vigilance is an enforcement route, but it is administrative, not judicial.
**Back:** *"CVC advises; courts convict."*

### Flashcard GOV-2096 — Lokpal and Lokayuktas Act, 2013
**Front:** What jurisdiction does the Lokpal and Lokayuktas Act, 2013 give?
**Definition (learn):** A statute providing inquiry into corruption complaints against covered public servants, with Lokpal at the Centre and Lokayuktas in the States (owner names the Act).
**Breakdown:**
- Corruption complaints against covered public servants.
- Not a general grievance portal.
- Not a service-delivery forum.
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** ✅ owner 08 (basic §13.4). Composition and jurisdiction detail is not restated here; the owner says Lokpal case statistics must not be asserted.
**Links:** GOV-2094, GOV-2095.
**Trap:** Assuming Lokpal handles all complaints about service delays.
**Mains hook:** Use Lokpal as the corruption route in the routing table.
**Back:** *"Lokpal chases corruption, not delays."*

### Flashcard GOV-2097 — Comptroller and Auditor General (CAG): Articles 148–151
**Front:** What does CAG audit provide, and what does it not do?
**Definition (learn):** The CAG is a constitutional authority (Arts 148–151) providing financial, compliance and performance audit that feeds legislative scrutiny through PAC/COPU.
**Breakdown:**
- Financial, compliance and performance audit.
- Reports to legislatures; PAC/COPU scrutiny.
- Not an investigator, prosecutor or grievance forum.
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** ✅ owner 08 (basic §4, §13.4). Constitutional anatomy is Polity-owned (`Polity/advanced/32_CAG.md`).
**Links:** GOV-2113 (social vs CAG trap), GOV-2099, GOV-2101.
**Trap:** Treating CAG audit as a conviction or as a grievance remedy.
**Mains hook:** Use CAG for money misuse and performance; legislative scrutiny closes the loop.
**Back:** *"Audit finds, the House questions."*

### Flashcard GOV-2098 — Whistle Blowers Protection Act, 2014: enacted but not commenced
**Front:** Is whistle-blower protection in force in India under the 2014 Act?
**Definition (learn):** The Whistle Blowers Protection Act, 2014 received assent on 9 May 2014 but, per the owner, has not been brought into force by notification. The 2015 Amendment Bill passed the Lok Sabha and lapsed.
**Breakdown:**
- Enacted: assent 9 May 2014 (owner).
- Not notified: no commencement notification (owner).
- 2015 amendment bill lapsed (owner).
