- Status words: proposed, partially implemented, adopted, notified, operational.
- Writing "the ARC established" or "the ARC mandated" is an error (owner 10 basic §13.10).

**Key facts / Dates / Data:** ✅ owner 10 basic §7 (trap entry); ✅ owner 10 basic §13.7 ("most examinable discipline").

**Links:** GOV-2157, GOV-2161

**Trap:** False option: "ARC recommendations are law". Correct option: "they are proposals requiring separate instruments".

**Mains hook:** Every citation carries its status label (basic §13.1).

**Back:** *"Recommended is not enacted: the ARC proposes, the state disposes."*

### Flashcard GOV-2163 — Report 1: Right to Information — Master Key to Good Governance

**Front:** What is Second ARC Report 1, and where does its depth sit?

**Definition (learn):** The first of the 15 Second ARC reports, titled "Right to Information: Master Key to Good Governance". Its depth is in topic 08 (RTI process and accountability).

**Breakdown:**
- Routing: Governance 08 (RTI process and accountability); Ethics and Polity cross-links.
- Owner 10 advanced §13.6 destination: proactive disclosure and the accountability ecosystem.

**Key facts / Dates / Data:** ✅ owner 10 basic §3 (routing table); ✅ Wikipedia "Second ARC" 15-title list (title confirmed). Report year: ⚠️ not given by any held source (owner gives only the 2006–2009 span).

**Links:** GOV-2157, GOV-2165

**Trap:** RTI-linked tools are not the Report itself: cite the Report and then the operative RTI instrument (Act, rule) separately, with its own status.

**Mains hook:** Use Report 1 for disclosure and accountability arguments. Qualified conclusion: disclosure works only when the instrument, staff and grievance route operate.

**Back:** *"One: RTI, the master key: open the file to unlock the file."*

### Flashcard GOV-2164 — Report 2: Unlocking Human Capital — Entitlements and Governance

**Front:** What is Second ARC Report 2, and what does it route to?

**Definition (learn):** Report 2, titled "Unlocking Human Capital: Entitlements and Governance". Its depth is in topic 02 (implementation), with a Social Justice cross-link.

**Breakdown:**
- Routing: Governance 02 (implementation); Social Justice cross-link.
- Destination lesson: entitlement design and implementation.

**Key facts / Dates / Data:** ✅ owner 10 basic §3 and §13.6; ✅ Wikipedia "Second ARC" list gives the full subtitle as "Entitlements and Governance, a Case Study" (⚠️ owner omits "a Case Study").

**Links:** GOV-2157, GOV-2165

**Trap:** Subtitle conflict flagged: owner 10 gives "Entitlements and Governance"; Wikipedia gives "Entitlements and Governance, a Case Study". Use the title as the owner states it, and note the subtitle difference.

**Mains hook:** Use Report 2 for entitlement design and delivery gaps. Qualified conclusion: entitlements need implementing capacity, not only legal rights.

**Back:** *"Two: entitlements unlock human capital only when governance delivers them."*

### Flashcard GOV-2165 — Report 3: Crisis Management — From Despair to Hope

**Front:** What is Second ARC Report 3, and why is it treated as a cross-reference only?

**Definition (learn):** Report 3, titled "Crisis Management: From Despair to Hope", sits in the GS-III disaster-management space.

**Breakdown:**
- Routing: GS-III disaster-management linkage; cross-reference only (owner 10 basic §3).
- Not a core GS-II route.

**Key facts / Dates / Data:** ✅ owner 10 basic §3 (routing); ✅ Wikipedia "Second ARC" title list (title confirmed).

**Links:** GOV-2163, GOV-2176

**Trap:** The owner warns against forcing GS-III reports into a GS-II answer (basic §13.6).

**Mains hook:** Use only if the question asks about governance of crisis management. Otherwise cross-reference.

**Back:** *"Three: crisis to hope, but in GS-III's house."*

### Flashcard GOV-2166 — Report 4: Ethics in Governance

**Front:** What is Second ARC Report 4, and which owner covers its ethics content?

**Definition (learn):** Report 4, titled "Ethics in Governance". It is the most Mains-tested ARC report overall (owner 10 basic §5), but personal ethical content is owned by the Ethics knowledge base.

**Breakdown:**
- Governance destinations: 08, 09, 11 for institutional accountability.
- Ethics owns personal probity.
- Ethics cross-link: Ethics 14 (probity concept and philosophical basis of governance).

**Key facts / Dates / Data:** ✅ owner 10 basic §3, §5, §13.6; ✅ Wikipedia "Second ARC" list (title confirmed).

**Links:** GOV-2157, GOV-2167, GOV-2153

**Trap:** Do not duplicate Ethics depth in a Governance answer. Cross-reference only (owner 10 advanced §5).

**Mains hook:** Use Report 4 for institutional accountability, not personal probity. Qualified conclusion: ethics codes help only when linked to institutional enforcement.

**Back:** *"Four: ethics in governance, owned by Ethics, routed to accountability."*

### Flashcard GOV-2167 — Report 5: Public Order

**Front:** What is Second ARC Report 5, and where does it route?

**Definition (learn):** Report 5, titled "Public Order" in the owner, sits mainly in the GS-III internal-security space.

**Breakdown:**
- Routing: GS-III internal-security linkage; cross-reference only (owner 10 basic §3).
- Limited GS-II core relevance.

**Key facts / Dates / Data:** ✅ owner 10 basic §3 (routing). ⚠️ Title conflict: Wikipedia's list reads "Public Law and Order – Justice for each..... peace for all". The owner's short title is "Public Order". Use the owner's short title with the note.

**Links:** GOV-2178, GOV-2168

**Trap:** Do not force Report 5 into a GS-II answer. The full subtitle must be checked against the primary report.

**Mains hook:** Cross-reference only. Qualified conclusion: law and order governance belongs to GS-III, not GS-II.

**Back:** *"Five: public order, GS-III's order."*

### Flashcard GOV-2168 — Report 6: Local Governance

**Front:** What is Second ARC Report 6, and what does it route to?

**Definition (learn):** Report 6, titled "Local Governance", routed to Governance 12 (local governance and service delivery).

**Breakdown:**
- Routing: 12 (local governance and service delivery).
- Destination detail: the 3Fs, activity mapping and parastatal encroachment (owner 10 basic §13.6).
- Grounds the 2024 local-bodies PYQ with Report 15 (owner 10 basic §5).

**Links:** GOV-2157, GOV-2169, GOV-2177

**Trap:** Report 6 requires State action for implementation (GOV-2180). A Union acceptance cannot deliver it alone.

**Mains hook:** Use Report 6 for decentralisation. Qualified conclusion: local bodies need functions, funds and functionaries, and state action decides all three.

**Back:** *"Six: local governance needs the state's hand."*

### Flashcard GOV-2169 — Report 7: Capacity Building for Conflict Resolution

**Front:** What is Second ARC Report 7, and why is it low-priority for GS-II?

**Definition (learn):** Report 7, titled "Capacity Building for Conflict Resolution" in the owner. Routed as a cross-reference with limited GS-II core relevance.

**Breakdown:**

**Links:** GOV-2157, GOV-2178

**Trap:** The owner's short title may not match the report's full title; verify against the primary text.

**Back:** *"Seven: capacity for peace, filed under cross-reference."*

### Flashcard GOV-2171 — Report 9: Social Capital — A Shared Destiny

**Front:** What is Second ARC Report 9, and what does it route to?

**Definition (learn):** Report 9, titled "Social Capital: A Shared Destiny", routed to Governance 04 (NGOs, SHGs and civil-society stakeholders).

**Breakdown:**
- Routing: 04.
- Destination detail: bonding, bridging and linking social capital; civil-society partnership (owner 10 basic §13.6).

**Key facts / Dates / Data:** ✅ owner 10 basic §3 and §13.6; ✅ Wikipedia "Second ARC" title list.

**Links:** GOV-2157, GOV-2160

**Trap:** Social capital is a framework, not a delivery mechanism: do not claim SHG outcomes from the report alone.

### Flashcard GOV-2172 — Report 10: Refurbishing of Personnel Administration

**Front:** What is Second ARC Report 10, and which civil-service and Mission Karmayogi lessons does it route to?

**Definition (learn):** Report 10, titled "Refurbishing of Personnel Administration: Scaling New Heights", routed to Governance 09 (civil services and Mission Karmayogi): neutrality, capacity and performance.

**Breakdown:**

- Destination detail: neutrality, capacity and performance (owner 10 basic §13.6).
- Owner 09 cross-references Report 10 as the civil-service reform source (basic §12).
- Personnel reforms often need state action for state cadres (GOV-2180).

**Key facts / Dates / Data:** ✅ owner 10 basic §3, §13.6; ✅ owner 09 basic §12; ✅ Wikipedia "Second ARC" title list.

**Links:** GOV-2126, GOV-2174, GOV-2180, GOV-2190

**Trap:** Do not claim Mission Karmayogi implements Report 10 without a sourced acceptance or decision trail. Thematic similarity is not implementation (owner 10 basic §13.7).

**Mains hook:** Use Report 10 for the civil-service reform argument, then state each recommendation's status separately from Mission Karmayogi.

**Back:** *"Ten: personnel refurbished; Karmayogi resembles it, but resemblance is not proof."*

### Flashcard GOV-2173 — Report 11: Promoting e-Governance — The Smart Way Forward

**Front:** What is Second ARC Report 11, and what does it route to?

**Definition (learn):** Report 11, titled "Promoting e-Governance: The Smart Way Forward", routed to Governance 05 (e-governance models and user-centricity). Its core lesson is process re-engineering before digitisation.

- Destination lesson: process re-engineering before digitisation (owner 10 basic §13.6).
- Owner 10 advanced §5 says its recommendations aligned with the later Digital India direction.

**Links:** GOV-2157, GOV-2187, GOV-2186

**Trap:** Alignment with Digital India is not proof of implementation (GOV-2186). Do not attribute later programmes to the Report without a documented linkage.

**Mains hook:** "Re-engineer process before digitising." Qualified conclusion: e-governance outcome depends on process reform, not on the portal.

**Back:** *"Eleven: smart e-governance starts with process, not the portal."*

### Flashcard GOV-2174 — Report 12: Citizen-Centric Administration — The Heart of Governance

**Front:** What is Second ARC Report 12, and what does it route to?

**Definition (learn):** Report 12, titled "Citizen-Centric Administration: The Heart of Governance", routed to Governance 07 (citizen-centric administration), covering Sevottam, charters and grievance redress.

**Trap:** Do not state that a Right to Services Act or charter implements Report 12 without a documented linkage (owner 10 basic §13.7).

**Mains hook:** Use Report 12 for the citizen-centric argument, with the status of each charter or grievance instrument stated separately.

### Flashcard GOV-2175 — Report 13: Organisational Structure of Government of India

- Machinery and coordination theme.

**Links:** GOV-2157, GOV-2130, GOV-2153

**Trap:** Machinery reform is not the same as Polity's constitutional composition. Cross-link instead of restating.

**Mains hook:** Use Report 13 for coordination problems across ministries. Qualified conclusion: structural change must come with coordination mechanisms to matter.

### Flashcard GOV-2176 — Report 14: Strengthening Financial Management Systems

**Front:** What is Second ARC Report 14, and what does it route to?

- Routing: 13.
- Destination detail: expenditure control, financial discipline (owner 10 basic §13.6).

### Flashcard GOV-2177 — Report 15: State and District Administration

**Front:** What is Second ARC Report 15, and what does it route to?

- Jointly grounds the 2024 local-bodies PYQ with Report 6 (owner 10 basic §5).

**Links:** GOV-2168, GOV-2180

**Trap:** Subtitle flagged: "A Fresh Look" is owner-only. Use the core title in the answer unless the primary report is checked.

### Flashcard GOV-2178 — Trap: all 15 Second ARC reports are equally central to GS-II

**Front:** Are all 15 Second ARC reports equally central to GS-II?

**Definition (learn):** No. Reports 3, 5, 7 and 8 (crisis management, public order, conflict resolution, terrorism) sit mainly in the GS-III space. GS-II's core routes are Reports 1, 2, 4, 6, 9, 10, 11, 12, 13, 14 and 15, with Report 4 owned by Ethics for probity.

**Breakdown:**
- Cross-reference only: 3, 5, 7, 8.
- Core GS-II routes: as listed in the routing table (GOV-2163 to GOV-2177).
- Owner 10 basic §7 lists the core GS-II routes as 04, 05, 07, 09, 12, 13 plus Ethics for Report 4.

**Key facts / Dates / Data:** ✅ owner 10 basic §3 and §7 (trap entry); ✅ owner 10 basic §13.6 (report-routing bank).

**Links:** GOV-2165, GOV-2167, GOV-2169, GOV-2170

**Trap:** Forcing GS-III reports into a GS-II answer is a routing error. Note: owner 10 basic §7 lists the core routes as 04, 05, 07, 09, 12 and 13, but the routing table also routes Reports 1, 2, 6, 10, 14 and 15 to GS-II topics. Treat the table as authoritative and §7 as a shorthand.

**Mains hook:** Route the question to the report first, then decide whether it sits in GS-II or GS-III.

**Back:** *"Three, Five, Seven, Eight: GS-III's reports; the rest carry GS-II."*

### Flashcard GOV-2179 — 2021 Prelims Q78 PYQ demand (Administrative Reforms Commission and personnel department)

**Front:** What is the 2021 Prelims Q78 demand on the Administrative Reforms Commission?

**Definition (learn):** An objective question on the Administrative Reforms Commission and the creation of a personnel department. Owner 10 routes it here.

**Breakdown:**
- Examinable content: the First ARC's place in administrative history and the institutional lineage of personnel administration.
- First ARC: 1966–1970 (owner); chairs Desai and Hanumanthaiah.
- No year of departmental creation and no recommendation number is asserted by the owner.

**Key facts / Dates / Data:** ✅ owner 10 basic §13.0 and PYQ integration (2018–2023 block). The official 2018–2023 Prelims key is not held locally, so no answer letter or distractor is recorded.

**Links:** GOV-2158, GOV-2182

**Trap:** Do not guess the year of the personnel department or assign a recommendation number. Owner 10 explicitly refuses both.

**Mains hook:** Not a Mains demand. Use it to test the First ARC's remit and the recommendation-versus-implementation distinction.

**Back:** *"Two-one, Seventy-eight: the First ARC's remit, not a year guess."*

### Flashcard GOV-2180 — Federal point: state action for Reports 6, 15 and 10

**Front:** Why does implementation of Reports 6, 15 and much of 10 depend on State action?

**Definition (learn):** Local governance, district administration and much of personnel administration fall wholly or partly in the State or Concurrent List. A Union acceptance cannot deliver them alone.

**Breakdown:**
- Report 6 (Local Governance): State and local action.
- Report 15 (State and District Administration): State action.
- Report 10 (Personnel Administration): much of it needs State action for state cadres.
- Owner 10 advanced §4 and §6: federal-design constraint on uniform implementation.

**Key facts / Dates / Data:** ✅ owner 10 basic §4, §13.5; ✅ owner 10 advanced §4, §6, §7 (State or Concurrent List subjects need state-level action).

**Links:** GOV-2168, GOV-2172, GOV-2177, GOV-2181

**Trap:** Non-implementation of these reports is often a federal-capacity fact, not Union indifference (owner 10 basic §13.5). The owner's "State List or Concurrent List" claims are ⚠️ inference where the report's subject is not specified.

**Mains hook:** The federal point is the most rewarded and under-used argument in ARC answers. Qualified conclusion: state political will and capacity decide these reports, not the Union alone.

**Back:** *"Local, district, state cadre: the Union accepts, the state acts."*

### Flashcard GOV-2181 — Causal chain: why ARC recommendations are only partly implemented

**Front:** What is the causal chain from ARC diagnosis to partial implementation?

**Definition (learn):** A chain of stages where a recommendation can stall at each step: diagnosis, acceptance, instrument, federal step, capacity, incentive and outcome.

**Breakdown:**
- Diagnosis: commission identifies a structural problem.
- Acceptance: government responds report by report (failure: acceptance in principle without instrument).
- Instrument: Act, rule, order or scheme must be created (failure: no legislative time, or a State subject).
- Federal step: local governance, personnel and district reform need State action.
- Capacity: staff, money, systems and skills.
- Incentive: who loses discretion or rent; concentrated resistance, diffuse benefit.
- Outcome: partial adoption, often unattributed, frequently unevaluated.

**Key facts / Dates / Data:** ⚠️ owner 10 basic §13.5 (owner-authored causal analysis; no external source).

**Links:** GOV-2161, GOV-2180, GOV-2190

**Trap:** Stalling at acceptance is the most common error. Do not treat "accepted in principle" as "operational".

**Mains hook:** Trace one report through the chain and name the stage where it stalled. Qualified conclusion: recommendations fail at the instrument and incentive stages more often than at diagnosis.

**Back:** *"Diagnose, accept, instrument, federate, staff, incentivise, outcome: most reforms stall after the second word."*

### Flashcard GOV-2182 — First ARC vs Second ARC: era and focus comparison

**Front:** How do the First and Second ARCs differ in era, remit and focus?

**Definition (learn):** The First ARC (1966–1970) addressed the basic machinery of a newly independent, planning-era state. The Second ARC (2005–2009) addressed governance quality: transparency, citizen-centricity, e-governance, ethics and capacity in a liberalised, technology-enabled, rights-based India.

**Breakdown:**
- First ARC: structure-building; ministries, personnel systems, Centre–State administrative relations; chairs Desai and Hanumanthaiah.
- Second ARC: quality and legitimacy; RTI, e-governance, citizen-centricity, ethics; chair Moily.
- T3 (owner): the First asked how to build an administrative structure; the Second asked how to make it accountable, participatory and technology-enabled.

**Key facts / Dates / Data:** ✅ owner 10 basic §2, §13.2; ✅ owner 10 advanced §2 (era contrast). Dates per Wikipedia as in GOV-2158 and GOV-2159.

**Links:** GOV-2158, GOV-2159, GOV-2179

**Trap:** The false option: "the two ARCs covered identical subject matter". Correct: different eras and emphases. Also note the period conflict for the First ARC (GOV-2158).

**Mains hook:** "Distinguish the First and Second ARCs." Qualified verdict: the shift from structure to quality is itself the comparison, though both carry unfinished agendas.

**Back:** *"First built the machine, Second asked it to answer."*

### Flashcard GOV-2183 — Trap: DARPG can compel ministries and states

**Front:** Can DARPG compel ministries or states to implement ARC recommendations?

**Definition (learn):** No. DARPG's role is coordinative and advisory. It has no independent power to compel implementation by other ministries or state governments.

**Breakdown:**
- Coordination: tracks and circulates recommendations.
- Implementation: rests with the concerned ministry or state.

**Key facts / Dates / Data:** ✅ owner 10 advanced §4 and §7 (trap entry; coordinative and advisory).

**Links:** GOV-2160, GOV-2180

**Trap:** The false option: "DARPG enforces ARC recommendations". Correct: "DARPG coordinates; line ministries and states act".

**Mains hook:** Use this to explain why follow-up tracking alone does not drive change.

**Back:** *"DARPG asks, ministries decide, states act."*

### Flashcard GOV-2184 — Trap: later reforms are direct implementations of ARC reports

**Front:** Are Mission Karmayogi or Digital India direct implementations of specific Second ARC recommendations?

**Definition (learn):** No, unless a specific documented linkage is cited. They are independently driven reforms that often align thematically with ARC direction.

**Breakdown:**
- Mission Karmayogi: thematic continuity with Report 10 (owner 10 basic §13.7).
- RTS Acts: thematic continuity with Report 12 (owner 10 basic §13.7).
- Digital India: alignment with Report 11 (owner 10 advanced §5).

**Key facts / Dates / Data:** ✅ owner 10 basic §13.7 and advanced §8 (trap entries).

**Links:** GOV-2126, GOV-2172, GOV-2173, GOV-2186

**Trap:** The owner calls this "the single most common fabricated claim in ARC answers" (basic §13.7). Thematic similarity is not implementation evidence.

**Mains hook:** Write "resembles" or "is consistent with", not "implements", unless a sourced acceptance or decision trail exists.

**Back:** *"Resemblance is not descent: cite the trail, not the theme."*

### Flashcard GOV-2185 — Four levels of administrative-reform evaluation (advanced)

**Front:** At which four levels should administrative reform be evaluated?

**Definition (learn):** Legal adoption, organisational capability, frontline behaviour and citizen outcome. An accepted recommendation addresses only the first level, if that.

**Breakdown:**
- 1. Legal adoption: instrument exists.
- 2. Organisational capability: staff, systems, budget work.
- 3. Frontline behaviour: officers act differently.
- 4. Citizen outcome: service delivered or grievances resolved.

**Key facts / Dates / Data:** ⚠️ owner 10 advanced §4 (owner-authored analysis).

**Links:** GOV-2161, GOV-2181, GOV-2146

**Trap:** Evaluating a reform by acceptance alone is an error. Level one is necessary but not sufficient.

**Mains hook:** Structure an evaluation by these four levels. Qualified conclusion: the reform's success is judged at level four.

**Back:** *"Law, body, behaviour, citizen: four rungs, not one."*

### Flashcard GOV-2186 — Selective implementation as the norm (advanced)

**Front:** Is selective and uneven implementation the norm for Second ARC recommendations?

**Definition (learn):** Yes, according to the owner: implementation has been selective. Some recommendations, such as aspects of RTI-adjacent transparency and e-governance promotion, were substantially adopted; personnel and structural recommendations remain partial or unadopted.

**Breakdown:**
- Substantial adoption: some RTI-adjacent transparency and e-governance elements (owner 10 advanced §2).
- Partial or no adoption: certain personnel and structural recommendations.
- Digital India alignment with Report 11 was independent, not direct (owner 10 advanced §5).

**Key facts / Dates / Data:** ⚠️ owner 10 advanced §2, §5, §12 (owner analysis; no per-recommendation status recorded).

**Links:** GOV-2173, GOV-2184, GOV-2187, GOV-2192

**Trap:** The false option: "All Second ARC recommendations have been implemented in some form". Owner 10 advanced §8 says that is false.

**Mains hook:** "Second ARC recommendations have been implemented selectively." Name two reports and their status for the answer. Qualified verdict: selective adoption reflects incentives and federal design, not the quality of diagnosis.

**Back:** *"Selective, not sweeping: name the report and its status."*

### Flashcard GOV-2187 — Time-bound commission and the episodic reach of ARC influence (advanced)

**Front:** Why is the Second ARC's influence necessarily episodic?

**Definition (learn):** The Second ARC was a time-bound commission producing analytical reports, not a permanent statutory reform body. Later reforms such as Mission Karmayogi, the DPDP Act and DPI came independently of it, though often consistent with its direction.

**Breakdown:**
- Design: time-bound commission, not a permanent body.
- Later reforms: independent of ARC, often consistent with its diagnosis.
- Treat later reforms as separate current reforms, not direct implementations.

**Key facts / Dates / Data:** ⚠️ owner 10 advanced §3 (deeper analytical layer; owner-authored).

**Links:** GOV-2184, GOV-2186, GOV-2126

**Trap:** Do not treat later statutes or programmes as ARC implementation without a documented link.

**Mains hook:** Explain why a commission's influence depends on later political and institutional channels.

**Back:** *"Time-bound commissions speak; permanent institutions carry it forward."*

### Flashcard GOV-2188 — Datedness objection and reply

**Front:** What is the datedness objection to the Second ARC, and how should it be answered?

**Definition (learn):** The objection: a 2006–09 diagnosis predates Aadhaar-scale DPI, the DPDP framework, statutory service guarantees at scale and post-GST fiscal federalism. The reply: the ARC's analytical categories (capacity, accountability, citizen-centricity, decentralisation) have aged better than its specific prescriptions.

**Breakdown:**
- Objection: the ARC predates DPI, DPDP, service guarantees at scale and GST-era fiscal federalism.
- Reply: separate categories (durable) from prescriptions (dated).
- State which one you are using.

**Key facts / Dates / Data:** ⚠️ owner 10 basic §13.7 (owner-authored counter-argument; the owner's reference to DPDP and GST is not separately sourced here).

**Links:** GOV-2157, GOV-2162, GOV-2187

**Trap:** Do not cite a specific prescription as current law because the category holds.

**Mains hook:** Use the categories to structure a 20-mark answer on continuing relevance; name the prescriptions that have aged.

**Back:** *"Categories age well, prescriptions age fast: say which you cite."*

### Flashcard GOV-2189 — Comprehensiveness vs implementability; commission fatigue

**Front:** What are the comprehensiveness and commission-fatigue objections to the ARC model?

**Definition (learn):** A fifteen-report programme is analytically complete but politically indigestible; incremental reform is implementable but may be incoherent. Repeated commissions can substitute for action, turning reform into a documented intention.

**Breakdown:**
- Comprehensiveness vs implementability.
- Commission fatigue: documentation replacing action.
- Expert-commission model: rigorous diagnosis, no direct enforcement power (owner 10 advanced §6).

**Key facts / Dates / Data:** ⚠️ owner 10 basic §13.7 and advanced §6 (owner-authored analysis).

**Links:** GOV-2157, GOV-2182, GOV-2178

**Trap:** Do not claim the fifteen reports were all rejected or all adopted. The owner treats both as unsupported.

**Mains hook:** Argue that more commissions are not the answer unless existing reports are routed to instruments.

**Back:** *"Too complete to swallow, too many to act on: pick the instrument, not the commission."*

### Flashcard GOV-2190 — Political economy of reform: concentrated cost, diffuse benefit

**Front:** Why do reform recommendations fail more on distribution of costs than on merit?

**Definition (learn):** Recommendations that reduce discretion or reallocate authority face concentrated resistance, while their benefits are diffuse. This is the classic reform political economy.

**Breakdown:**
- Union ministry: accepts what fits its mandate; resists reallocation.
- State government: must act on local governance, personnel and district reports, with its own political economy.
- DARPG: coordinates, cannot compel.
- Civil-service associations: organised interest in tenure, cadre and promotion rules.
- Citizens: diffuse beneficiaries, weakly organised.

**Key facts / Dates / Data:** ⚠️ owner 10 basic §13.2 (T4) and §13.8 (stakeholder variation; owner-authored).

**Links:** GOV-2172, GOV-2181, GOV-2191, GOV-2148

**Trap:** Do not attribute failure to lack of merit in the diagnosis. The owner's point is that diagnosis rarely fails; instruments and incentives do.

**Mains hook:** Explain partial adoption by the cost distribution, not by blame. Qualified conclusion: reform succeeds when the costs are shared or compensated.

**Back:** *"Costs concentrated, benefits diffuse: reform's old political arithmetic."*

### Flashcard GOV-2191 — Boundary case: Report 10 and performance-linked promotion (advanced)

**Front:** Why are Report 10's recommendations on performance-linked promotion and cadre restructuring a boundary case?

**Definition (learn):** These recommendations sit at the harder, politically contested end of the implementation spectrum, and adoption has been much more partial.

**Breakdown:**
- Subject: performance-linked promotion, cadre restructuring.
- Stage: partial adoption (owner's judgment).
- Contrast: Report 11 (e-governance) at the more adopted end.

**Key facts / Dates / Data:** ⚠️ owner 10 advanced §5 (owner boundary case; no per-recommendation status recorded).

**Links:** GOV-2172, GOV-2190, GOV-2186

**Trap:** Do not cite a specific adoption percentage or date for these recommendations; the owner asserts none.

**Mains hook:** Use Report 10 to show where political cost blocks reform. Qualified conclusion: performance-linked promotion needs a reconciled design, not a rhetorical endorsement.

**Back:** *"Ten's hard end: promotion and cadre change politics, not paperwork."*

### Flashcard GOV-2192 — Verified status: DARPG hosts the 15 reports; classify each recommendation

**Front:** What must each ARC recommendation be classified as, and what did the owner verify about DARPG?

**Definition (learn):** Every recommendation must be classified separately as accepted, modified, rejected, implemented or pending. The owner records DARPG as hosting the Second ARC's fifteen reports and related government decisions.

**Breakdown:**
- Labels: accepted, modified, rejected, implemented, pending.
- Source: darpg.gov.in/en/arc-reports (owner-cited; not fetched in this session).
- Owner status note: 5 September 2026.

**Key facts / Dates / Data:** ✅ owner 10 basic §13.10 and advanced semantic-completeness note (rechecked 2026-09-05; not fetched by this session).

**Links:** GOV-2160, GOV-2161, GOV-2193

**Trap:** Do not treat DARPG's hosting of the reports as proof that each is implemented. Hosting is archival; status is per recommendation.

**Mains hook:** Demand a recommendation-by-recommendation status before asserting current practice.

**Back:** *"Hosted is not enacted: classify each one."*

### Flashcard GOV-2193 — Current anchor: no single implementation status across all 15 reports

**Front:** Is there one fixed implementation status for all 15 Second ARC reports?

**Definition (learn):** No. The owner (checked 21 July 2026) asserts no single status across all reports. Status varies recommendation by recommendation and report by report.

**Breakdown:**
- Do not assert a percentage of recommendations accepted or implemented (owner 10 basic §13.10).
- Do not assert a date of implementation for any recommendation.
- Use "proposed", "partially implemented" or "substantially adopted" as the status for each.

**Key facts / Dates / Data:** ✅ owner 10 basic §8 and advanced §9 (21 July 2026 status check).

**Links:** GOV-2192, GOV-2161, GOV-2186

**Trap:** Quoting a single status for the whole Second ARC is an error. Also avoid "the ARC mandated".

**Mains hook:** State the status for two named reports rather than generalising.

**Back:** *"Fifteen reports, fifteen statuses: never one blanket verdict."*

### Flashcard GOV-2194 — PYQ application: no direct Second ARC Mains question in 2024–25

**Front:** Does any 2024–2025 GS-II Mains question name the Second ARC directly?

**Definition (learn):** No. The owner's audit finds no GS-II Mains question in the 2024–2025 papers naming the Second ARC by number. Several PYQs, on local bodies, e-governance, Citizens' Charter and civil services, are conceptually grounded in specific reports.

**Breakdown:**
- Local bodies: Reports 6 and 15 (see `12`).
- e-governance: Report 11 (see `05`).
- Citizens' Charter: Report 12 (see `07`).
- Civil services: Report 10 (see `09`).

**Key facts / Dates / Data:** ⚠️ owner 10 basic §9 (owner-audit statement; the audit ledger itself is not held in this session).

**Links:** GOV-2168, GOV-2173, GOV-2174, GOV-2172

**Trap:** Do not claim that a 2024–25 question "is" a Second ARC question. The routing is conceptual.

**Mains hook:** When a question draws on ARC material, name the report by title and state its stage.

**Back:** *"No ARC by name, but the report's theme in the question: route, then answer."*

### Flashcard GOV-2195 — Stakeholder variation in reform adoption

**Front:** How does each stakeholder shape whether an ARC recommendation is adopted?

**Definition (learn):** A map of the actors who accept, resist or implement recommendations.

**Breakdown:**
- Union ministry: accepts what fits its mandate; resists what reallocates it.
- State government: must act on local governance, personnel and district reports.
- DARPG: tracks and coordinates; cannot compel a line ministry.
- Civil-service association: organised interest in tenure, cadre and promotion.
- Citizen and civil society: diffuse beneficiary; weakly organised.

**Key facts / Dates / Data:** ⚠️ owner 10 basic §13.8 (owner-authored analysis).

**Links:** GOV-2190, GOV-2160, GOV-2180

**Trap:** The citizen is the beneficiary and the least organised actor. Do not assume that public demand alone drives adoption.

**Mains hook:** Name the actor whose cost is concentrated to explain partial adoption.

**Back:** *"Ministry accepts its own, states act on theirs, citizens wait."*

### Flashcard GOV-2196 — Second ARC report years and count: what the owner does and does not give

**Front:** What are the count and years of the Second ARC reports, and what is not verified?

**Definition (learn):** Fifteen reports, produced between 2006 and 2009 according to owner 10. Wikipedia confirms the count of 15 and the constitution in 2005.

**Breakdown:**
- Count: 15 (owner and Wikipedia agree).
- Span: 2006–2009 (owner only; not confirmed by a fetched list of report dates).
- Per-report year: not given by any held source, so none is asserted.

**Key facts / Dates / Data:** ✅ owner 10 basic §2, §6; ✅ Wikipedia "Second ARC" (count 15; constituted 31 August 2005). ⚠️ 2006–2009 span: owner-only, unverified per report.

**Links:** GOV-2159, GOV-2157, GOV-2192

**Trap:** Do not assign a year to a specific report. If asked, say "2006–2009 span" and check the primary report.

**Mains hook:** Use the span only in the sense of the period of production, not as the date of implementation.

**Back:** *"Fifteen reports, two-oh-oh-six to oh-nine: no year guessed for any single one."*

### GROUP — Regulatory Governance and Independent Regulators

### Flashcard GOV-2197 — Independent sectoral regulator (core theme)
**Front:** Why does India use independent sectoral regulators, and what is the core design problem they create?
**Definition (learn):** A statutory body given rule-making, monitoring, licensing and enforcement powers over one economic or technical sector, structured (fixed tenure, funding, technical composition) to be insulated from routine political direction (✅ owner 11).
**Breakdown:**
- Why they exist: technical complexity + need for insulation from day-to-day political control + need for predictable, consistent sector rules (✅ owner 11).
- Examples: RBI, SEBI, IRDAI, PFRDA, TRAI, CERC, PNGRB, CCI (✅ owner 11).
- Each is created by its own statute; the statute alone defines its powers (✅ owner 11).
- Core risk: regulatory capture; the design problem is the trilemma (GOV-2198).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** Principal Indian sectoral/economic regulators named by owner: RBI, SEBI, IRDAI, PFRDA, TRAI, CERC, PNGRB, CCI (✅ owner 11).
**Links:** GOV-2198 (trilemma), GOV-2199 (capture), GOV-2218 (anatomy), GOV-2239 (identical-design trap), GOV-2249 (spectrum).
**Trap:** Independence is protection from improper direction, not immunity from Parliament, courts, audit or reasoned-decision duties (✅ owner 11).
**Mains hook:** Explain why sector regulators exist, then name the trilemma they must manage. Conclusion: independence is a calibrated variable, not a virtue to maximise.
**Back:** *"Insulate for expertise, answer for power, guard against capture."*

### Flashcard GOV-2198 — Autonomy-accountability-expertise trilemma
**Front:** What is the autonomy-accountability-expertise trilemma?
**Definition (learn):** The recurring governance-design tension: more autonomy improves apolitical technical decisions but can weaken democratic accountability; more ministerial control improves accountability but can undermine the technical insulation regulators need. Expertise is the value both arrangements serve (⚠️ owner 11 analytical inference).
**Breakdown:**
- Autonomy pole: fixed tenure, own funding, technical composition, statutory rule-making.
- Accountability pole: annual report to Parliament, government policy directions in specified circumstances, judicial and tribunal review.
- Expertise: the goal both poles must protect; either extreme can compromise it.
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** Named in the owner's core proposition as the analytical frame for any regulator (✅ owner 11).
**Links:** GOV-2197, GOV-2221 (paired design features), GOV-2231 (trade-offs), GOV-2256 (framework).
**Trap:** A generic "independence is good" answer does not engage the trilemma; name all three corners.
**Mains hook:** Use the trilemma to analyse any regulator's design. Verdict: "calibrate, not maximise" (T1 thesis in owner 11).
**Back:** *"Autonomy, accountability, expertise: pick two, never lose the third."*

### Flashcard GOV-2199 — Regulatory capture
**Front:** What is regulatory capture, and how can it occur without corruption?
**Definition (learn):** A regulator comes over time to serve the interests of the industry it regulates rather than the public interest (✅ owner 11).
**Breakdown:**
- Channels: revolving-door staffing, information asymmetry, lobbying, industry-funded research shaping regulatory thinking (✅ owner 11).
- Capture is usually lawful; no illegal transaction need occur (✅ owner 11).
- Outcome: rules serving incumbents; entry barriers framed as quality standards (⚠️ owner 11 causal chain).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** Owner gives no Indian case; do not invent one (owner 11 boundary).
**Links:** GOV-2223 (causal chain and correctives), GOV-2250 (ministry-capture opposite failure), GOV-2243 (trap).
**Trap:** "Capture only happens through direct corruption" is wrong (see GOV-2243).
**Mains hook:** Diagnose capture structurally (information asymmetry) and prescribe procedural correctives (consultation, reasoned orders, cooling-off), not exhortation.
**Back:** *"Capture is quiet: revolving door, information gap, funded expertise."*

### Flashcard GOV-2200 — Tribunalisation and the tribunal rationale
**Front:** What is tribunalisation, and why are specialised tribunals created?
**Definition (learn):** The proliferation of specialised quasi-judicial tribunals to adjudicate disputes in technical or regulatory domains, outside the ordinary court hierarchy (✅ owner 11).
**Breakdown:**
- Rationale: technical expertise unavailable in generalist courts; speed; reduced court backlog (✅ owner 11).
- Typical route: a regulator's specific decision (licence denial, penalty order) is appealed to a specialised tribunal rather than directly to ordinary civil courts (✅ owner 11).
- Dual risk: erosion of procedural safeguards of ordinary courts; proliferation without independence safeguards (✅ owner 11, advanced).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** Tribunal examples in this chain: TDSAT (GOV-2209), APTEL (GOV-2211), SAT (GOV-2204).
**Links:** GOV-2201 (2021 Act), GOV-2245 (dual rationale and risk), GOV-2235 (PYQ 2025).
**Trap:** Tribunals are not courts; their decisions remain subject to higher judicial review (⚠️ general public-law point).
**Mains hook:** Answer in two halves: why tribunals exist, then what the reform changed. The 2025 PYQ rewards both.
**Back:** *"Tribunals buy expertise and speed; they must buy independence too."*

### Flashcard GOV-2201 — Tribunals Reforms Act, 2021
**Front:** What did the Tribunals Reforms Act, 2021 do?
**Definition (learn):** A Union statute that abolished several existing appellate tribunals/authorities, transferred their functions mainly to courts, and prescribed appointment and service conditions for the remaining tribunals (✅ owner 11).
**Breakdown:**
- Statute: Tribunals Reforms Act, 2021; 35 sections (✅ Wikipedia, fetched 2026-11-01).
- Abolition: several appellate tribunals abolished; cases moved mainly to High Courts or other judicial bodies (✅ Wikipedia; names of abolished bodies are owner-only, see GOV-2229).
- Appointment: a Search-cum-Selection Committee recommends members (✅ Wikipedia).
- Tenure: four years; minimum age 50; upper age 67 for members and 70 for chairpersons, or end of term; reappointment allowed (✅ Wikipedia).
- Removal: Central Government removes chairperson or members on the Committee's recommendation (✅ Wikipedia).
- Owner's original provision list also names a "two-name" recommendation panel and a "merely preferable" decision timeline (✅ owner 11); the fetched Wikipedia page does not mention the two-name panel (⚠️ conflict-check).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** Assent date not verified (⚠️). The 2025 Supreme Court ruling on the re-enacted provisions is GOV-2226.
**Links:** GOV-2200, GOV-2226, GOV-2228, GOV-2229, GOV-2240, GOV-2248.
**Trap:** It did not abolish all tribunals (GOV-2240). Its appointment design is subject to constitutional review (GOV-2226).
**Mains hook:** Assess the 2021 reform as a trade of proliferation and delay control against loss of domain-specific adjudication (GOV-2254).
**Back:** *"Fewer tribunals, fifty-year floor, four-year term, search committee."*

### Flashcard GOV-2202 — FSLRC (Financial Sector Legislative Reforms Commission, 2013)
**Front:** What was the FSLRC, and what did it propose for financial regulation?
**Definition (learn):** A commission chaired by Justice B.N. Srikrishna that proposed a Unified Financial Agency (UFA) to subsume most financial regulators except the RBI (for banking and payments) (✅ owner 11).
**Breakdown:**
- Constituted by the Ministry of Finance on 24 March 2011 (✅ Wikipedia, fetched 2026-11-01).
- Chairman: Justice (Retd.) B.N. Srikrishna (✅ Wikipedia).
- Report: owner gives 2013 (✅ owner 11); fetched page says only that it was planned for completion by March 2013 (⚠️ submission date not verified).
- UFA scope: all financial firms other than banking and payments; would take over certain oversight from RBI and the Forward Markets Commission (✅ Wikipedia).
- Companion proposal: draft Indian Financial Code (owner 11, advanced); not on fetched page (⚠️).
- Status: not implemented in full (✅ owner 11).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** Commission constituted 24 March 2011; chair B.N. Srikrishna.
**Links:** GOV-2247 (regulation by activity), GOV-2242 (trap), GOV-2252 (FMC-SEBI), GOV-2255 (unification trade-off), GOV-2258 (UFA vs IFC), GOV-2261 (RBI position).
**Trap:** Do not treat UFA and Indian Financial Code as the same proposal (GOV-2258). Do not assert the UFA was implemented (GOV-2242).
**Mains hook:** Use FSLRC as the standard example of comprehensive reform meeting political-economy resistance; the 2015 FMC-SEBI merger shows incremental success.
**Back:** *"Srikrishna, 2011 constituted, Unified Agency proposed, RBI kept for banking and payments."*

### Flashcard GOV-2203 — Reserve Bank of India (RBI)
**Front:** Which statute governs the RBI, and what is its governance structure?
**Definition (learn):** India's central bank and banking and payments regulator, owned by the Government of India and described as an autonomous regulator with substantial independence (✅ owner 11; ✅ Wikipedia).
**Breakdown:**
- Statute: Reserve Bank of India Act, 1934 (✅ Wikipedia).
- Established: 1 April 1935 (✅ Wikipedia; operations began 2 April 1935).
- Nationalised: 1 January 1949; fully owned by the Ministry of Finance (✅ Wikipedia).
- Central Board (per Wikipedia lead): Governor, up to four Deputy Governors, two finance-ministry representatives, ten government-nominated directors, four regional-board directors (✅ Wikipedia, board count 21; verify before exam use).
- Directors appointed by the Government for four-year terms (✅ Wikipedia).
- Regulated sectors in this owner: banking and payments (✅ owner 11).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** RBI Act 1934; est. 1 April 1935; nationalised 1 January 1949.
**Links:** GOV-2197, GOV-2245 (de facto independence), GOV-2251 (RBI as strong de facto case), GOV-2261 (FSLRC position).
**Trap:** RBI is not a ministry department despite full government ownership; its autonomy is statutory and operational (⚠️ owner 11 analytical point).
**Mains hook:** RBI is the benchmark for relatively strong de facto independence; contrast with regulators dependent on budgetary support.
**Back:** *"Thirty-four Act, thirty-five start, forty-nine nationalised."*

### Flashcard GOV-2204 — Securities and Exchange Board of India (SEBI)
**Front:** What is SEBI's statute, composition and appellate route?
**Definition (learn):** The regulator of India's securities markets (✅ owner 11), given statutory powers by the SEBI Act, 1992 (✅ Wikipedia).
**Breakdown:**
- Set up 12 April 1988 as an executive body; statutory powers from 30 January 1992 through the SEBI Act, 1992 (✅ Wikipedia).
- Parent ministry: Ministry of Finance (✅ Wikipedia).
- Board of nine: chairman nominated by the Union Government; two Finance Ministry members; one RBI member; five others nominated by the Union Government, at least three whole-time (✅ Wikipedia).
- Appellate tribunal: Securities Appellate Tribunal (SAT), three members; second appeal lies directly to the Supreme Court (✅ Wikipedia).
- Powers (quasi-legislative regulations, quasi-judicial orders, executive enforcement): ⚠️ standard knowledge, not fetched.
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** 1988 executive set-up; 1992 statutory; SAT is a three-member appellate body.
**Links:** GOV-2205 (FMC merger), GOV-2200 (tribunal rationale), GOV-2218 (anatomy), GOV-2222 (six tests).
**Trap:** 1988 is the set-up year; 1992 is the statutory year. Do not write SEBI as statutory from 1988 (✅ Wikipedia).
**Mains hook:** SEBI shows a regulator with a multi-member board and a dedicated tribunal; test its independence using the six tests.
**Back:** *"Eighty-eight set up, ninety-two made statutory."*

### Flashcard GOV-2205 — Forward Markets Commission merged into SEBI (2015)
**Front:** What was the Forward Markets Commission, and when was it merged into SEBI?
**Definition (learn):** The commodity-derivatives regulator established under the Forward Contracts (Regulation) Act, 1952, later merged into SEBI (✅ owner 11; ✅ Wikipedia).
**Breakdown:**
- Statute: Forward Contracts (Regulation) Act, 1952 (✅ Wikipedia).
- Established: 1953 (✅ Wikipedia).
- Merger: 28 September 2015 into SEBI (✅ Wikipedia).
- Caveat: Wikipedia gives no Government notification; the effective-date nuance is unverified (⚠️).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** 1952 Act; 1953 set up; 28 September 2015 merged into SEBI.
**Links:** GOV-2204 (SEBI), GOV-2252 (incremental merger lesson), GOV-2202 (FSLRC).
**Trap:** The 1952 Act is the statute; 2015 is the merger year, not the establishment year.
**Mains hook:** The FMC-SEBI merger is a single-sector consolidation that succeeded where FSLRC's comprehensive unification did not.
**Back:** *"Fifty-two Act, fifty-three born, fifteen merged into SEBI."*

### Flashcard GOV-2206 — Insurance Regulatory and Development Authority of India (IRDAI)
**Front:** What is IRDAI's statutory basis, composition, and main powers?
**Definition (learn):** The statutory regulator of the insurance sector, constituted under the Insurance Regulatory and Development Authority Act, 1999 (✅ owner 11; ✅ Wikipedia).
**Breakdown:**
- Constituted: 1999; incorporated April 2000 (✅ Wikipedia).
- Parent ministry: Ministry of Finance (✅ Wikipedia).
- Composition: ten members under Section 4: chairman, five full-time, four part-time, all appointed by the government (✅ Wikipedia).
- Section 14 functions include: registration and cancellation; policyholder protection; qualifications and conduct of intermediaries; inspection and investigation; regulation of investment and solvency margins; fees; accounts; adjudicating disputes between insurers and intermediaries; supervising the Tariff Advisory Committee (✅ Wikipedia).
- Makes regulations under Section 114A of the Insurance Act, 1938 (✅ Wikipedia).
- Appellate route: not confirmed by fetch (⚠️; Securities Appellate Tribunal is the usual route, unverified here).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** IRDA Act 1999; incorporated April 2000; 10-member body.
**Links:** GOV-2221 (accountability pairs), GOV-2218 (anatomy), GOV-2216 (NCLAT contrast), GOV-2222.
**Trap:** IRDA Act 1999 is the statute; do not attribute IRDAI's creation to the 2014 or other amendments without a source (⚠️ owner silent).
**Mains hook:** IRDAI's dispute-adjudication power is a rare example of a regulator also adjudicating between its regulated entities; test that against the six-test checklist.
**Back:** *"Ninety-nine Act, ten members, Section fourteen functions."*

### Flashcard GOV-2207 — Pension Fund Regulatory and Development Authority (PFRDA)
**Front:** What is PFRDA's statute, composition and link to the National Pension System?
**Definition (learn):** The statutory pension-fund regulator, created by the Pension Fund Regulatory and Development Authority Act, 2013 (✅ owner 11; ✅ Wikipedia).
**Breakdown:**
- Act passed 19 September 2013 (assent); notified 1 February 2014 (✅ Wikipedia).
- Interim PFRDA created by government resolution on 23 August 2003 (✅ Wikipedia).
- Parent ministry: Ministry of Finance (✅ Wikipedia).
- Composition: a chairperson plus up to six members, at least three whole-time, all appointed by the Central Government (✅ Wikipedia).
- NPS: government notification 22 December 2003, effective 1 January 2004; extended to all citizens from 1 May 2009 (✅ Wikipedia).
- Appellate route: not confirmed (⚠️).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** 2003 interim body; 2013 Act; 2014 notification; NPS 2004 start.
**Links:** GOV-2206, GOV-2218, GOV-2222.
**Trap:** The NPS began by government notification, not by statute; the PFRDA Act regulates it (✅ Wikipedia).
**Mains hook:** PFRDA shows a regulator whose statutory mandate came after its executive creation; compare with SEBI's 1988-1992 path.
**Back:** *"Oh-three interim, thirteen Act, fourteen notified."*

### Flashcard GOV-2208 — Telecom Regulatory Authority of India (TRAI)
**Front:** What is TRAI's statute, parent ministry, composition and main regulatory field?
**Definition (learn):** The statutory telecom regulator, established by the Telecom Regulatory Authority of India Act, 1997, regulating telecom services and tariffs (✅ owner 11; ✅ Wikipedia).
**Breakdown:**
- Established 20 February 1997 (✅ Wikipedia).
- Parent department: Department of Telecommunications, Ministry of Communications (✅ Wikipedia).
- Composition: a chairperson, up to two full-time members and up to two part-time members (✅ Wikipedia); appointment mechanism not on fetched page (⚠️).
- Main fields: tariffs, interconnection, quality of service, DTH, mobile number portability (✅ Wikipedia).
- Adjudication shifted to TDSAT by 2000 amendment (GOV-2209) (✅ Wikipedia).
- Recommendatory versus regulatory powers split: ⚠️ not verified by fetch.
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** TRAI Act 1997; established 20 February 1997.
**Links:** GOV-2209 (TDSAT), GOV-2215 (ex-ante overlap with CCI), GOV-2210 (CERC comparison), GOV-2239.
**Trap:** TRAI is not the adjudicator of its own disputes after 2000; TDSAT is (GOV-2209).
**Mains hook:** TRAI's tariff power is a classic ex-ante sector power; its overlap with CCI's ex-post conduct law is a live forum issue.
**Back:** *"Ninety-seven Act, two full, two part, TDSAT takes the disputes."*

### Flashcard GOV-2209 — Telecom Disputes Settlement and Appellate Tribunal (TDSAT)
**Front:** What is TDSAT, when and how was it created, and what is its appeal route?
**Definition (learn):** The specialised tribunal created to take over TRAI's adjudicatory and dispute functions so TRAI could focus on regulation (✅ Wikipedia).
**Breakdown:**
- Created in 2000 by amending the TRAI Act, 1997, through an ordinance effective 24 January 2000 (✅ Wikipedia).
- Composition: chairperson and two members, three-year term (✅ Wikipedia, infobox).
- Jurisdiction: any TRAI direction, order or decision can be challenged by appeal to TDSAT (✅ Wikipedia); general disputes among licensor, licensee, service providers and consumers (✅ Wikipedia lead); specific dispute categories and section numbers not verified (⚠️).
- Appeals from TDSAT lie to the Supreme Court (✅ Wikipedia infobox).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** 2000 creation; ordinance effective 24 January 2000.
**Links:** GOV-2208 (TRAI), GOV-2210 (CERC appeal analogy), GOV-2211 (APTEL), GOV-2200.
**Trap:** TDSAT was created by amendment and ordinance, not by the Tribunals Reforms Act, 2021.
**Mains hook:** TDSAT illustrates tribunalisation for telecom disputes; check whether its independence, tenure and appointment rules match the 2025 judicial standards (GOV-2226).
**Back:** *"Two thousand, TRAI's judges, Supreme Court beyond."*

### Flashcard GOV-2210 — Central Electricity Regulatory Commission (CERC)
**Front:** Which Acts constitute and govern CERC, and what is its composition?
**Definition (learn):** The national electricity regulator for inter-State matters and specified central functions (⚠️ scope from general knowledge; owner names CERC as electricity regulator, ✅ owner 11).
**Breakdown:**
- Constituted under the Electricity Regulatory Commissions Act, 1998; formation date 24 July 1998 (✅ Wikipedia).
- Governed now by the Electricity Act, 2003 (✅ Wikipedia).
- Parent ministry: Ministry of Power (✅ Wikipedia).
- Composition under Section 76 of the Electricity Act, 2003: chairperson plus three members, one of whom is ex-officio the Chairperson of the Central Electricity Authority (✅ Wikipedia).
- Appeal: an aggrieved party approaches the Appellate Tribunal for Electricity (APTEL) within 45 days of the order (✅ Wikipedia).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** 1998 ERC Act; 2003 Electricity Act; Section 76 composition; 45-day appeal window.
**Links:** GOV-2211 (APTEL), GOV-2215 (sector-regulator overlap), GOV-2208 (TRAI comparison).
**Trap:** CERC is governed by the Electricity Act 2003, not by the 1998 Act alone (✅ Wikipedia).
**Mains hook:** CERC shows a sector regulator with a dedicated appellate tribunal; compare its appeal window and composition with TRAI's TDSAT route.
**Back:** *"Ninety-eight founded, oh-three governed, forty-five days to appeal."*

### Flashcard GOV-2211 — Appellate Tribunal for Electricity (APTEL)
**Front:** What is APTEL's role in the electricity sector?
**Definition (learn):** The appellate body that hears appeals against CERC and State regulatory commission orders (✅ Wikipedia for CERC appeals; State-commission role ⚠️).
**Breakdown:**
- Set up by the Central Government (✅ Wikipedia).
- Can overrule or amend CERC orders (✅ Wikipedia).
- Appeal window: 45 days from receipt of the order (✅ Wikipedia).
- Establishment year: not fetched (⚠️).
- Next appeal beyond APTEL: not confirmed by fetch (⚠️).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** 45-day filing window (✅ Wikipedia).
**Links:** GOV-2210, GOV-2209 (TDSAT analogy), GOV-2212 (PNGRB comparison, route unverified).
**Trap:** Do not write APTEL's founding year without a source; the owner does not supply it.
**Mains hook:** Appellate tribunals in regulated sectors trade specialist review for delay; cite the 45-day window as a time-bound check.
**Back:** *"Electricity appeals, forty-five days, overrule or amend."*

### Flashcard GOV-2212 — Petroleum and Natural Gas Regulatory Board (PNGRB)
**Front:** Which statute created PNGRB, and what does it regulate?
**Definition (learn):** The statutory board for the downstream petroleum and natural gas sector (✅ owner 11; ✅ Wikipedia).
**Breakdown:**
- Created under the Petroleum and Natural Gas Regulatory Board Act, 2006; board formation date 31 March 2006 (✅ Wikipedia).
- Parent ministry: Ministry of Petroleum and Natural Gas (✅ Wikipedia).
- Downstream functions: refining, transportation, distribution, storage, marketing, supply and sale (✅ Wikipedia).
- Member appointment mechanism: not on fetched page; owner declines to assert composition (⚠️).
- Appeal route (APTEL or other): not verified (⚠️).
- Current membership changes: omitted as time-varying.
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** PNGRB Act 2006; formation 31 March 2006.
**Links:** GOV-2237 (2019 PYQ), GOV-2211 (APTEL), GOV-2218.
**Trap:** PNGRB regulates downstream pipelines and marketing, not upstream exploration (⚠️ general knowledge; the downstream scope is supported by Wikipedia).
**Mains hook:** Use PNGRB as a case of statutory board with a ministry relationship; test the six points, leaving composition unasserted.
**Back:** *"Oh-six Act, oh-six formed, downstream only."*

### Flashcard GOV-2213 — Competition Commission of India (CCI) and the Competition Act, 2002
**Front:** What is the CCI, under which Act, and what are its main powers?
**Definition (learn):** The economy-wide competition regulator, enforcing the prohibition of anti-competitive agreements, abuse of dominant position and regulation of combinations under the Competition Act, 2002 (✅ owner 11; ✅ Wikipedia).
**Breakdown:**
- Competition Act 2002, amended by Competition (Amendment) Act, 2007 (✅ Wikipedia).
- CCI established 14 October 2003; fully functional May 2009 (✅ Wikipedia).
- Parent ministry: Ministry of Corporate Affairs (✅ Wikipedia).
- Composition: chairperson plus minimum two and maximum six members, appointed by the Central Government (✅ Wikipedia).
- Ex-post: abuse of dominant position (GOV-2214); ex-ante: merger control of combinations (GOV-2215).
- Appeal route: NCLAT then Supreme Court (✅ owner 11; not confirmed by fetch).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** Act 2002; amended 2007; CCI est. 14 October 2003; fully functional May 2009.
**Links:** GOV-2214, GOV-2215, GOV-2216, GOV-2236 (2023 PYQ).
**Trap:** Dominance is not unlawful; its abuse is (✅ owner 11). Do not cite any CCI case, party or penalty from this card; the owner forbids it and no source is held.
**Mains hook:** CCI is the correct instrument for conduct, but conduct regulation is slow against structural market power (owner 11 verdict).
**Back:** *"Two-zero-zero-two Act, oh-three born, nine-years-to-function."*

### Flashcard GOV-2214 — CCI and abuse of dominant position: forms of abuse
**Front:** What forms can an abuse of dominant position take, and what is the governance logic?
**Definition (learn):** Conduct by a dominant enterprise that harms competition and consumers; the CCI acts on it ex-post (✅ owner 11; ✅ Wikipedia for prohibition).
**Breakdown:**
- Forms named by owner: predatory or unfair pricing; unfair or discriminatory conditions; limiting production or technical development; denial of market access; tying and bundling; leveraging dominance into another market (✅ owner 11).
- Logic: competition law protects the process, not individual competitors; object is consumer welfare and market access (✅ owner 11).
- Limitations: proving dominance needs contested market definition; digital markets (network effects, zero-price services, data) strain turnover-based tests; remedies arrive late (⚠️ owner 11).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** Six forms as listed; no case numbers held in owner.
**Links:** GOV-2213, GOV-2215, GOV-2236 (PYQ 2023).
**Trap:** Dominance alone is not an offence; the question is abuse (GOV-2213).
**Mains hook:** Structure a 10-mark answer around dominance-to-abuse logic, the six forms, and the market-definition and digital limitations.
**Back:** *"Price, condition, limit, deny, tie, leverage: six abuses."*

### Flashcard GOV-2215 — CCI versus sector regulators: ex-post conduct vs ex-ante licensing
**Front:** How does the CCI's jurisdiction differ from sector regulators such as TRAI, CERC, PNGRB, SEBI and IRDAI?
**Definition (learn):** The CCI regulates conduct after it occurs and reviews combinations; sector regulators act ex-ante on licences, tariffs and sector rules (✅ owner 11).
**Breakdown:**
- CCI: economy-wide, ex-post conduct scrutiny; merger control of combinations causing or likely to cause appreciable adverse effect on competition in India (✅ Wikipedia).
- Sector regulators: ex-ante licence conditions and tariffs (✅ owner 11).
- Overlap is a genuine jurisdictional question creating forum uncertainty for the regulated entity (✅ owner 11).
- Ex-ante and ex-post labels are analytical characterisation (⚠️ Wikipedia does not use the terms).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** Combinations test: "appreciable adverse effect on competition within India" (✅ Wikipedia).
**Links:** GOV-2213, GOV-2214, GOV-2208 (TRAI tariffs), GOV-2231 (trade-off).
**Trap:** The CCI is not a sector regulator; sector regulators do not supersede Competition Act prohibitions automatically (⚠️ general; owner frames it as an overlap question).
**Mains hook:** Use the ex-ante/ex-post distinction to explain why both regimes exist and where forum conflicts arise.
**Back:** *"Sector licenses first, competition checks conduct after."*

### Flashcard GOV-2216 — National Company Law Appellate Tribunal (NCLAT) as CCI appellate route
**Front:** Where do appeals against CCI orders lie?
**Definition (learn):** Under the owner's account, appeals from CCI go to the NCLAT, then to the Supreme Court (✅ owner 11).
**Breakdown:**
- Route: CCI, then NCLAT, then Supreme Court (✅ owner 11).
- Fetched Wikipedia page does not describe this route (⚠️ not confirmed by fetch).
- Owner notes the CCI's own enforcement outcomes are frequently suspended in appeal (✅ owner 11).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** Appeal chain as above.
**Links:** GOV-2213, GOV-2209 (TDSAT contrast), GOV-2218 (appellate route in anatomy).
**Trap:** Do not route CCI appeals to TDSAT or APTEL; those belong to telecom and electricity respectively.
**Mains hook:** Appellate delay (suspensions in appeal) is itself a limitation of the CCI; mention it under the "discuss the role" limitations.
**Back:** *"CCI to NCLAT, NCLAT to Supreme Court."*

### Flashcard GOV-2217 — Food Safety and Standards Authority of India (FSSAI)
**Front:** What statute created FSSAI, and what is its structure?
**Definition (learn):** The statutory food regulator created under the Food Safety and Standards Act, 2006, which consolidated earlier food laws handled by different ministries (✅ owner 11; ✅ Wikipedia).
**Breakdown:**
- Act: Food Safety and Standards Act, 2006 (✅ Wikipedia).
- Formation date: 5 September 2008 (✅ Wikipedia); the Act was operationalised in 2006 (✅ Wikipedia).
- Parent ministry: Ministry of Health and Family Welfare (✅ Wikipedia).
- Composition: a chairperson and 22 members; chairperson is non-executive, appointed by the Central Government, of at least Secretary-to-GoI rank under Section 5(3) (✅ Wikipedia).
- Mandate: science-based food-safety standards; regulation of manufacture, storage, distribution, sale and import of food; accreditation guidelines for testing labs (✅ Wikipedia).
- Boundary: owner declines to assert composition for FSSAI; the composition above is a fetched check, not a PYQ answer key (owner 11 boundary).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** FSS Act 2006; formation 5 September 2008; Ministry of Health and Family Welfare.
**Links:** GOV-2238 (2018 PYQ), GOV-2218, GOV-2221.
**Trap:** The 2006 Act is the statute; the 2008 date is the formation date. Do not confuse the ministry (MoHFW) with the Ministry of Food Processing (⚠️ general).
**Mains hook:** FSSAI shows a single-statute consolidation of food law; test it against the six-test checklist.
**Back:** *"Oh-six Act, oh-eight formed, health ministry parent."*

### Flashcard GOV-2218 — Regulator anatomy: the six-part test for any statute-based body
**Front:** What generic anatomy lets you answer statement-based questions about an unfamiliar regulator?
**Definition (learn):** Every Indian sectoral regulator has (i) a parent statute that defines its powers; (ii) a defined jurisdiction (sector, activity or both); (iii) rule-making, licensing/registration, monitoring and enforcement functions; (iv) an appellate route, usually a specified tribunal rather than a direct writ; (v) a parent ministry for policy, which is not control over decisions (✅ owner 11).
**Breakdown:**
- Test each statement against the statute named in the stem, never against a remembered composition (✅ owner 11).
- Parent ministry sets policy; it does not control individual regulatory decisions (✅ owner 11).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** Anatomy applies to RBI, SEBI, IRDAI, PFRDA, TRAI, CERC, PNGRB, CCI, FSSAI as named in the owner.
**Links:** GOV-2219 (parent ministry trap), GOV-2222 (six-test checklist), GOV-2237, GOV-2238.
**Trap:** A regulator's statute, not its ministry or a generic model, determines its powers (✅ owner 11).
**Mains hook:** Start any regulator answer with the statute, then the function and the appellate route.
**Back:** *"Statute, jurisdiction, functions, appeal, ministry: five boxes."*

### Flashcard GOV-2219 — Parent ministry is not control over regulatory decisions
**Front:** Does the parent ministry control a regulator's decisions?
**Definition (learn):** No. The ministry holds policy responsibility; statute-specific powers, not ministerial command, govern individual regulatory decisions (✅ owner 11).
**Breakdown:**
- Examples of parent ministries: SEBI and IRDAI and PFRDA and CCI (MCA) and RBI (Finance); TRAI (DoT under Communications); CERC (Power); PNGRB (Petroleum and Natural Gas); FSSAI (Health and Family Welfare) (✅ Wikipedia).
- Government policy directions are an accountability tool in specified circumstances, not routine control (✅ owner 11).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** Parent ministries as listed in GOV-2203 to GOV-2217.
**Links:** GOV-2218, GOV-2221, GOV-2250.
**Trap:** Treating the parent ministry as the regulator's boss is an error; the statute is the source of the regulator's power.
**Mains hook:** Distinguish policy (ministry) from case decisions (regulator); the owner says the parent ministry should hold policy but not cases.
**Back:** *"Ministry steers policy, the statute governs the case."*

### Flashcard GOV-2220 — Statutory vs constitutional vs executive bodies
**Front:** How should a body be classified: statutory, constitutional or executive?
**Definition (learn):** Regulators in this owner are statutory bodies created by Act of Parliament; CAG is constitutional; a body created by executive order is neither (✅ owner 11).
**Breakdown:**
- Statutory: SEBI (1992 Act), TRAI (1997 Act), IRDAI (1999 Act), PFRDA (2013 Act), CERC (1998 and 2003 Acts), PNGRB (2006 Act), CCI (2002 Act), FSSAI (2006 Act) (✅ owner 11; ✅ Wikipedia for years).
- Constitutional: CAG (✅ owner 11).
- Executive: created by government resolution or order; e.g., SEBI in 1988 and PFRDA interim in 2003 before their statutes (✅ Wikipedia).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** SEBI executive 1988 -> statutory 1992; PFRDA interim resolution 23 August 2003 -> statutory 2013.
**Links:** GOV-2204, GOV-2207, GOV-2197.
**Trap:** Getting this wrong in the first line of an answer costs disproportionately (✅ owner 11).
**Mains hook:** Open with the classification, then move to design features.
**Back:** *"Parliament makes statutory, Constitution makes constitutional, orders make executive."*

### Flashcard GOV-2221 — Autonomy design features paired with accountability counterparts
**Front:** What autonomy features protect independence, and what accountability feature restores answerability for each?
**Definition (learn):** A paired table showing each design tool as a double-edged choice (✅ owner 11, evidence bank A).
**Breakdown:**
- Fixed-term appointment (protects against removal for unwelcome decisions) <-> annual report laid before Parliament (legislative visibility).
- Funding from fees or cess rather than annual grant (freedom from budgetary leverage) <-> audit of the regulator's own accounts (financial answerability).
- Technical or expert composition (decision quality) <-> government power to issue policy directions in specified circumstances (democratic primacy on policy, not cases).
- Statutory rule-making power (act without case-by-case approval) <-> mandatory consultation and published response (procedural legitimacy).
- Security of tenure and service conditions (resistance to pressure) <-> appellate tribunal and judicial review (correction of individual decisions).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** Five pairs as listed (✅ owner 11).
**Links:** GOV-2198 (trilemma), GOV-2222 (six tests), GOV-2254, GOV-2257.
**Trap:** Listing autonomy features alone reads as advocacy; pair each with its counterpart (✅ owner 11).
**Mains hook:** Use the five pairs to show the trilemma is a design problem with explicit trade-offs.
**Back:** *"Each shield gets its mirror: tenure and tribunal, fees and audit."*

### Flashcard GOV-2222 — Six-test design checklist for any regulator
**Front:** What six tests should be applied to any regulator, including an unfamiliar one?
**Definition (learn):** A checklist: statutory mandate; appointment and removal; funding; rule-making process; enforcement due process; review and reporting (✅ owner 11).
**Breakdown:**
- 1. Mandate: what it may do, under which Act.
- 2. Appointment and removal: who selects, criteria, arbitrary removal risk.
- 3. Funding: source controlled by the regulated entity or ministry?
- 4. Rule-making: pre-consultation, published response, regulatory impact assessment.
- 5. Enforcement: notice, hearing, reasoned order, proportionate penalty, no bias.
- 6. Review: appellate route; reporting to the legislature.
- A regulator that fails tests 4 and 5 is not legitimised by passing 1-3 (✅ owner 11).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** Six tests as above.
**Links:** GOV-2218, GOV-2221, GOV-2225 (RIA), GOV-2233 (process), GOV-2257 (rule-quality).
**Trap:** Strong statutory design does not compensate for poor consultation or enforcement due process (✅ owner 11).
**Mains hook:** Apply the six tests in a 20-mark answer; name which test fails and why.
**Back:** *"Mandate, appoint, fund, consult, enforce, appeal."*

### Flashcard GOV-2223 — Capture causal chain and correctives
**Front:** What is the causal chain from independence design to capture, and what corrects it?
**Definition (learn):** A structural sequence in which technical complexity and information asymmetry lead to dependence on industry, then to revolving door and agenda capture (✅ owner 11, section 13.6).
**Breakdown:**
- Technical complexity + predictability need -> statutory regulator created.
- Information asymmetry: industry knows the sector better.
- Dependence on industry data, expertise and personnel.
- Revolving door, funded research, repeat-player access.
- Agenda and framing capture: regulator adopts industry's problem definition.
- Outcome: rules serving incumbents; entry barriers framed as quality standards.
- Correctives: mandatory consultation with published responses; reasoned orders; cooling-off periods; in-house technical capacity; regulatory impact assessment; consumer representation; appellate review; parliamentary reporting; conflict-of-interest disclosure (✅ owner 11).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** No Indian case named in owner (owner 11 boundary).
**Links:** GOV-2199, GOV-2250, GOV-2225, GOV-2232.
**Trap:** No illegal transaction is needed for capture (✅ owner 11).
**Mains hook:** Use the chain to explain capture as a structural consequence rather than moral failing; prescribe the procedural correctives.
**Back:** *"Asymmetry, revolving door, framing, incumbents win, consult and reason."*

### Flashcard GOV-2224 — Jan Vishwas (Amendment of Provisions) Act, 2023
**Front:** What did the Jan Vishwas Act, 2023 do?
**Definition (learn):** A Central Act that decriminalised or rationalised provisions across multiple Central Acts, as an anchor for trust-based regulation (✅ owner 11).
**Breakdown:**
- Decriminalised or rationalised 183 provisions across 42 Central Acts (✅ owner 11; fetch of the Wikipedia page returned 404, so the count is not independently verified).
- Principle: reserve criminal process for serious wrongdoing; use civil or administrative penalties for minor compliance default (✅ owner 11).
- Decriminalisation is not deregulation; proportional penalty, hearing, reasoned order and appeal remain necessary (✅ owner 11).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** 2023 Act; 183 provisions; 42 Central Acts (owner-only count).
**Links:** GOV-2230 (trust-based route), GOV-2222 (due process test).
**Trap:** Decriminalisation is not the same as removing regulation (✅ owner 11).
**Mains hook:** Argue for graded penalties on technical default, with criminal sanctions kept for fraud, violence and public-safety harm.
**Back:** *"One-eighty-three provisions, forty-two Acts, civil not criminal for defaults."*

### Flashcard GOV-2225 — Regulatory Impact Assessment (RIA)
**Front:** What is a Regulatory Impact Assessment, and is it mandatory in India?
**Definition (learn):** An ex-ante appraisal of a proposed regulation's costs, benefits and alternatives (✅ owner 11).
**Breakdown:**
- Main available discipline on rule-making quality (⚠️ owner 11).
- Institutionalised unevenly in India; not a uniformly binding requirement (✅ owner 11).
- Pairs with consultation, published responses and ex-post review (✅ owner 11, advanced).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** None fixed in owner.
**Links:** GOV-2222, GOV-2223, GOV-2257.
**Trap:** Do not assert that RIA is mandatory for all regulators (✅ owner 11 "do not assert").
**Mains hook:** Use RIA as the test of regulatory process quality; name its uneven adoption as a limitation.
**Back:** *"Before the rule, count costs and alternatives."*

### Flashcard GOV-2226 — 2025 INSC 1330: Supreme Court on tribunal appointments (19 November 2025)
**Front:** What did the Supreme Court decide on 19 November 2025 about the Tribunals Reforms Act, 2021?
**Definition (learn):** The Court invalidated the re-enacted provisions of the 2021 Act as an impermissible legislative override and made earlier Madras Bar Association standards controlling (✅ owner 11). Judgment reference 2025 INSC 1330 (owner-only).
**Breakdown:**
- Invalidated: fifty-year minimum age; two-name recommendation panel and merely preferable decision timeline; four-year tenure; impugned allowance framework (✅ owner 11).
- Fetched Wikipedia page confirms the 50-year minimum age was struck down as arbitrary and a National Tribunals Commission was directed (✅ Wikipedia, fetched 2026-11-01); the other heads are owner-only (⚠️).
- Controlling standards: MBA (IV) and MBA (V) (✅ owner 11).
- Direction: Union Government to establish a National Tribunals Commission within four months (✅ owner 11; ✅ Wikipedia).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** 19 November 2025; 2025 INSC 1330; four-month NTC direction.
**Links:** GOV-2201, GOV-2227, GOV-2228, GOV-2241 (trap), GOV-2259.
**Trap:** Not only the four-year tenure was invalidated (GOV-2241).
**Mains hook:** The judgment makes tribunal design a constitutionally controlled field; interim judicial management fills the gap until legislation is compliant.
**Back:** *"Nineteen November, fifty-year floor fell, four months to a Commission."*

### Flashcard GOV-2227 — 9 March 2026 interim order and current-status conflict
**Front:** What did the 9 March 2026 Supreme Court order do, and what is the status of the tribunal reform?
**Definition (learn):** An interim continuity order that allowed temporary extensions for specified serving incumbents until 8 September 2026 or the applicable maximum age, whichever was earlier (✅ owner 11, owner-only).
**Breakdown:**
- Did not revive the invalidated provisions (✅ owner 11).
- The owner gives two status cut-off dates: 10 August 2026 (basic section 8) and 13 August 2026 (basic section 13.7 and 13.10). Flag conflict (⚠️).
- No official establishment notification for the National Tribunals Commission was located by the cut-off (✅ owner 11, status unresolved).
- Today's date is 1 November 2026, after the 8 September 2026 end date; current status is not verified (⚠️).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** 9 March 2026; 8 September 2026; as-of 10 or 13 August 2026 (conflicting in owner).
**Links:** GOV-2226, GOV-2228, GOV-2201.
**Trap:** The March 2026 order is a continuity measure, not a merits reversal (✅ owner 11).
**Mains hook:** Describe implementation as unresolved, not completed, and state the as-of date.
**Back:** *"March extension, September end, Commission unresolved."*

### Flashcard GOV-2228 — National Tribunals Commission (NTC)
**Front:** What is the National Tribunals Commission, and what is its status?
**Definition (learn):** A body the Supreme Court directed the Union Government to set up within four months of 19 November 2025, to institutionalise tribunal appointment and administration (✅ owner 11; ✅ Wikipedia).
**Breakdown:**
- Direction date: 19 November 2025; four-month deadline (✅ owner 11; ✅ Wikipedia).
- Court described it as a structural safeguard for independence, transparency and uniformity (✅ Wikipedia).
- Establishment notification not located by cut-off (✅ owner 11).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** Four months from 19 November 2025.
**Links:** GOV-2226, GOV-2227, GOV-2248.
**Trap:** Do not assert the Commission was established or not established as a settled fact (✅ owner 11).
**Mains hook:** Present the NTC as the institutional answer to appointment-design defects, with status flagged.
**Back:** *"Commission to standardise, not yet confirmed."*

### Flashcard GOV-2229 — Appellate bodies abolished by the Tribunals Reforms Act, 2021
**Front:** Which appellate bodies did the Tribunals Reforms Act, 2021 abolish, and where did their functions go?
**Definition (learn):** The Act abolished several appellate tribunals or authorities, transferring functions mainly to High Courts (✅ owner 11; ✅ Wikipedia for the general rule).
**Breakdown:**
- Named by owner: appellate bodies under the Cinematograph Act, 1952; Trade Marks Act, 1999; Copyright Act, 1957; certain Customs Act appellate bodies; and certain Airports Authority of India Act bodies (✅ owner 11).
- Functions transferred mainly to High Courts (✅ owner 11; ✅ Wikipedia general).
- Names of abolished bodies not verified by fetch (⚠️).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** Cinematograph Act 1952; Trade Marks Act 1999; Copyright Act 1957.
**Links:** GOV-2201, GOV-2240 (trap), GOV-2254.
**Trap:** It abolished named bodies, not all tribunals (GOV-2240).
**Mains hook:** Discuss the cost of the transfer to High Courts for specialised IP and customs matters (owner advanced 3).
**Back:** *"Cinema, marks, copyright, customs, airports: transferred to courts."*

### Flashcard GOV-2230 — Trust-based regulation and the decriminalisation route
**Front:** How should a regulator decide between criminal and civil sanctions?
**Definition (learn):** Diagnose the conduct: fraud, violence, public safety or deliberate evasion call for criminal sanction; technical or default failures use warnings, cure periods, graded civil penalties and digital compliance (✅ owner 11, section 13.7; ⚠️ design route is analytical).
**Breakdown:**
- Serious or repeated harm: retain criminal sanction (✅ owner 11).
- Minor default: warning, cure period, graded civil penalty (✅ owner 11).
- Test reform against deterrence, proportionality, administrative discretion, due process, appellate review (✅ owner 11).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** Anchored to Jan Vishwas Act 2023 (GOV-2224).
**Links:** GOV-2224, GOV-2222.
**Trap:** Decriminalisation is not deregulation (✅ owner 11).
**Mains hook:** Avoid the false choice between criminalising everything and removing regulation.
**Back:** *"Fraud stays criminal, defaults get cured."*

### Flashcard GOV-2231 — Core trade-offs in regulatory governance
**Front:** What trade-offs should a balanced regulatory answer name?
