**Definition (learn):** The Act made NADA a statutory body, replacing its earlier society-registered form, and created the framework for anti-doping rule violations and results management in India.
**Breakdown:**
- Before 2022: a registered society.
- After 2022: statutory body.
- Governs violations and results management.
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** National Anti-Doping Act, 2022 (✅ owner 16 §6.2 and §6.3).
**Links:** GOV-3127, GOV-3134, GOV-3138.
**Trap:** NADA did not become statutory through the 2025 Governance Act; its statute is 2022.
**Mains hook:** Anti-doping has statutory footing; the question is independence.
**Back:** *"Twenty-twenty-two made NADA a statute, not a society."*

### Flashcard GOV-3134 — National Anti-Doping (Amendment) Act, 2025 (secondary reporting)
**Front:** What does the National Anti-Doping (Amendment) Act, 2025 reportedly do, and how certain is it?
**Definition (learn):** An enacted amendment whose reported thrust is operational independence of NADA's leadership, relocation of the power to constitute the appeal panel, and clearer appeal routes to CAS.
**Breakdown:**
- Recorded from secondary reporting, not a Gazette text obtained by the owner.
- Section numbers and commencement dates not stated.
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** ⚠️ Owner 16 §6.2 (secondary; verify before citation).
**Links:** GOV-3133, GOV-3128, GOV-3127.
**Trap:** Do not state section numbers or commencement dates for the 2025 amendment.
**Mains hook:** Anti-doping independence is a live reform question; cite it as enacted with details to verify.
**Back:** *"Twenty-twenty-five amends NADA's independence: verify the text before quoting it."*

### Flashcard GOV-3135 — SAI: a registered society, not a statutory corporation
**Front:** What is the legal character of the Sports Authority of India?
**Definition (learn):** An autonomous body registered as a society under the Societies Registration Act, 1860, under the Ministry of Youth Affairs and Sports.
**Breakdown:**
- Training, infrastructure and athlete-support implementation.
- Not created by its own statute as a statutory corporation.
- The 2025 Act does not convert SAI's organisational form.
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** Societies Registration Act, 1860 (✅ owner 16 §6.3).
**Links:** GOV-3125, GOV-3136, GOV-3131.
**Trap:** SAI is commonly mistaken for a statutory body; this is the owner's precision point (❌ owner 16 §6.3).
**Mains hook:** Legal form determines accountability; a society is not a statutory corporation.
**Back:** *"SAI is a society under the 1860 Act, not a statute's child."*

### Flashcard GOV-3136 — Indian Olympic Association: a private national Olympic committee
**Front:** What is the Indian Olympic Association, and is it immune from national law?
**Definition (learn):** The National Olympic Committee, a private body within the Olympic Movement.
**Breakdown:**
- Autonomy under the Olympic Charter coexists with national law.
- Not immune from national law.
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** ✅ owner 16 §6.3 and §8 (recognised national Olympic committee).
**Links:** GOV-3125, GOV-3135, GOV-3137.
**Trap:** Olympic Charter autonomy does not exempt the IOA from national statutes.
**Mains hook:** Autonomy under charter is not exemption from law.
**Back:** *"IOA: Olympic by charter, accountable by law."*

### Flashcard GOV-3137 — BCCI is not a statutory national federation
**Front:** How should the BCCI be described in a sports-governance answer?
**Definition (learn):** The Board of Control for Cricket in India is a cricket governing body, not a statutory national federation under the 2025 framework (owner view).
**Breakdown:**
- Tournament, governing body and organiser must not be conflated.
- Owner distinguishes it from national sports federations.
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** ✅ owner 16 §8 (indispensable distinction note).
**Links:** GOV-3136, GOV-3125, GOV-3124.
**Trap:** Do not describe the BCCI as a national federation or as a statutory body without verification.
**Mains hook:** The BCCI case shows why governing body, organiser and tournament must be kept apart.
**Back:** *"Cricket's board is a governing body, not a statutory federation."*

### Flashcard GOV-3138 — Strict liability in anti-doping
**Front:** What is strict liability in anti-doping, and what is its counter-argument?
**Definition (learn):** An athlete is responsible for what is in their body irrespective of intent.
**Breakdown:**
- Intent is unprovable, so strict liability is necessary.
- Harsh on athletes affected by contamination or negligent support staff.
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** ✅ owner 16 §7.5 and §7.6.
**Links:** GOV-3127, GOV-3133, GOV-3128.
**Trap:** Strict liability does not mean a sanction is automatic without process; due process still matters.
**Mains hook:** Strict liability is a practical necessity that places a heavy burden on athletes.
**Back:** *"Intent is not a defence; the body is the evidence."*

### Flashcard GOV-3139 — Four Grand Slam tournaments: first years and venues
**Front:** Which are the four Grand Slam tournaments, and when was each first held?
**Definition (learn):** The Australian Open, Roland-Garros, Wimbledon and the US Open.
**Breakdown:**
- Wimbledon: first held 1877; grass; All England Lawn Tennis and Croquet Club, London.
- US Open: first held 1881; hard courts; Flushing Meadows, New York.
- Australian Open: first held 1905; hard courts; Melbourne Park.
- French Open (Roland-Garros): first held 1891 as a club event; major status from 1925; clay; Paris.
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** Wikipedia "Grand Slam (tennis)" fetched 1 November 2026 (✅ Wikipedia). Owner 16 names the four tournaments (✅ owner 16 §3).
**Links:** GOV-3140, GOV-3141, GOV-3142.
**Trap:** The first-held year is not the year the tournament became a Grand Slam; French Open became a major in 1925.
**Mains hook:** Use the four tournaments as an example of independent organisers with shared governance.
**Back:** *"Seventy-seven, eighty-one, nineteen-oh-five, ninety-one: first editions, not majors."*

### Flashcard GOV-3140 — Grand Slam governance: shared structure, separate ownership
**Front:** How do the four Grand Slams govern themselves?
**Definition (learn):** Each event has its own organiser and national body, while the four cooperate through a shared governance structure and a common Grand Slam Rule Book.
**Breakdown:**
- Owner 16: shared governance does not erase separate ownership.
- Wikipedia fetch (1 Nov 2026): the Grand Slams are overseen by "World Tennis", with ATP and WTA awarding ranking points; each event is run by its national body (Tennis Australia, FFT, All England Club, USTA).
- Owner 16 does not name "World Tennis"; flagged as a conflict to verify before citation.
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** Common Grand Slam Rule Book (✅ owner 16 §3). "World Tennis" (✅ Wikipedia fetched; ⚠️ not checked against a tournament rule book).
**Links:** GOV-3139, GOV-3141, GOV-3144.
**Trap:** Shared governance does not mean a single owner operates all four (❌ owner 16 §5).
**Mains hook:** Separate cooperation from ownership in any multi-organiser event.
**Back:** *"Four owners, one rule book, possibly a world body: ownership and rules are different questions."*

### Flashcard GOV-3141 — Four entry routes to a competition
**Front:** What are the four distinct entry routes to a tournament?
**Definition (learn):** Routes are not interchangeable, and eligibility is a separate question from acceptance into a draw.
**Breakdown:**
- Direct ranking acceptance.
- Qualifying.
- Wild card: discretionary entry outside ordinary ranking acceptance.
- Protected or special entry.
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** ✅ owner 16 §7.5.
**Links:** GOV-3139, GOV-3142, GOV-3143.
**Trap:** "Internationally ranked" does not mean automatic entry (owner 16 §3).
**Mains hook:** Each route has its own rule; state which route a player uses.
**Back:** *"Rank, qualify, wild card, protected: four doors, not one."*

### Flashcard GOV-3142 — Wild card: slot limit is not a player cap
**Front:** Does a wild-card rule limit how many wild cards a player may get?
**Definition (learn):** A wild card is discretionary entry. A limit on wild-card slots in a draw is not a cap on the player.
**Breakdown:**
- Wild card bypasses ordinary ranking acceptance, not eligibility, conduct or anti-doping rules.
- Distinguish limits on slots in a draw from any alleged lifetime cap on a player.
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** ✅ owner 16 §3 and §7.5.
**Links:** GOV-3141, GOV-3144.
**Trap:** A wild card does not mean rules do not apply (❌ owner 16 §5).
**Mains hook:** Evaluate each statement about wild cards against the rule book separately.
**Back:** *"Wild card skips ranking, not the rulebook; slots are not a cap on people."*

### Flashcard GOV-3143 — Main draw, qualifying, and age as eligibility
**Front:** Are main draw and qualifying the same stage, and is an age threshold an acceptance rule?
**Definition (learn):** No. They are separate stages with separate draw sizes and entry routes. An age threshold is an eligibility condition, not proof of acceptance into the draw.
**Breakdown:**
- Main draw: the main competition.
- Qualifying: a separate stage with separate entry.
- Eligibility and acceptance are distinct tests.
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** ✅ owner 16 §3 and §7.5.
**Links:** GOV-3141, GOV-3144.
**Trap:** Meeting an age rule does not guarantee entry.
**Mains hook:** Check eligibility and acceptance as two tests.
**Back:** *"Eligible is not accepted; qualifying is not the main draw."*

### Flashcard GOV-3144 — PYQ: 2026 Prelims Q82 (Grand Slam governance, eligibility and wild cards)
**Front:** Grand Slam tennis governance, eligibility and wild-card participation rules (2026 Prelims GS-I Q82).
**Definition (learn):** The item combines three propositions: shared governance, age and ranking eligibility, and wild-card caps.
**Breakdown:**
- Institutional proposition: whether the four tournaments share a governance partnership (✅).
- Eligibility proposition: whether age and international ranking alone make all players eligible for entry.
- Wild-card proposition: whether rules cap how many wild cards a player may receive.
- Evaluate each against the rule book independently.
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** 2026 Set-A key provisional and held locally; no option or answer inferred (✅ owner 16 §8).
**Links:** GOV-3140, GOV-3141, GOV-3142, GOV-3143.
**Trap:** A broadly true description of elite tennis is not evidence of a specific entry rule.
**Mains hook:** Statement-by-statement discipline is the exam skill.
**Back:** *"Three claims, three tests: governance, eligibility, wild-card cap."*

### Flashcard GOV-3145 — Six-test checklist for any sports body
**Front:** What six tests should you apply to any sports body or governance failure?
**Definition (learn):** A diagnostic checklist.
**Breakdown:**
- 1 Basis of authority: statute, recognition, contract or private rule book?
- 2 Recognition and conditions: published criteria; withdrawable with reasons?
- 3 Internal governance: elections, tenure limits, athlete and gender representation, conflict rules, audit.
- 4 Selection and eligibility: criteria published before the cycle; reasons for exclusion.
- 5 Dispute resolution: independent forum with notice, hearing, reasons, appeal; waiver of courts?
- 6 Athlete protection: safeguarding, independent grievance channels, health support, anti-retaliation.
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** ✅ owner 16 §7.4 (analytical checklist).
**Links:** GOV-3123, GOV-3146, GOV-3147.
**Trap:** A body that cannot state selection criteria in advance has already failed the governance test.
**Mains hook:** Use the six tests as the structure for any novel sports-governance case.
**Back:** *"Authority, recognition, governance, selection, disputes, protection."*

### Flashcard GOV-3146 — Autonomy as a functional shield, not a jurisdictional immunity
**Front:** Why is sporting autonomy a functional protection rather than a jurisdictional immunity?
**Definition (learn):** Autonomy protects technical and selection decisions. A body with monopoly power over an athlete's career exercises public power in substance.
**Breakdown:**
- Monopoly: one federation per sport, no athlete exit.
- Ordinary market and contract disciplines do not apply, which justifies statutory oversight.
- Oversight of governance, finance and rights is different from interference in team selection or technical rules.
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** ✅ owner 16 §7.2 (T1 and T2 theses).
**Links:** GOV-3123, GOV-3148, GOV-3145.
**Trap:** Autonomy is not a shield against accountability for money, selection and safeguarding.
**Mains hook:** The autonomy argument is strongest for technical decisions, weakest for finance and rights.
**Back:** *"Autonomy shields the game, not the abuse."*

### Flashcard GOV-3147 — Athlete rights: due process in a private body
**Front:** What due-process elements must an athlete's selection or eligibility dispute receive?
**Definition (learn):** Notice, criteria published in advance, reasons, appeal and an independent forum.
**Breakdown:**
- Notice of the decision.
- Criteria published before the selection cycle, applied consistently.
- Reasons given for exclusion.
- Independent appeal; avoid compulsory waiver of court access.
- Protection from retaliation.
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** ✅ owner 16 §7.1 and §7.4.
**Links:** GOV-3145, GOV-3146, GOV-3131.
**Trap:** An athlete required to waive court access loses a basic remedy (owner 16 §7.4 test 5).
**Mains hook:** Athlete rights are the reason sports needs governing, because athletes have the least bargaining power.
**Back:** *"Notice, criteria, reasons, appeal, independent forum."*

### Flashcard GOV-3148 — Autonomy versus state oversight: the counter-argument and reply
**Front:** What is the counter-argument to statutory sports regulation, and what is the reply?
**Definition (learn):** Charters protect sports bodies from government interference, and excessive interference can risk international suspension of a national body.
**Breakdown:**
- Reply: oversight directed at governance standards, finance and rights differs from interference in team selection or technical rules.
- This distinction makes statutory regulation defensible.
- Specialist tribunal: speed and expertise, but appointment-independence questions (⚠️ owner 16 §7.6).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** ✅ owner 16 §7.6.
**Links:** GOV-3146, GOV-3131, GOV-3129.
**Trap:** Treating every state oversight as interference misses the reply.
**Mains hook:** Draw the line at selection and technical rules; accept oversight of money and rights.
**Back:** *"Oversee the money and rights, not the team sheet."*

### Flashcard GOV-3149 — Federal dimension of sport
**Front:** Why does a national statute not by itself reform State-level sports bodies?
**Definition (learn):** Sport involves Union and State bodies and State associations, so a national Act does not automatically change State federations.
**Breakdown:**
- Union level: national federations and the Act.
- State level: State associations and their governance.
- Reform needs federal coordination.
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** ⚠️ owner 16 §7.6 (analytical).
**Links:** GOV-3129, GOV-3131.
**Trap:** Do not assume the Act reformed all State bodies.
**Mains hook:** Name the federal gap in any reform assessment.
**Back:** *"A national Act, state associations: two rooms to reform."*

### Flashcard GOV-3150 — Governance issue, risk and reform principle matrix
**Front:** What risk and reform principle pair applies to each sports-governance issue?
**Definition (learn):** A matrix from the advanced owner.
**Breakdown:**
- Federation elections: entrenchment and conflict; transparent constitution, terms and independent scrutiny.
- Athlete selection: unreasoned exclusion; published criteria, recorded decisions and appeal.
- Event entry or wild cards: discretion or favouritism; rule-bound discretion and conflict disclosure.
- Anti-doping: rights error or uneven enforcement; due process, accredited testing, reasoned results management.
- Safeguarding: power imbalance and abuse; independent reporting and athlete-centred protection.
- Commercial rights: revenue concentration; transparent contracting and reinvestment.
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** ✅ owner 16 advanced §2 (matrix).
**Links:** GOV-3145, GOV-3147, GOV-3142.
**Trap:** Revenue concentration is a commercial issue that athlete representation addresses (owner 16 §7.6).
**Mains hook:** Pair each risk with a reform principle rather than a generic call for reform.
**Back:** *"Elections, selection, wild cards, doping, safeguarding, money: each risk, its principle."*
